## Page 1


CAN WE TEACH GENERATIVE ARTIFICIAL INTELLIGENCE THE DESIGN 
LANGUAGE OF ENGINEERED LIVING MATERIALS? 
 
Andrés Díaz Lantada 1,2*, José A. Yáñez 1, William Solórzano-Requejo 1, Monsur Islam 1 
 
1 Mechanical Engineering Department, Universidad Politécnica de Madrid, c/ José Gutiérrez 
Abascal 2, 28006 Madrid, Spain.  
2 IMDEA Materials Institute, c/ Eric Kandel 2, 28906 Getafe, Spain. 
*Contact: andres.diaz@upm.es / andres.diaz@imdeamaterials.org  
 
Abstract 
This study presents a versatile ontology and a useful codification scheme for describing all 
kinds of engineered living materials (ELMs). The different components of the ontology, 
namely: families according to the taxonomy for ELMs, industrial applications and 
synthesis or processing methods, are systematically organized, enumerated, classified, 
codified and explained. The methodic application of the ontology to a set of 100 relevant 
examples of ELMs helps to demonstrate its utility and adaptability to many different types 
of ELMs with a wide range of industrial applications and obtained through numerous 
synthesis and processing methods. This proves that the developed ontology and 
codification schemes, with the glossary provided to support its implementation and 
application, can serve as a comprehensive classification tool for the emergent field of 
ELMs. Furthermore, the usability of the ELMs ontology and codification by a generative 
artificial intelligence (AI) is explored and validated by different means, checking that both 
natural language and the codification are understandable for describing ELMs, verifying 
that the generative AI adequately codifies examples of ELMs according to the ontology, 
and validating the synergic applicability of the ontology and codification with generative 
AI tools for illustrating novel ELMs and supporting their conceptual design. This study is 
expected to provide a universal language to facilitate communication in the ELMs field and 
to foster the discovery of new ELMs and related innovations, hoping it may accelerate 
scientific and technological discoveries.  
Keywords: Engineered living materials; living matter; generative artificial intelligence; 
design languages.


## Page 2


1. Introduction 
Engineered living materials have emerged as a new family of materials characterized by their 
biohybrid nature. Foundational references describe them as materials, in which living cells, 
including eukaryotic, prokaryotic and synthetic cell types, and biomaterials as scaffolding 
structures coexist. Usually ELMs are divided into biological, made of cells and their self-
generated extracellular matrices (ECMs), and biohybrid, based on cells colonizing artificial 
EMCs.1–6 ELMs have emerged thanks to decades of research in converging fields including: tissue 
engineering and biofabrication 7,8, micro-physiological systems and organs-on-chips 9,10, multi-
cellular engineered living systems (MCELS),11,12 biohybrid robotics,13,14 and even “Baubotanik” 
or living plant construction,15,16 to cite a few. These ELMs constitute a contemporary revolution 
in materials science and engineering, and pioneer studies have pointed out that we are currently 
living their defining moment.17 Indeed, relevant contributions have focused on organizing the 
research field of ELMs,18 providing a taxonomy for ELMs,19 and reviewing the more recent 
advances in an organized manner oriented to their engineering design and promote their societal 
impacts.20,21 
Being a nascent field, specific engineering design approaches are required for overcoming 
challenges linked to their reliability, strength, autonomy, and sustainable development. Relevant 
advances have been carried out recently in connection with achieving higher performances thanks 
to ceramic and carbon chassis,22,23 expanding their autonomy and lifespan by combining cell 
types,24,25 or mapping their ethical and societal challenges for enabling ethics-guided ELMs 
research.26 Still, engineering design methodologies purposefully developed for ELMs are 
required, as well as tools for systematizing ELMs research and empowering innovators to create 
new ELMs and derived technologies navigating the turbulent waters of such a multidisciplinary 
and rapidly evolving field.  
In parallel, advances in generative artificial intelligence (AI), especially since the advent of large 
language models and supporting algorithms for generative knowledge extraction, graph-based 
representation, and multimodal intelligent graph reasoning, are importantly accelerating scientific 
discovery with intriguing implications for designing materials and structures, as an inspiring 
reference has pointed out.27 Indeed, generative artificial intelligence is redefining the innovation 
cycle and reformulating traditional approaches to materials engineering in connection with 
product design. To mention some examples, the application of innovative biomaterials for 
designing new structures, buildings, vehicles, medical devices and everyday products has been 
extensively explored and systematized employing “text-to-image” resources.28–30 In the current 
state, the interactions with generative AIs lead to conceptual designs and promote the screening 
of ideas, in connection with complex-shaped geometries usually requiring additive manufacturing 
technologies for their materialization, from the beginning of the innovation cycle. In addition, the 
interaction with these AIs may prove useful along the whole design cycle, helping to refine the 
concepts,31 to approach manufacturing, for instance by means of 3D and 4D printing,32,33 to plan 
their functional integration, and even to validate nature-inspired designs in silico.34 In this context, 
the combination of ontologies, codification schemes and generative AIs has been also explored 
by our team for designing stimuli-responsive or smart (but not living) materials and structures 
and derived actuators.35 
In our hypothesis, generative AI is bound to have a highly transformative role for ELMs research, 
paving the way towards straightforward, effective and safe solutions in an enormous variety of 
industrial fields, once specific supporting resources and design methods are developed. For 
developing these resources, a new design language for ELMs may be required, which may 
facilitate interactions with generative AIs in connection with ELMs. With this objective in mind, 
our study presents the development of a codified ontology for ELMs, to the authors’ best 
knowledge the first available, and demonstrates its synergic employment with generative AI tools.


## Page 3


To this end, expanding the taxonomy for ELMs,19 a complete ontology with an accompanying 
codification scheme is developed in this study for describing all kinds of ELMs. The different 
components of the ontology: families according to the mentioned taxonomy, industrial 
applications and synthesis or processing methods, are systematically organized, classified, coded 
and explained. Then, the ontology and codification schemes are methodically applied to a set of 
100 relevant examples of ELMs for demonstrating their utility and adaptability. This proves that 
the developed ontology and codification schemes, with the glossary provided to support its 
implementation and application, serve as a reliable classification tool for the emergent field of 
ELMs. Finally, the usability of the ELMs ontology and codification by a generative artificial 
intelligence (AI) is explored and validated by different means: checking that both natural language 
and the codification are understandable for describing ELMs, verifying that the generative AI 
adequately codifies examples of ELMs according to the ontology, and validating the synergic 
applicability of the ontology and codification with generative AI tools for illustrating novel ELMs 
and supporting their conceptual design.  
The materials and methods employed are presented in the following section, before describing 
and discussing the main results obtained and analyzing the detected current limitations and 
proposed research directions, hoping to contribute to this nascent field. 
2. Ontology and knowledge graph representation of engineered living materials 
2.1. Search criteria for the references employed for evaluating the ELMs ontology 
Employing Google Scholar, searching for “engineered living materials” within the temporal 
framework of years 2018 and 2025, a total of 150 relevant references on ELMs are found and 
chronologically ordered. For different reasons (redundance, lack of detail, non-living entity) 30 
references are discarded. From the remaining 120, the first 100 are employed for evaluating the 
developed ELMs ontology and the applicability of the proposed codification scheme, and for 
training the generative AIs employed (see Sections 2.3 and 3). The 20 more recent ones are used 
for validation purposes, so that the trained generative AIs are presented with examples never seen 
before during the training, following the procedure explained in subsection 2.3 and discussed in 
more detail in section 3. 
2.2. Ontology and codification scheme for ELMs 
Starting from the Linnean-style taxonomy for ELMs developed by our team,19 in order to reach a 
truly applicable ontology for supporting the engineering design of new living materials, additional 
fundamental components should be considered, including industrial application fields and 
synthesis processes or production methods. Furthermore, facilitating the interaction with 
generative AIs and reaching a higher level of abstraction for conceptual design requires the 
development of a coding scheme and codification, which can be considered a new language for 
engineering living materials through synergic interactions with generative AIs. In agreement with 
the basic taxonomy for ELMs, families are organized and hierarchically classified employing 
higher rank taxa (domains and kingdoms) that take inspiration from life’s taxonomy, considering 
the cell types within ELMs as the most fundamental features of living materials, which determine 
their essence and key functions. The lower rank taxa (classes and below) depend on the material 
types used for the living materials’ “skeletons” or scaffolds, providing structural support to the 
living cell colonies. In this way, the classical life’s taxonomy is merged with common materials 
classifications. 
Importantly, expanding from the available taxonomy for ELMs, Table 1 presents the hierarchical 
organization of ELMs families, including taxonomic ranks or taxa, categories, descriptions, and 
codifications for engineered living materials (ELMs).


## Page 4


In addition, for the first time the taxonomy is revised, expanded with detailed descriptions for the 
different categories and provided with a biunivocal coding (three-letter codes) for all the ranked 
categories. It can be advanced that the descriptions prove fundamental in the process of training 
the generative AI, towards an understanding of taxa and categories, and their essence and 
boundaries, as analyzed in section 3. At the same time, the codes contribute to achieving a sort of 
genomic representation for ELMs, which is expected to leverage the capabilities of AIs as 
creativity promotion tools in this field (for instance, employing already classical genetic 
algorithms and more recent large language models). Besides, Table 2 lists the more common 
production methods and industrial applications for ELMs, as well as their descriptions and 
codifications, as a complement to Table 1, towards a more comprehensive codified ontology for 
ELMs. 
Once developed, the ontology and codification scheme are applied to describing, classifying and 
coding the gathered (see subsection 2.1) set of 100 pioneering examples of ELMs, achieving one 
of the most comprehensive overviews of the current panorama in the field of ELMs. Table 3 
presents a subset of 10 examples following the rules of the ontology and codification and 
providing detailed textual descriptions for the different examples, which is necessary for the 
subsequent interactions with the AI. For a full overview of the 100 examples, please consult Table 
S1 in the Supplementary Material S1. References [See the references in supplementary 
information] detailing the engineering of the different examples of living materials are also 
provided as part of Supplementary Material S1, while the subset of 10 examples from Table 3 
also includes the relevant references.36–45 
For visual exploration and communication of the dataset structure, RAWGraphs 2.046 is employed 
to represent the 100 codified examples. Based on the comprehensive review, Figure 2A¡Error! 
No se encuentra el origen de la referencia. shows a dendritic dendrogram organizing the dataset 
of 100 examples in families and subfamilies according to the proposed ontology and codification 
scheme. Colors correspond to the five main domains of ELMs: archaeal (purple), bacterial 
(orange), eukaryotic (green), cross-domain (yellow) and synthetic (blue). To complement this, a 
linear dendrogram as well as graphic representations of relationships between species, application 
areas and production methods, are also provided in Figure S1.1 and Figure S1.2 in Supplementary 
Material S1. 
To introduce the potential of generative AI for ELMs innovation, Figure 1 presents AI-generated 
illustrations for a variety ELMs, obtained by employing the textual descriptions of different 
examples from Table 3 as “prompts”. Microsoft Copilot was employed as accessible and easy to 
use generative artificial intelligence capable of generating images from textual instructions.

PLN

cells ELMs

Phylum 
0D ELMs
ELMs with a quasi-punctual structure, usually cells coupled to micro- / nano-particles.
0D
1D ELMs 
ELMs with a linear or one-dimensional structure, usually based on cells populating or generating fibers as 
extracellular matrices.
1D 
2D ELMs 
ELMs with a planar or bi-dimensional structure, usually biofilms or based on cells populating a planar 
biomaterial or generating a planar extracellular matrix.
2D 
3D ELMs 
ELMs with a three-dimensional structure, usually cells within a three-dimensional biomaterial or three-
dimensional scaffold.
3D


## Page 6


4D ELMs 
ELMs with a four-dimensional structure, mainly for cases in which the temporal dimension, involving 
metamorphoses, plays a relevant functional role.
4D 
Fractal ELMs
ELMs with a irregular, fractured or self-similar structure that cannot be defined by integer dimensional values.
FR
Cross-phylum ELMs 
ELMs with structures combining different of the 0D, 1D, 2D, 3D, 4D and fractal phyla, besides, multi-scale 
ELMs whose structures are not fractal.
CP 
Class 
Living alloys 
ELMs with extracellular matrices made predominantly of alloys. Examples of families within the class of living 
alloys include: Fe-based, Ti-based, Au-based, Cu-based, Ag-based, to cite a few.
LAL 
Living carbons 
ELMs with extracellular matrices made predominantly of carbons. Examples of families within the class of 
living carbons include: allotropic forms of C, such as diamond, graphite, graphene, carbon nanotubes, glassy 
carbon, carbon dots and fullerenes to cite a few.
LCA 
Living ceramics 
ELMs with extracellular matrices made predominantly of ceramics. Examples of families within the class of 
living ceramics include: oxides, silicates, carbides, nitrides, to cite a few.
LCE 
Living composites 
ELMs with extracellular matrices made predominantly of composites. Examples of families within the class of 
living composites include metal-matrix composites, polymer-matrix composites, ceramic-matrix composites, to 
cite a few.
LCO 
Living polymers 
ELMs with extracellular matrices made predominantly of polymers. Examples of families within the class of 
living polymers include: thermosets, thermoplastics, elastomers, hydrogels, liquid crystals, to cite a few.
LPO 
Cross-class ELMs 
ELMs with extracellular matrices made of various materials classes, which cannot be described by the traditional 
term composite.
CRF

Transport and related infrastructures 
ELMs employed as components or subsystems in transport applications (vehicles, ships, drones, 
airplanes) and related infrastructures (f.e. biocement for self-healing roads).
TRI 
Manufacturing 
and 
production 
methods for 
engineered 
living 
materials 
(ELMs) 
Engineering cells to produce ELMs 
Artificially manipulated cells lead to special extracellular matrix production and to materials 
synthesizing materials.
ECP 
Extracellular matrix (ECM) grown in 
culture 
Cultured cells grow an extracellular matrix in some cases aided by a biomaterial acting as substrate or 
support, the process applies both to hybrid living materials (HLMs) and to biological engineered living 
materials (bio-ELMs).
EMG 
Extracellular matrix (ECM) 
bioaccumulation  
Cultured cells foster chemical reactions, typically redox, capable of reducing ions leading usually to 
metallic deposits or aggregates as part of their extracellular matrices, related to processes like 
biomineralization but with metals.
EMB 
Intracellular synthesis, intracellular 
transport or invagination 
Materials production inside cells in culture, normally involving the intracellular transport or the 
invagination of a material to modify cellular states and functionalities to trigger the synthesis, trapping 
or deposit, usually aimed at polymeric production or the generation of metallic nanoparticles within the 
cells (bio-ELMs).
ICS 
Biofilm formation and related 
extracellular matrix (ECM) growth
Cultured cells form a biofilm, and an extracellular matrix is grown, usually for biological engineered 
living materials (bio-ELMs) grown in Petri dishes or bioreactors.
BFF 
Biofilm based trapping or anchoring 
of inorganic particles or materials and 
biofilms interfacing with inorganic 
materials
Cultured cells form a biofilm, and an extracellular matrix is grown, trapping or anchoring inorganic 
micro / nano-particles or a biofilm interfaces with inorganic materials, usually leading to living 
composites, mainly HLMs. 
BTI 
Surface bio-patterning for selective 
cell culture / extracellular matrix 
(ECM) growth
Selective surface patterning creates special anchoring regions for cells capable of growing an 
extracellular matrix that generates a pattern of ELM upon the original substrate. 
SBP 
Biomineralization 
Process by which living cells generate minerals for creating stiff mineralized tissues, for magnetic 
sensing, for storing elements and for protection, a process that is present in all biological kingdoms of 
life and proves interesting both for HLMs and bio-ELMs.
BMN 
Molecular self-assembly 
Process by which molecules spontaneously aggregate and form ordered structures without human 
intervention. 
MSA 
3D bioprinting of cells and 
biomaterials
Additive manufacturing processes involving the processing of biomaterials and living cells for creating 
living three-dimensional constructs, usually applied to HLMs.
3DB 
4D bioprinting of cells and 
biomaterials
Similar to 3D bioprinting but with the incorporation of a relevant temporal dimension linked to the fact 
that 4D bioprinted ELMs (usually HLMs) exhibit one or various metamorphoses during their life.
4DB 
Scaffold (synthetic) structure 3D 
printing and cell colonization 
Additive manufacturing processes aimed at the creation of three-dimensional structures or scaffolds (as 
in tissue engineering) adequately mimicking extracellular matrices for subsequent cell colonization 
leading to HLMs. 
3DP 
Scaffold (synthetic) structure 4D 
printing and cell colonization 
Similar to 3D printed scaffolds for cell colonization but with the incorporation of a relevant temporal 
dimension linked to the fact that 4D printed scaffolds exhibit one or various metamorphoses during their 
life, hence leading to shape-morphing HLMs.
4DP

Solvent casting or melt molding, 
eventual particulate leaching and cell 
colonization or solvent casting with 
cells
Process involving the casting of a solvent with soluble particles within a mold, the subsequent leaching 
of the soluble particles to reach a porous biomaterial or synthetic scaffold and the final cell colonization 
to reach HLMs. 
SCP 
Gas foaming of porous structures and 
cell colonization
As in the previous process but using gas for foaming and thus generating the porous biomaterial or 
synthetic scaffold for HLMs. 
GFP 
Fiber based technologies and cell 
colonization or processing of fiber-
encapsulated microorganisms
Different spinning processes for generating fibers, for weaving textiles and for obtaining non-wovens, 
as biomaterials or synthetic scaffolds for cell colonization leading to HLMs. 
FBT 
Phase separation processes and cell 
colonization
Processes aimed at the creation of two or more phases from an originally single homogeneous mixture, 
usually leading to a porous biomaterials or structure for HLMs.
PSP 
Pyrolysis of polymeric precursors and 
cell colonization 
Process focused on the generation of glassy carbon or pyrolytic carbon structures as scaffolds for cell 
colonization achieved by pyrolysis of polymeric precursors, usually oriented to the generation of living 
carbons (HLMs with synthetic carbon matrix).
PPP 
Composite manufacturing methods 
and cell colonization
Processes from the composites industry leading to woven, porous or functionally graded structures as 
synthetic scaffolds for cells, again for HLMs. 
CMM 
Other production methods or 
combinations
Other processes or combinations of the above described and encapsulation of cells within engineered 
constructs for the generation of bio-ELMs and HLMs.
OPM

## Page 13


2.3. Knowledge graph representation of ELM ontology database 
While the proposed ontology establishes a novel formal semantics for ELMs, ontology-based 
classification alone is insufficient to capture how design choices co-occur, dominate, or remain 
underexplored across available knowledge. The proposed ontology defines the admissible 
semantic space of ELM design; however, it does not represent the empirical structure of the design 
space as observed in the literature. To support evidence-grounded design and innovation 
workflows, the ontology-coded ELM dataset (subset in Table 3, complete version as 
Supplementary Material S1) was therefore instantiated as a knowledge graph (KG) derived 
entirely from data extracted from peer-reviewed journal articles. Graph-based reasoning methods, 
especially the pioneering works by Prof. Dr. Markus J. Buehler,27 inspired us in this direction, in 
our case focusing on engineered living matter. 
Each ELM case, encoded through a seven-slot code aligned with the seven ontology ranks or 
taxons, is represented as a case node in the graph. The ontology codes used to annotate the ELM 
cases are also represented as nodes, yielding a bipartite graph generated in Python 3.13.7 
employing the NetworkX library.47 The resulting graph therefore consists of two partitions: (i) 
ELM cases reported in the literature, and (ii) ontology-derived category nodes spanning Domain, 
Super kingdom, Kingdom, Phylum, Class, Applications and Production Methods. An edge is 
created whenever an ontology code is assigned to a given ELM case. 
This modeling choice preserves the semantic structure of the ontology while making the ELM 
case base computationally queryable. Importantly, edges are not defined between ontology 
category nodes in this representation. As a result, the graph codes only the explicit use of ontology 
categories in published ELM designs and use cases. It should therefore be interpreted as a partial 
observational projection of the ontological design space, reflecting reported evidence rather than 
the full set of ontologically admissible ELM configurations. Consequently, the absence of a 
configuration in the graph indicates a lack of reported evidence, not ontological or biological 
impossibility. This distinction is critical for the subsequent design and innovation analyses. 
The KG provides a structured, computable representation of bibliographic evidence that 
complements the provided ontology tables and code lists (Tables 1-3 and S1). It enables the 
quantitative characterization of empirical design trends, sparsity patterns, and potential biases in 
the reported ELM literature, while preserving ontological semantics. In this study, the KG serves 
as an operational semantic base for: (i) retrieving relevant ELM cases for design tasks, (ii) 
ontology-constrained data augmentation, and (iii) application-conditioned gap detection to 
support innovative design workflows. In this way, the ontology, codification scheme, and KG 
jointly define a reusable design language for advancing ELM research and enabling its 
downstream integration with generative AI approaches. 
Complementary graph-level exploration is performed using Gephi 0.10.1,48 which is used for KG 
visualization and exploratory inspection of the dataset. This environment enables interactive 
analysis of network structure, facilitating the manual identification of recurrent node usage, 
emerging literature trends and potential biases. Figure 2a presents the global graph (dendritic 
dendrogram or “tree of life of ELMs”), with node color distinguishing partitions and ontology 
slots (ELM case nodes are shown in light purple), and node size proportional to degree (number 
of edges connected to a node). The visualization highlights a non-uniform occupation of the 
ontology design space: a small set of recurrent ontology categories form high-connectivity hubs 
(e.g., HYB, LPO and 3D), whereas many categories remain weakly connected. In addition, Figure 
2b ranks ontology category nodes by degree, making the uneven distribution of bibliographic 
evidence across ontology-coded categories more explicit.


## Page 14


Quantitative network statistics further characterize the structural properties of the KG. The graph 
contains 160 nodes, including 100 ELM case nodes and 60 ontology categories nodes, connected 
by 738 edges. The graph consists of a single connected component, indicating that all ELM cases 
are indirectly linked through shared ontology categories. In the undirected network 
representation, density is 0.058 and the average degree is 9.225.  
ELM case nodes exhibit a mean degree of 7.38 (range: 7-9), reflecting their assignment across 
the seven ontology ranks, with a limited number of cases showing multiple assignments within 
individual slots. Ontology category nodes instead show a long-tailed degree distribution (mean 
12.3, median 5.5, maximum 82), indicating that bibliographic evidence is strongly concentrated 
around a limited number of recurrent categories. The most connected category nodes include 
Superkingdom HYB (82), Class LPO (70), Phylum 3D (62), and Domain BAC / Kingdom EUB 
(49 each). In contrast, several ontology category nodes appear only once or a few times, indicating 
sparsely evidenced regions of the empirically observed ELM design space; in particular, 4DB, 
4DP, CP, PPP, and PSP are among the least represented categories. 
To translate annotation co-occurrence evidence into actionable “design-by-evidence” guidance, 
association rules are additionally mined. Rules are reported using support (the proportion of cases 
in which the pattern appears), confidence (the probability that the consequent appears when the 
antecedent is present), and lift (the strength of the association relative to what would be expected 
by chance), and should be interpreted as co-occurrence patterns rather than causal relations, hence 
providing additional insights about the current state-of-the-art in ELMs.  
Representative rules with high confidence restricted to single-item and cross-slot associations, 
that can be used as quick empirical priors when selecting structural classes, are further detailed in 
Supplementary Material S1. Rule subsets with production method as consequents and application 
field as antecedents are also reported providing direct evidence-based guidance for: (i) 
manufacturing selection based on defined ontological code and (ii) ontological code selection 
based on target application. In this sense, association rules complement graph-level analysis by 
converting recurrent patterns in literature into interpretable design heuristics. 
Considering the current panorama of ELMs, the quantitative analysis of the graph highlight the 
actual relevance of hybrid living materials, a superkingdom in which synthetic materials are 
colonized by the living entities. In hybrid living materials, the possibility of designing the 
extracellular matrix considering the morphological requirements of the final application brings in 
some advantages, as compared to biological living materials, in which the cells in culture generate 
their own extracellular matrix forming more amorphous and difficult to tune geometries. Hybrid 
living materials in a way evolve from the concept of scaffold-based solutions for tissue 
engineering and are already supported by a plethora of advanced manufacturing technologies 
enabling multi-scale and multi-material approaches. Furthermore, the living polymers class stands 
out because most biological living materials generate polymeric extracellular matrices and as a 
consequence of the versatile employment of polymers for biohybrid systems in general.

3. Ontology- and knowledge graph-grounded generative AI framework for designing and 
innovating engineered living materials 
The framework is built on the developed coding scheme and its bipartite knowledge graph, which 
contains literature-derived ELMs and incorporates ChatGPT 5.2 Thinking as a reasoning engine 
for this challenging task. This framework is created to aid and foster creativity in the selection, 
generation, and design of ELMs, either directly from ontological codes or in response to specific 
application requirements. Consisting of five pipelines and six prompts, the framework may be 
used as either a set of independent modules or as part of multi-stage processes for: (i) database 
augmentation, (ii) application-conditioned candidate selection, (iii) gap-driven innovation, (iv) 
downstream design generation, and (v) visual representation of ELM candidates. Each pipeline 
operates through a prompt-response-analysis loop to ensure that generative outputs are critically 
assessed by the expert interacting with the AI in terms of feasibility, novelty and translational 
relevance, rather than being accepted unquestioningly. 
In our approach, derived from previous studies by our team,19,28,35 the generative AI is considered 
a supporting colleague along the creativity promotion steps, and quasi-human dialogues 
employing natural language are lived through during the grounding, evaluation and pipeline-
based application of the model. 
Figure 3 illustrates the framework and its pipeline instances in schematic form. Meanwhile, Table 
4 summarizes the proposed prompts, indicating their function, as well as their expected inputs 
and outputs. Full prompt texts are provided in a directly reusable form for interaction with 
ChatGPT in the Supplementary Material S2, supporting reproducibility. Among these, Prompt F 
is shared across most pipelines and is aimed at generating a visual representation of the designed 
ELM. It takes either a 7-slot ontology code or a complete ontology-derived instantiation as input, 
optionally supplemented with user-defined visualization preferences including style (e.g., 
schematic, multiscale, cutaway, mechanism, workflow, ontology iconography) and formatting 
controls (e.g. aspect ratio, view, abstraction, annotations). The output is a single, living-matter-
centered image that explicitly depicts the ELM and, where relevant, its application context. The 
individual pipelines are described in detail in the following sections.

## Page 18


3.1. Pipeline A: AI ontology semantic grounding validation 
Pipeline A is a prerequisite for all subsequent generative and inferential tasks. At this stage, the 
generative AI employed, ChatGPT in our case, is constrained by the ELM ontology, whose coding 
scheme defines valid classes, codes and combinations within the design space, explicitly 
forbidding the extension, reinterpretation or invention of codes. This shared semantic basis is 
established to ensure that downstream outputs remain interpretable, reproducible, and 
ontologically consistent. As shown in Figure 3, if the model does not demonstrate an adequate 
understanding of the ontology, this step may be iterative until satisfactory grounding is achieved 
by refining and reapplying Prompt A. In this sense, ontology functions as both a semantic 
constraint and a communication interface between the user and the generative AI. Only after 
ontological grounding has been achieved can the remaining pipelines be implemented. 
The knowledge graph is treated as bibliographic evidence rather than as a constraint on admissible 
ELM ontological configurations. The model is then required to demonstrate its ontology 
comprehension by extracting ontology codes and definitions; reconstructing the codification 
syntax; providing one or two ELM examples using only ontology definitions; and identifying 
inconsistencies, ambiguities, or deviations in code usage and codification. 
3.2. Pipeline B: Bibliography-based data augmentation 
As is widely recognized, ELMs as field of knowledge is continuously and very rapidly expanding 
and evolving. Therefore, the knowledge graph containing examples of existing ELMs should 
grow accordingly. ChatGPT (or other generative AIs) can be employed for bibliography-based 
data augmentation through Pipeline B using Prompt B, under two possible input strategies: (i) the 
scientific paper abstract or (ii) the full text. In both cases, the source data is processed through the 
same ontology-constrained decoding workflow. First, the relevance of the evidence to ELMs is 
confirmed, and then the evidence is translated into seven-slot ELM codes. This enables the graph 
to be expanded while preserving the integrity of the coding scheme. 
Beyond its role in data augmentation, Pipeline B also functions as a secondary validation 
procedure for determining the ontology grounding and coding performance of the AI model on 
unseen bibliographic evidence through comparison with human codification. The details of this 
evaluation and its results are presented in Use case 1. 
3.3. Pipeline C: Graph-based gap detection for ELM innovation 
Pipeline C performs graph-based gap detection to enable unbiased hypothesis generation for 
ELMs. The pipeline operates in two modes: (i) generic, in which the application domain is not 
used as a constraint, and (ii) application-conditioned, in which gap prioritization is guided by a 
specified target application domain while preserving application-independent novelty criteria. 
Based on the identified gaps, ChatGPT -or the generative AI of choice- proposes ELM candidates 
guided by biological, structural, and technological plausibility criteria. 
This pipeline consists of two main steps. First, a structural analysis of the knowledge graph is 
performed to characterize sparsity and bias in the observed literature design space. This analysis 
serves exclusively to inform the interpretation and prioritization of candidate gaps and is not 
treated as a constraint on biological admissibility. Second, structure-informed gap detection is 
carried out within the ontology-defined space by enumerating biologically admissible 
combinations across the structural part of the code (comprising: Domain, Superkingdom, 
Kingdom, Phylum, and Class), and identifying combinations that are absent or strongly 
underrepresented in the knowledge graph.  
Importantly, innovation is defined strictly at this structural level, such that gaps are detected 
exclusively within these five slots. For each selected gap, the model generates a complete 7-slot


## Page 19


ELM hypothesis, in which Application and Production Method, as secondary taxons, are 
introduced solely to complete a coherent and testable hypothesis. In the application-conditioned 
mode, the target application domain is used only as a downstream prioritization factor, whereas 
gap definition and novelty remain strictly determined by these five structural slots. 
All generated candidates must remain living-matter-centric and satisfy explicit plausibility 
constraints along three complementary axes: (i) biological plausibility, referring to the 
compatibility between the living system and the organizational scale or matrix environment; (ii) 
structural/material plausibility, referring to the coherence between Phylum/Class and the expected 
physical or material organization; and (iii) technological feasibility, referring to whether the 
concept is realizable in principle using known or foreseeable fabrication approaches. 
Furthermore, the reasoning process must explicitly consider practical implications, such as 
manufacturability, scalability, experimental feasibility and integration potential. 
Pipeline C produces a structured list of 7-slot ELM candidates. Each one is accompanied by a 
concise concept description and bullet-point justifications. These candidate designs are then 
subjected to expert validation. If an output is not approved by the expert, the prompt is refined 
iteratively, and a new output is generated until a coherent response is obtained. Moreover, this 
pipeline could support dataset augmentation by incorporating ELMs derived from gaps and 
validated by experts into the current database. 
3.4. Pipeline D: Application-conditioned ELM retrieval 
Pipeline D enables the selection of ELMs for a specific application by using ChatGPT to filter, 
categorize, and contextualize up to five concepts from the KG. In this strictly goal-directed, non-
generative procedure, prompt D identifies the most suitable candidate from ELMs derived from: 
(i) the initial bibliographic references, (ii) the data augmentation procedure in Pipeline B, and (iii) 
the gap-derived generation process in Pipeline C. 
Selection is carried out under user-defined application boundary conditions that serve as 
acceptance and rejection criteria. The five-slot structural core is preserved while the Application 
and Production Method slots are completed during the reasoning process. The final output is the 
AI model response, which includes the full seven-slot code, a description of the selected ELM 
concept, and justification of its biological, structural, and technological plausibility. 
3.5. Pipeline E: Ontology-based AI-driven ELM full instantiation 
Pipeline E aids the creation of ELMs from a 7-slot ontological code that has been previously 
defined, by translating it into an output that is both scientifically coherent and technically 
plausible. Regardless of its origin, the input incorporates always a complete seven-slot code. This 
code might come from: (i) hypotheses generated in Pipeline C, (ii) candidates selected in Pipeline 
D based on the application, or (iii) raw ontological codes provided without additional context. 
Prompt E first verifies the integrity of each input code and then converts the ontological 
specification into a design proposal by translating each slot into constraints relating to biology, 
materials, geometry, applications and processes. Based on this, it provides concrete information 
on the biological entities involved while preserving consistency with the predefined code: 
compatible matrix materials, geometries consistent with the structural slot, deployment scenarios 
associated with the application slot and feasible workflows aligned with the production method 
slot. Reasoning is restricted to approaches centered on (engineered) living matter and considers 
manufacturability, scalability, experimental feasibility and system integration. Each major design 
decision must be justified and at least two candidate options must be proposed for the biological 
and material components. This prepares the concept for experimental planning, simulation or 
optimization.


## Page 20


4. Ontology-grounded AI-driven framework assessment via use cases for innovating new 
ELMs: on the understanding of the design language by a trained generative AI 
This section presents the performance of the proposed ELM design framework. Rather than being 
organized as a single linear workflow, the framework is evaluated through a set of selected 
workflows representative of its modular capabilities. This choice reflects the modular nature of 
the proposed methodology: each pipeline constitutes a self-contained, reproducible component 
with well-defined inputs, outputs, and constraints, thereby enabling multiple workflow 
configurations to address distinct research objectives. Thus, the framework does not prescribe a 
unique execution path; instead, different combinations of pipelines can be assembled depending 
on the target application and the specific design objective. 
Two target application domains, one more realistic or pragmatic, one more futuristic, are 
considered in this study: (i) self-sustaining buildings in desert environments and (ii) ELM-enabled 
habitats and infrastructure for the long-term colonization of Titan. These applications were 
selected as use case to evaluate the framework under markedly different boundary conditions, 
which are detailed in Table 5. 
From the range of possible pipeline combinations, a subset of workflows was selected to illustrate 
and assess framework performance. Accordingly, the results are structured around three 
representative workflow-based use cases as illustrated in Figure 4: 
i. 
Use case 1: data augmentation from bibliographic sources, together with coding-
performance validation on 20 unseen articles, in order to assess the ontology-grounded 
extraction capabilities of the model.  
ii. 
Use case 2: application-driven ELM innovation for target application domain 1 (self-
sustaining buildings in desert environments), implemented through workflow C (ELM 
Innovation) combining application-conditioned gap detection and ELM detailed design 
generation. Workflow C is executed multiple times to evaluate the stability and variability 
of the generated outputs across repeated runs. 
iii. 
Use case 3: ELM design for target application domain 2 (long-term colonization of Titan), 
for which workflow D (ELM retrieval) is executed multiple times to evaluate stability 
and variability of the generated outputs across repeated runs.

Prior to any AI-driven ELM design, generative AI is grounded to the developed ontology by 
following the methods described in subsection 2.3, employing the ontology and codification 
scheme, as presented in Table 1 and Table 2, and the knowledge on ELMs gathered in Table 3 and 
S1, as an ontology-grounded graph-based reasoning context. The model understanding and 
adherence to the ontology and codification scheme are evaluated using pipeline A. The ontology 
grounding performance of the generative AI model was evaluated using Prompt A and the model 
response is shown in Supplementary Material S3. The grounding validation step confirms that the 
AI model correctly interprets ontology dimensions, codification rules, and formal semantics. This 
validation enforces alignment with the ontology. 
4.1. Use case 1: bibliographic data augmentation and coding-performance validation 
Use case 1 consists of a bibliography-based data augmentation procedure implemented through 
Pipeline B. By expanding the database, this step increases the scope of candidate ELMs available 
for downstream selection in target-specific applications. This same data augmentation function is 
also used as a validation procedure to assess the AI model’s ontology-grounded coding 
performance. Performance was compared using full-text articles versus abstracts to assess 
whether abstract-only input is sufficient for ELM encoding. 
For this validation, the model is asked to codify 20 additional abstracts49–68 and full papers 
describing ELM examples that are not included in the current database (selection of Table 3 from 
the complete 100 dataset of S1) and are incorporated specifically for this evaluation. The resulting 
model-generated codifications are then compared against human codifications of the same 
examples. This comparison provides an external assessment of whether the model can reliably 
encode unseen bibliographic evidence into ontology-consistent ELM representations, rather than 
assuming the correctness of its outputs a priori. Table 6 presents the ELM codes obtained from 
generative AI-based abstract and paper encoding. Detailed information on the responses is 
provided in Supplementary Material S4, which illustrate the reliability and explanatory ability of 
the generative AI, even providing rationales for ambiguous codifications.

4.2. Use case 2: Application-conditioned gap detection and downstream ELM design 
Use case 2 corresponds to Workflow C in Figure 4 and combines Pipelines C and E for target 
application domain 1 (self-sustaining buildings in desert environments). The workflow is 
implemented on the initial dataset obtained from bibliography annotations. Application-
conditioned gap detection is then performed using Pipeline C to identify biologically admissible 
but absent or underrepresented structural combinations in the knowledge-graph database, while 
explicitly orienting this process toward target application domain 1. For each selected structural 
gap, Pipeline C produces a completed 7-slot ELM hypothesis together with its associated 
description, which are subsequently passed to Pipeline E for full design generation.  
To assess robustness and exploratory diversity, the workflow was implemented following a 
cascaded multi-iteration strategy. First, Pipeline C was executed in three independent iterations. 
Based on the outcomes of these runs, three ELM candidates were selected, one from each 
iteration, in order to preserve inter-run variability and capture potentially complementary 
opportunity spaces. 
Subsequently, Pipeline E was applied to each of the three selected ELMs. For each input ELM, 
Pipeline E was again executed in three independent iterations, thereby extending the exploration 
while maintaining consistency with the iterative evaluation framework adopted in the previous 
stage. From the resulting set of outputs, three ELMs generated through Pipeline E were selected 
for downstream analysis and as input for Prompt F, which was also conducted in three independent 
iterations per selected ELM to support final visualization and comparative interpretation of the 
generated designs. For every Pipeline output, the selected ELM candidates are ELM candidate 1 
of iteration 1; ELM candidate 2 of iteration 2; ELM candidate 3 of iteration 3.   
This cascaded execution scheme allows the analysis to determine whether the workflow 
converges toward recurrent solution patterns or, alternatively, produces distinct yet application-
relevant opportunities across iterations. Table 7 and Table 8 summarizes the results obtained from 
Pipeline C and Pipeline E for use case 2, whereas the complete generative AI outputs are provided 
in Supplementary Material S5 (S5_1 providing the generative AI output following prompt C and 
S5_2 giving the generative AI response to prompt E). Table 9 shows the generated images from 
prompt F. As output of workflow C, three ELMs fully instantiated are proposed for target 
application domain 1, after hierarchical analysis performed by the generative AI and our team 
about the different possibilities generated and discussed along the iterations:  
 
ELM candidate 1: BAC–BIO–EUB–FR–LCE–SLB–BFF+BMN 
 
ELM candidate 2: ARC–HYB–ARA–3D–LCE–SLB+BRE–CMM 
 
ELM candidate3: CRD–HYB–CRK–CP–LCO–SLB–CMM+BMN 
Intriguingly, the generative AI is capable of proposing novel engineered living materials (a total 
of 9 in the first stage if we consider the 3 iterations for the 3 candidates of Table 7), with a 
justification of their biological and structural plausibility and providing indications towards their 
technological materialization. In the second stage (results shown in Table 8), for the selected 
candidates, progressively more detailed and reliable descriptions are provided toward the practical 
implementation of these ELMs. Both qualitative and quantitative indications are provided for 
their engineering design, practical implementation and operation. The level of detail achieved and 
the explained design decisions demonstrate that the generative AI, after being trained with our 
described ontology and knowledge graph and guided with our collection of prompts, constitutes 
a remarkable companion for the discovery and co-creation of highly innovative ELMs with 
outstanding industrial potential. The detailed information of these discoveries is provided in 
Tables 7 and 8, in supplementary materials S5_1 and S5_2, and the resulting ELMs schematically 
illustrated in Table 9.

Concept description 
A branched bacterial mineralizing bioceramic layer that 
grows inside cracks or in near-surface sand networks to 
seal defects and stabilize wind-erodible ground around 
desert buildings.
A sealed mineralized façade insert containing 
nonpathogenic spore-forming bacteria in protected pores, 
designed to undergo useful day–night shape change while 
self-sealing microcracks after rare wetting events. 
A modular living ceramic service block containing 
biomineralizing bacteria for local graywater polishing, 
mineral retention, and autonomous sealing of microcracks in 
building service zones.
Justification 
 
Biological plausibility 
Spore-forming or mineralizing eubacteria are 
compatible with intermittent activation, 
dormancy, and reactivation after rare moisture 
inputs, which fits desert operation better than 
continuously metabolically active systems. 
 
Structural / material plausibility 
The FR phylum matches irregular crack paths 
and branched pore/sand networks, while LCE is 
coherent with mineral deposition forming a stiff 
ceramic-like repair phase rather than a soft 
biofilm only. 
 
Technological feasibility and practical 
implications 
This can be integrated as a low-maintenance 
repair grout, dry powder patch, or perimeter 
stabilization treatment activated by recycled 
water or brief wetting events; it is construction-
compatible and field-repairable without complex 
hardware.
 
Biological plausibility 
Spore-forming eubacteria are compatible with long 
dry periods, intermittent activation, and protected 
pore habitats; the living fraction can remain 
dormant under desert stress and revive after dew, 
maintenance wetting, or recycled-water exposure. 
 
Structural / material plausibility 
4D is appropriate because thermal cycling is 
functionally central, and LCE is coherent with a 
mineral-dominated façade insert intended for crack 
resistance, UV shielding, and abrasive dust 
exposure. 
 
Technological feasibility (non-prescriptive) 
A thermally responsive porous ceramic bilayer 
with embedded bacterial niches is feasible in 
principle using scaffold-based shaping plus 
mineralization; modular inserts are construction-
compatible, replaceable, and field-repairable. 
 
Biological plausibility 
Non-pathogenic biomineralizing bacteria such as 
Sporosarcina pasteurii or spore-forming Bacillus 
subtilis are compatible with dormant survival during 
dry periods and reactivation when graywater flows 
through the module. 
 
Structural / material plausibility 
A 3D living ceramic is coherent because porous 
ceramic blocks provide filtration pathways, 
mechanical stability, thermal resistance, and a 
mineral-friendly environment for precipitation-based 
crack sealing. 
 
Technological feasibility (non-prescriptive) 
Service-wall cartridges, replaceable filter bricks, or 
distributed utility modules are realistic integration 
routes; the concept scales better than exposed living 
façades and fits desert buildings where water 
recycling and maintainability are critical.

Concept description 
A replaceable cross-domain fiber cartridge, for example 
a fungal–cyanobacterial or fungal–microalgal capillary 
network, that traps dust, buffers humidity locally, and 
contributes to localized CO₂ control in air-intake or 
courtyard ventilation systems.
A lichen-inspired ramified cyanobacteria–fungal 
mineralized skin grown within a porous ceramic screen to 
intercept dust, locally polish CO₂, and slowly consolidate 
eroding building surfaces. 
A protected bacterial–fungal living render in which bacteria 
mineralize and fungi reinforce the matrix, enabling self-
healing, dust suppression, and stabilization of sand-exposed 
exterior surfaces. 
Justification 
 
Biological plausibility 
Cross-domain consortia are plausible where one 
partner provides structural colonization and the 
other contributes gas exchange or nutrient 
cycling, provided the system is immobilized and 
physically contained in a sheltered cartridge. 
 
Structural / material plausibility 
The 1D phylum fits capillary fibers and woven 
filter media, while LPO is coherent with 
hydrogel-coated or polymer-supported fibrous 
architectures designed for air handling rather 
than load-bearing structure. 
 
Technological feasibility and practical 
implications 
This is much more credible as a serviceable 
building subsystem than as an exposed façade 
skin: it can be swapped like a filter, isolated 
from occupants, protected from wind abrasion, 
and integrated into low-pressure ventilation 
hardware.
 
Biological plausibility 
A cyanobacteria–fungus consortium is cell-centric 
and biologically credible for a cross-domain ELM; 
both partners can tolerate repeated desiccation 
better than many hydrated cell systems when held 
in shaded pore networks and supplied only 
intermittently. 
 
Structural / material plausibility 
FR fits a ramified, branching colonization pattern, 
and LCE fits a mineral-rich crust or ceramic-
supported shell intended to face wind, UV, and 
abrasive particles. 
 
Technological feasibility (non-prescriptive) 
Patterned inoculation of porous ceramic screens 
and subsequent mineral consolidation are feasible 
in principle; the format is scalable as replaceable 
façade or wind-screen modules rather than as free-
growing environmental release. 
 
 
Biological plausibility 
A cross-kingdom pairing such as Sporosarcina 
pasteurii with Aureobasidium pullulans is cell-
centrically coherent: the bacterium contributes 
mineral precipitation, while the fungus contributes 
surface adhesion, extracellular binding, and crack-
spanning growth. 
 
Structural / material plausibility 
CP–LCO is appropriate because the render is 
inherently multi-scale: bacterial microcolonies, fungal 
filament networks, and a bulk mineral–polymer 
composite matrix act together rather than as a simple 
single-phase material. 
 
Technological feasibility (non-prescriptive) 
The concept is realistic only as a contained or semi-
contained render layer with controlled nutrient access; 
that constraint is acceptable in desert buildings, where 
serviceable façade skins and sacrificial outer layers 
are already practical design strategies.

4.3. Use case 3: ELM design for target application domain 2 via workflow comparison 
Use case 3 addresses ELM design for target application domain 2 (long-term colonization of 
Titan) by executing workflow D, which consists of an application-conditioned retrieval from the 
database followed by downstream design generation. In this case, candidate ELMs already 
represented in the database are selected according to their relevance to the boundary conditions 
and functional requirements of the Titan application domain (Pipeline D), and their corresponding 
7-slot ontological codes are then passed to Pipeline E for full specification. Workflow D operates 
on the initial dataset from bibliography annotations. 
For use case 3, the same cascaded multi-iteration strategy adopted in use case 2 was followed. 
First, Pipeline D was executed in three independent iterations. Based on the outcomes of these 
runs, three ELM candidates were selected, with one candidate chosen from each iteration (in this 
case the ELM candidate 1 of every iteration). Subsequently, Pipeline E was applied to each of the 
three selected ELMs, again using three independent iterations per ELM input. From these outputs, 
the first iteration of each candidate was selected for the final stage. Prompt F was conducted on 
the chosen final candidates also under three independent iterations to support the final generation 
and comparative assessment of the resulting designs (Table 12). This sequential implementation 
ensures methodological consistency with use case 2 while enabling the exploration of robustness, 
convergence, and diversity of outcomes in use case 3.  
Table 10 and Table 11 summarize the generative AI responses for use case 3, while the complete 
model outputs are provided in Supplementary Material S6 (S6_1 providing the generative AI 
output following prompt D and S6_2 giving the generative AI response to prompt E). As output 
of workflow D, three ELMs fully instantiated are proposed for target application domain 2:  
 
ELM candidate 1: BAC–HYB–EUB–3D–LPO–CAT+BTP–OPM 
 
ELM candidate 2: BAC–HYB–EUB–3D–LCE–SPE+SLB–BMN 
 
ELM candidate3: BAC–HYB–EUB–2D–LCA–SPE+ENP+BTP+CAT–BFF  
Further assessment is intended to examine whether application-conditioned selection from the 
database, when combined with downstream design generation, yields plausible and informative 
candidate solutions under the imposed boundary conditions. In any case, again, the generative AI 
is able to propose highly innovative engineered living materials (a total of 9 in the first stage if 
we consider the 3 iterations for the 3 candidates of Table 10), even if working upon and already 
existing type of ELM from the database in this third use case. Also in this case, a justification of 
their biological and structural plausibility is provided and indications towards their technological 
materialization are given. In the second stage (results shown in Table 11), for the selected 
candidates, descriptions with a remarkable level of detail and justifying reliability are provided, 
toward the practical implementation of these ELMs. Both qualitative, as well as extremely precise 
quantitative indications are provided for their engineering design, materialization and in-service 
operation. The accuracy of the descriptions and the well-justified design decisions demonstrate 
that the generative AI, after being trained with our described ontology and knowledge graph and 
guided with our collection of prompts, constitutes a remarkable companion, not only for the 
discovery and co-creation of  ELMs, but also for their reinvention or reengineering towards 
practical application in new environments and aiming at increased societal impacts. The detailed 
information of these breakthroughs is provided in Tables 10 and 11, in supplementary materials 
S6_1 and S6_2, and the resulting ELMs schematically illustrated in Table 12.

## Page 38


5. Current challenges and future directions 
5.1. Limitations of the study and detected challenges 
Despite the efforts dedicated to construct a comprehensive ontology and codification scheme for 
ELMs, evolving from previous pioneering studies and taking into account more than a hundred 
references for constructing and validating them, authors understand that the ontological 
framework requires additional efforts and contributions from the research community, to detect 
possible coding mistakes, missing processes or applications, lacking entities, and thus reach a 
more universal version usable as internationally-agreed design language for ELMs. To this end, 
community building efforts and the development of training events open to researchers worldwide 
would be extremely impactful, especially considering the current pressing need for regulation and 
standardization of ELMs to reach their safe deployment and impactful technological transfer to 
society. In addition, the study has been limited to presenting the ontology, codification scheme 
and current knowledge graph for ELMs and to proposing and analyzing the usability of a 
methodology for co-creating ELMs with generative AIs. The achieved results are purely 
conceptual, demonstrating that the generative AI employed understands the design language and 
contributes to ideating and describing the functionality and materialization ability of reinvented 
or newly discovered ELMs, but still lacking physical realization, which would require specific 
research projects and additional resources for each of the conceptual examples described and is 
beyond the scope of current study. 
5.2. Proposed research directions 
Considering the promising future of ELMs, and the potential to accelerate progress in the field 
through the synergistic use of our ontological framework, increasingly rich knowledge graphs, 
and our dedicated set of design pipelines and prompts for the systematic application of generative 
AIs in the discovery, reinvention and engineering design of ELMs, the authors foresee some 
relevant research directions. To monitor the evolution of the field and expand its knowledge graph, 
towards enhanced results and accelerated pace of discovery, the training of agents for 
automatically monitoring publications on ELMs, codifying the newly reported examples and 
continuously updating the database and knowledge graph would be an extremely powerful 
resource for the field. This could even lead to the automated generation of periodic reports 
highlighting the more relevant innovations in the field. Besides, the practical materialization and 
validation of the ELMs candidates and related engineering design proposals provided by 
generative AIs would be relevant to adequately assess the full potential of these AIs to support 
researchers in the co-creation of industrially and socially relevant living materials, especially as 
regards the contribution of AIs to foster safety and efficiency. The reliability of these AIs and their 
capability to (be trained to) detect and correct potential design errors and mistakes should be 
systematically evaluated. Dedicated failure mode and effect analysis methods for the field of 
ELMs should be implemented and the possibility of their contributing to a better performance of 
the generative AIs assessed. Furthermore, acknowledging that the ontological framework may be 
subject to constructive reviews and improvements, especially in connection with other ontologies, 
codes and design languages employed in the fields of synthetic biology and cell engineering, 
exploring its expansion or integration with existing languages of life would be highly advisable 
in such transdisciplinary field. Specifically, the efforts of the iGEM (International Genetically 
Engineered Machine) community and their contributions to the Synthetic Biology Open 
Language (https://sbolstandard.org/) and their visual representations of engineered cells and 
biological designs should be considered. Bridging the gap between the ELMs community and the 
main international synthetic biology alliances, through an integration of ontologies and 
codification schemes, can lead to the desired universal design language for biohybrid systems and 
engineered living materials and matter in general.


## Page 39


6. Conclusions 
The study has presented an ontological framework and a versatile codification scheme for 
engineered living materials. A comprehensive overview of the field with a relevant set of 
pioneering references covering from 2018 to 2025, which have been classified and codified 
according to the ontological framework, has enabled the creation of a current knowledge graph 
for ELMs. The collection of classified and codified ELMs constitutes probably the largest 
systematically gathered, analyzed and organized so far. The ontology, codification, knowledge 
graphs and information gathered have been used to train a generative artificial intelligence as 
design companion for future research tasks in the field. The experiments performed demonstrate 
that the generative AI understands the ontology and coding scheme, being able to classify and 
codify ELMs from natural language and viceversa. Besides, six different design pipelines have 
been implemented with dedicated textual instructions or prompts and applied to the discovery and 
conceptual design of innovative ELMs for two different scenarios, a more pragmatic one linked 
to ELMs for sustainable buildings in desertic environments, and a futuristic one focused on ELMs 
supporting the colonization of Titan. For each use scenario nine novel ELMs have been proposed 
by the trained generative AI and a selection of three detailed ELMs for each case have been 
conceptualized, their usability explained, their feasibility analyzed and their practical 
materialization approached by the generative AI. Our results show the intriguing potential for the 
application of methodically trained generative AIs to the accelerated discovery, conceptual design 
and engineering of future ELMs and to the automated monitoring of this fascinating field.
