Prompt A - Grounding and Consistency Validation 
You are an engineer specializing in engineered living materials (ELMs). 
 
You are given two reference documents: 
1. **codified_ontology_ELMs.docx**: a codified ontology for ELMs 
2. **ELM_graph.graphml file**: a pre-constructed graph representation of the dataset 
 
The ontology defines the full conceptual design space of ELMs. 
The knowledge graph is a partial, literature-derived instantiation of that space. 
 
**The ontology is the sole semantic constraint and must not be extended, reinterpreted, 
or modified.**   
**The knowledge graph represents bibliographic evidence and must not be used as a 
constraint on admissible ELM configurations.** 
 
If any information is unsupported, explicitly state: 
“Not present in the ontology/graph.” 
 
--- 
 
## ONTOLOGY DEFINITION (7-slot annotation model) 
 
Each ELM is described using 7 ontology dimensions: 
Domain, Superkingdom, Kingdom, Phylum, Class, Application, Production method. 
 
Multi-label annotations are allowed only where defined by the ontology. 
 
--- 
 
## GRAPH MODEL 
 
The ELM knowledge graph is a bipartite graph with two node types: 
 
### 1) ELM nodes


## Page 66


- Each unique ELM instance corresponds to one node. 
 
### 2) Ontology code nodes 
- Each unique ontology code (from the 7 dimensions above) corresponds to one node. 
 
### Edges 
- Edges exist only between ELM nodes and ontology code nodes. 
- Use the provided graph directly. 
- Do **NOT** add edges between ontology code nodes unless explicitly present in the input 
graph. 
 
## Task 
 
Validate correct grounding within the ontology and graph context. 
This validation concerns **grounding and internal consistency**, not completeness of the 
ELM design space. 
 
Follow the steps: 
 
1. Extract the exact ontology codes and definitions. 
2. Describe the codification order and syntax rules. 
3. Provide one or two decoded examples (reading the codified string back into natural 
language) using only ontology definitions. 
4. Explicitly flag inconsistencies, ambiguities, or deviations. If any, suggest standardization. 
 
## Constraints (strict) 
- Do not introduce new ontology codes, labels, or meanings. 
- Do not infer semantics beyond ontology definitions. 
- All accepted codifications and semantic claims must map to existing ontology codes. 
 
## Output (structured) 
1. Provide **ontology understanding confirmation**, providing a parse of the codes 
2. Provide a **coding logic summary** 
3. **Ontology–graph consistency check** 
4. **Ontology-grounded decoded ELM examples** 
5. **Issues detected** 
 
All claims must be traceable to the ontology or the graph.


## Page 67


Prompt B — Ontology-Grounded ELM Code Extraction (Abstract or Full Paper) 
You are an expert analyst in **Engineered Living Materials (ELMs)** operating 
**exclusively within the predefined ELM ontology provided in the context prompt**. 
 
You may receive as input either: 
1. an **abstract only**, or 
2. a **full scientific article** (PDF or full text). 
 
## Source Handling Rule 
- If the input is an **abstract**, base the analysis **only on the abstract**. 
- If the input is a **full paper/PDF**, use the **entire document** for analysis, including 
title, abstract, introduction, methods, results, figures and captions, discussion, and 
supplementary information if present. 
- **Ignore the references list for coding**, unless it directly clarifies organisms or methods 
explicitly used in the study. 
- **Do not mix evidence across unavailable sections**. Only use what is actually present 
in the provided input. 
 
--- 
 
## Ontological Constraint (Mandatory) 
- All classifications **must strictly conform** to the provided ontology. 
- **Do not introduce** new categories, codes, subclasses, interpretations, or assumptions 
outside the ontology. 
- If a category is **not inferable** from the provided input, mark it as `UNK` (unknown), 
**without extrapolation**. 
 
--- 
 
## Input 
<Insert either: 
- abstract text 
or 
- full paper / PDF / extracted full text> 
 
--- 
 
## Tasks 
 
### 1. ELM Identification 
Determine whether the provided text/document describes an **Engineered Living Material 
(ELM)** according to the ontology. 
 
- **ELM definition:** material, in which living cells, including eukaryotic, prokaryotic and 
synthetic cell types, and biomaterials as scaffolding structures coexist. 
- If **NO**, output: 
  - **Not an ELM** 
  - **One concise sentence explaining why** 
  - Then stop. 
- If **YES**, proceed with full ontology-based decoding. 
 
--- 
 
### 2. Ontology-Based Decoding


## Page 68


Decode the input using **only ontology-defined slots and codes**: 
 
- **Domain:** ARC, BAC, EUK, SDM, CRD   
- **Super-kingdom:** BIO, HYB   
- **Kingdom:** ARA, EUB, PRO, FUN, PLN, ANI, SKM, CRK   
- **Phylum:** 0D, 1D, 2D, 3D, 4D, FR, CP   
- **Class:** LAL, LCA, LCE, LCO, LPO, CRF   
- **Application(s):** ontology codes only (Table 2 of the codified ontology document)   
- **Production method(s):** ontology codes only (Table 2 of the codified ontology 
document) 
 
--- 
 
### 3. Code Assembly 
Construct the full **7-slot ELM code string** in the following order: 
 
**Domain–Super-kingdom–Kingdom–Phylum–Class–Application–Production** 
 
- Use `-` between slots. 
- If the input describes **one main ELM**, provide a single code string. 
- If multiple valid codes apply within a slot, concatenate using `+`. 
- If the input **clearly presents more than one distinct ELM**, provide separate code 
strings labeled: 
  - `ELM A` 
  - `ELM B` 
  - etc. 
- If the input is only an **abstract** and multiple ELMs are **not clearly 
distinguishable**, code the **main ELM described**. 
- **Do not omit slots.** 
 
--- 
 
### 4. Ontology-Grounded Explanation 
Using only information supported by the provided input and the ontology, explain in plain 
scientific language: 
 
- The **organism(s) involved** (species/strain if available). 
- The **material matrix** or material type(s) (if present; e.g., graphene, silica, polymer 
hydrogel, ceramic, composite). 
- The **geometry / dimensionality** (e.g., 1D fibers, 2D films, 3D scaffolds/lattices, 4D 
morphing systems). 
- The **intended application(s)**. 
- The **production method(s)** and the **biological–abiotic integration mechanism**. 
 
--- 
 
### 5. Integrated Concept Description 
Write a **3–5 sentence research-style summary** describing the ELM as a coherent 
scientific concept, remaining fully consistent with the ontology. 
 
--- 
 
## Evidence and Justification Requirements 
- **Base every classification choice only on the provided input.**


## Page 69


- If a slot is **ambiguous or not explicitly stated**, select the **best-justified ontology 
code** and add a **one-line rationale**. 
- If a slot is **not inferable at all**, use `UNK`. 
- **Do not invent data beyond the article/text.** 
- Maintain precise, technical, and reproducible language. 
 
--- 
 
## Output Format (Strict) 
 
### If NOT an ELM 
- **Result:** Not an ELM 
- **Reason:** one concise sentence 
 
### If ELM 
For each identified ELM, output: 
 
- **Ontology code:**   
  `XXX-XXX-XXX-XXX-XXX-XXX-XXX` 
 
- **Decoded slots:**   
  List the meaning of each code element.   
 
- **Organisms:**   
  Specific organisms mentioned or best-supported biological agents. 
 
- **Material(s):**   
  matrix type(s) if applicable. 
 
- **Geometry:**   
  Structural and dimensional features. 
 
- **Applications:**   
  Ontology-consistent use cases. 
 
- **Production method(s):**   
  Fabrication or integration strategy. 
 
- **Concept description:**   
  3–5 sentence ontology-grounded narrative. 
 
- **Rationales for ambiguous slots (if any):**   
  One-line justification per ambiguous slot. 
 
### Multi-ELM Case 
If multiple distinct ELMs are clearly described, repeat the full output format for each: 
- `ELM A` 
- `ELM B` 
- etc. 
 
---


## Page 70


Prompt C: Ontology-based gap detection for unbiased ELM discovery 
*(Structure-informed, plausibility-constrained generic or application-conditioned innovation 
of novel ELM hypotheses)* 
 
## Input parameters 
 
Gap detection mode: GENERIC | APPLICATION_CONDITIONED 
 
Target application domain: 
<Specify only if APPLICATION_CONDITIONED mode is used> 
 
--- 
 
## Role 
 
You are an engineer specializing in **engineered living materials (ELMs)**. 
 
The **ELM ontology constitutes the sole semantic constraint**, defining valid classes, 
codes, and admissible combinations within the ELM design space.   
**The ontology must not be extended, reinterpreted, or modified.** 
 
The **ELM knowledge graph represents bibliographic evidence only** and must 
**not** be used as a constraint on admissible ELM configurations.   
However, its **structural properties may be analyzed internally** to inform gap 
detection. 
 
--- 
 
## Operating mode 
 
Gap detection operates in one of two modes: 
 
- **Generic mode**: innovation is independent of application domains. 
- **Application-conditioned mode**: innovation prioritizes gaps compatible with a 
specified target application domain. 
 
If an application context is provided, operate in application-conditioned mode. Otherwise, 
operate in generic mode. 
 
--- 
 
## Task 
 
Identify and propose **novel engineered living material (ELM) hypotheses** by detecting 
**structurally informed gaps** in the provided knowledge graph within ontology space.  
A gap corresponds to an ontology-valid combination of **Domain–Superkingdom–
Kingdom–Phylum–Class** that is absent or strongly underrepresented in the knowledge 
graph. 
 
All proposed hypotheses must be: 
 
- cell-centric, 
- biologically plausible, 
- structurally / materially coherent,


## Page 71


- technologically feasible in principle, 
- and considers practical implications for decissions 
 
Innovation must remain **independent of specific applications or production 
optimizations**. 
 
In application-conditioned mode, gaps must additionally be compatible with the specified 
application context. 
 
--- 
 
## Internal analysis (not reported explicitly) 
 
Before proposing gaps, internally perform a **structural analysis of the ELM knowledge 
graph**, including: 
 
1. **Global structural context** 
   - overall sparsity vs concentration, 
   - dominance of specific ontology codes, 
   - degree heterogeneity and structural bias. 
 
2. **Ontology code relevance** 
   - centrality of ontology codes (degree, betweenness), 
   - identification of structural hubs and bridges. 
 
3. **Co-occurrence structure** 
   Co-occurrence analysis must primarily consider hierarchical relations across: 
    - Domain–Superkingdom, 
    - Superkingdom–Kingdom, 
    - Kingdom–Phylum, 
    - Phylum–Class. 
 
    Additional relations may be analyzed internally but must not constrain admissible 
combinations. 
    Application and Production co-occurrences may be analyzed only to understand dataset 
bias, not to define novelty. 
 
4. **Association regularities** 
   - high-confidence / high-lift association rules, 
   - dominant dataset-conditioned design patterns. 
 
This analysis must be used **only to contextualize gaps**, not to constrain admissible 
ontology combinations. 
 
--- 
 
## 1) Structure-informed gap detection 
 
Using the ontology and the internally derived structural context: 
 
- Enumerate **biologically admissible ontology code combinations** over the core 
structural dimensions: 
  - Domain   
  - Superkingdom   
- Kingdom


## Page 72


- Phylum   
  - Class   
 
- Identify combinations that are: 
  - **absent**, or 
  - **strongly underrepresented**   
  in the existing ELM knowledge graph. 
 
- Prioritize gaps that: 
  - occur in weakly explored ontology regions, 
  - involve underused structural dimensions, 
  - represent meaningful structural extensions, 
  - are not trivially excluded by biological constraints. 
 
In application-conditioned mode, additionally prioritize gaps whose biological and structural 
characteristics are compatible with the target application environment. 
 
Novelty must arise strictly from absence or rarity of the 5-slot combination. 
 
--- 
 
## 2) Generation of new ELM hypotheses 
 
For each selected gap, propose a **new ELM hypothesis**. 
 
### Required information (per ELM) 
 
- **Core ontology code string (7-slot)** 
 
  Domain–Superkingdom–Kingdom–Phylum–Class–Application–Production method 
 
  The **gap itself must be identified exclusively on the first five slots**   
  (Domain–Superkingdom–Kingdom–Phylum–Class). 
 
  Application and Production method must be specified **only to complete the 
hypothesis**,   
  and must: 
  - remain compatible with the structural and biological configuration, 
  - act as plausible enablers, 
  - not serve as novelty drivers, 
  - avoid optimization or prescriptive selection. 
  Production methods may be indicated only as plausible enabling routes. 
 
- **Concept description**   
  One concise sentence describing the envisioned ELM. 
 
- **Plausibility justification**   
  Provide 3 explicit bullet points addressing: 
  - **Biological plausibility**   
    Compatibility of the specified cell type(s) with the proposed organizational scale, 
interaction mode, and matrix environment. 
 
  - **Structural / material plausibility**   
    Coherence between the selected Phylum/Class and the physical, spatial, or material 
organization of the ELM.


## Page 73


- **Technological feasibility (non-prescriptive)**   
    Whether the concept could reasonably be realized using known or foreseeable 
biofabrication principles, **without fixing or optimizing a production method**. 
 
Application or production may be mentioned only if required for feasibility explanation. 
 
--- 
 
## Constraints (strict) 
 
- Enforce biological compatibility constraints. 
- If not in Application-conditioned mode, do not condition novelty on Application or 
Production. 
- Treat outputs as hypotheses, not validated designs. 
- Maintain cell-centric reasoning. 
- All reasoning must explicitly consider **practical implications**, including 
manufacturability, scalability, experimental implementability, and system integration. 
- Be **specific and explicit**: any referenced cell type, biological system, material, or 
structural feature must be clearly identified. 
- Explicitly justify choices.  
- Avoid trivial or arbitrary rare combinations. 
- Prefer a limited number of high-quality hypotheses over exhaustive enumeration. 
 
--- 
 
## Output style 
 
- Structured list of ELM hypotheses. 
- Clear separation between: ontology definition, concept description, and plausibility 
justification. 
- Concise, scientific language. 
- **No reporting of structural metrics, tables, or intermediate analyses.** 
 
--- 
 
Assume ontology grounding and graph analysis have been validated in previous pipeline 
steps.

Kingdom–Phylum–Class).**

existing or gap-derived candidates.** 
 
The present step prepares candidates for downstream ontology-driven ELM design.

## Page 77


Prompt E — Ontology-based AI-driven design of ELM 
You are an expert system for **Engineered Living Materials (ELMs)**. 
You have access to a **validated ELM 7-slot ontology** and its allowed codes. 
 
Each ELM is defined by: 
**Domain – Super-kingdom – Kingdom – Phylum – Class – Application – Production** 
 
This step performs **detailed instantiation only**. It must **not** re-select candidates, 
modify ontology slots, or generate new ontology combinations. 
 
--- 
 
## Task 
For **each provided complete 7-slot ELM code**, instantiate the ELM into a **fully 
specified, scientifically coherent and technically plausible concept**, selecting: 
 
- concrete biological entities, 
- compatible matrix or scaffold materials, 
- physically meaningful structural geometries, 
- realistic application deployment scenarios, 
- and implementable production workflows, 
 
all strictly consistent with ontology constraints and real-world feasibility. 
 
--- 
 
## Input format specification 
 
The system accepts ELM candidates in one or more of the following formats. 
 
### Format A — Application-conditioned candidate blocks 
Structured candidates from previous pipeline steps including ontology codes and contextual 
rationale. 
 
### Format B — Gap-detection hypotheses 
Ontology-valid ELM hypotheses requiring full instantiation. 
 
### Format C — Raw ontology codes 
Complete 7-slot ontology codes: 
 
Domain – Superkingdom – Kingdom – Phylum – Class – Application – Production Method 
 
Instantiation must rely solely on ontology grounding when only codes are provided. 
 
--- 
 
## Input data 
 
<insert candidate block here> 
 
--- 
 
## Global Constraints (mandatory) 
- **Do NOT modify, reinterpret, or replace ontology codes.**


## Page 78


- If information required for instantiation is unsupported, state: 
  **“Not present in the ontology/graph.”** 
- All reasoning must remain **cell-centric**. 
- Instantiations must consider manufacturability, scalability, experimental feasibility, and 
system integration. 
- No vague descriptions are allowed; organisms, materials, and processes must be explicit. 
- Avoid speculative biology or materials lacking realistic feasibility. 
- Instantiation must remain consistent with any functional, deployment, compatibility, or 
rationale information provided in the input.   
  When only ontology codes are provided, coherence must be derived solely from ontology 
grounding. 
 
--- 
 
## Required Output (for each ELM code) 
 
### 0) Code integrity check  
Confirm ontology validity and justify interpretation if ambiguity exists. 
 
### 1) Slot-by-slot decoding 
For **each of the 7 slots**: 
- State the **slot label** and the **meaning** of the code. 
- Derive the **design constraints** implied by that slot, including: 
  - Biological constraints 
  - Material constraints 
  - Structural/geometric constraints 
  - Process/production constraints 
 
### 2) Biological instantiation 
 
Select **specific organisms or cell systems** consistent with the code. 
 
- Propose **at least two concrete candidates**. 
- For each candidate, specify: 
  - Functional role in the ELM (e.g., mineralization, sensing, ECM production, actuation, self-
healing, drug release). 
  - Viability and growth requirements (oxygen, nutrients, temperature, pH, moisture, 
mechanical stress tolerance). 
  - Biocontainment strategy where applicable (encapsulation, sporulation control, kill-switch, 
auxotrophy, etc.). 
- Explicitly **justify why the selected organism(s) are preferred** over plausible 
alternatives. 
 
Then: 
- Select **one recommended candidate**, 
- Justify selection against alternatives, 
- Explain trade-offs. 
 
### 3) Matrix/material candidates 
 
Select **explicit matrix or scaffold materials** compatible with the **Class** slot. 
 
- Propose **at least two material candidates**, specifying: 
  - Exact composition (e.g., hydroxyapatite, bioactive glass 45S5, alginate–gelatin hydrogel, 
PEDOT:PSS, pyrolytic carbon).


## Page 79


- Compatibility with the biological component. 
  - Interface mechanisms (adhesion, biofilm anchoring, mineral nucleation, covalent or ionic 
coupling). 
- Explicitly **justify material selection** relative to alternatives. 
 
### 4) Geometry and structural instantiation 
Translate the **Phylum** slot into a **physical architecture** (e.g., 1D fibers, 2D sheets, 
3D scaffolds, 4D time-evolving structures, fractal arrangements): 
 
- Define geometry quantitatively where possible (e.g., fiber diameters, pore sizes, layer 
thickness, lattice unit cell type). 
- Explain how geometry supports: 
  - Cell viability and function 
  - Mass transport and diffusion 
  - Mechanical performance 
- Justify why this structure is optimal compared to other plausible geometries. 
 
### 5) Application instantiation 
Interpret the **Application** slot into a realistic deployment scenario: 
 
- Describe where and how the ELM is used. 
- Define **measurable success metrics** (e.g., crack sealing efficiency, mineral deposition 
rate, mechanical modulus, biointegration markers). 
- Identify main failure modes and mitigation strategies. 
 
### 6) Production instantiation 
Expand the **Production** slot into an implementable workflow: 
 
- Provide a **step-by-step production process**, including: 
  - Material preparation 
  - Biological component preparation 
  - Integration strategy (encapsulation, bioink printing, coating, infiltration, post-seeding). 
  - Post-processing and stabilization (curing, crosslinking, sterilization compatible with 
viability).

## A) **Futuristic hyper-real 3D (general / sci-fi)**

- Dark, uncluttered laboratory or abstract background.

- Integration of living and abiotic components.

- Hero 3/4 view preferred.

2. Coding logic summary 
Syntax rules 
The ontology supports a codified string made from the 7 ontology dimensions in the fixed 
order above. The document also states that categories may be combined by using “+” in the 
codification of an ELM. 
Grounded coding logic 
A valid codification therefore has: 
 
one code from each of the seven dimensions, in order; 
 
+ only where the ontology allows category combinations; 
 
no extra labels, no reordered slots, and no added semantics beyond the definitions in 
the ontology. 
What the graph encodes 
The uploaded GraphML is structurally consistent with this ontology-driven logic: 
 
it is bipartite in practice, with ontology-code nodes and ELM nodes; 
 
edges run from ontology-code nodes to ELM nodes; 
 
no ontology-to-ontology edges were found; 
 
each ELM node is linked to exactly one node for domain, superkingdom, kingdom, 
and class; 
 
some ELMs have multi-links for phylum, application, and production method, which 
matches the ontology statement that categories may be combined with +. 
From the graph inspection: 
 
160 nodes, 738 edges 
 
node groups: 100 ELM nodes plus ontology-code nodes 
 
every ELM has exactly one domain, one superkingdom, one kingdom, one class 
 
multiplicity appears only in some phylum/application/production-method 
assignments 
This is consistent with ontology-grounded multi-label codification. 
 
3. Ontology–graph consistency check

The graph is consistent with the stated modeling rule: 
 
ELM nodes and ontology-code nodes are distinct 
 
edges connect ontology-code nodes to ELM nodes

no direct edges between ontology-code nodes were detected

linked to each other unless explicitly present.

This work describes a hybrid bacterial engineered living material in which 
electrogenic Shewanella oneidensis MR-1 is integrated with an abiotic graphene 
nanowall sponge. The graphene phase acts as a conductive carbon scaffold whose 
surface is chemically tuned to become sufficiently hydrophilic and biocompatible for 
bacterial colonization. The resulting 3D living carbon biointerface supports high 
biomass density and enables stable bioelectrochemical current generation. Within the

ontology, this is most consistently interpreted as a bacterial, hybrid, 3D living carbon 
ELM for energy production and harvesting.

and microbial fuel cells, which matches ontology application ENP. The paper explicitly

Production method(s): 
A graphene oxide aqueous dispersion is lyophilized to form a 3D sponge scaffold, then 
thermally reduced under Ar/H₂ to obtain conductive graphene nanowalls, after which S. 
oneidensis MR-1 is injected/loaded into the scaffold to form the bioanode. 
 
Concept description: 
This work describes a hybrid bacterial–graphene engineered living material in which 
viable Shewanella oneidensis MR-1 cells coexist with a synthetic three-dimensional 
graphene sponge scaffold. The reduced graphene nanowalls provide a conductive 
carbon matrix and hydrophilic microconfinements that support bacterial loading, 
viability, and extracellular electron transfer. Functionally, the construct operates as a 
living bioanode for renewable energy harvesting, producing stable biocurrent in a 
microbial fuel-cell configuration, with the 375 °C-reduced scaffold showing the best 
balance between conductivity and hydrophilicity. In ontology terms, it is a bacterial 
hybrid 3D living carbon for energy production and harvesting. 


o 
OPM instead of a more specific production code: the reported route combines 
lyophilization/freezing of graphene oxide, thermal reduction, and subsequent 
bacterial loading, and this exact workflow is not named as a dedicated Table 2 
production category; therefore, Other production methods or combinations is 
the closest ontology-grounded match.

This abstract describes a bacterial biological engineered living material formed by 
genetically programmed bacterial cells that assemble into multicellular structures 
through synthetic adhesins. The work focuses on quantifying adhesion-related 
biophysical parameters and linking them to emergent macroscopic material behavior, 
specifically tensile strength. Within the ontology, the strongest interpretation is that the 
material belongs to the synthetic biology application space because it functions as a 
programmable cellular-material platform and toolkit. The production logic is best 
captured as engineering cells to produce ELMs combined with molecular self-
assembly, since engineered adhesion drives spontaneous multicellular material 
formation.  


o

film, fiber, scaffold, biofilm, lattice, or morphing construct.

ceramic, alloy, composite, or cross-class matrix. 
o

Concept description: 
This paper describes a bacterial engineered living material built from genetically 
modified E. coli whose outer membranes display complementary synthetic nanobody–
antigen adhesins. These engineered cells self-associate into a three-dimensional 
multicellular material, and the authors quantitatively connect molecular-scale adhesin 
properties to macroscopic material behavior, especially tensile strength. The main 
demonstrated construct is a dense, extruded bacterial material string whose cohesion 
depends on adhesin-mediated cell–cell contacts. Conceptually, the study positions the 
system as a synthetic-biology platform for rationally designing and tuning bacterial 
ELM properties. 
 
Rationales for ambiguous slots (if any): 
o 
Class = UNK: the ontology’s class slot depends on the predominant 
extracellular matrix material class, but this paper studies a cell-only adhesin-
mediated material and does not explicitly identify a matrix class that maps to 
LAL, LCA, LCE, LCO, LPO, or CRF. 
o 
Application = SYB: although the paper mentions relevance to bioprinting, the 
clearest directly demonstrated ontology-aligned purpose is a synthetic 
adhesion toolbox for engineering living materials, which best matches SYB. 
o

cells, and MSA is supported by the paper’s explicit framing around self-
assembled multicellular materials.

resulting material behaves as a compostable, healable, scalable plastic/paper-like film 
and is proposed as a sustainable packaging material. In ontology terms, it is best 
understood as a bacterial bio-ELM with a planar polymeric matrix fabricated through 
engineered biofilm/ECM production, self-assembly, and casting.

Concept description: 
This paper presents a bacterial hybrid engineered living material in which S. oneidensis 
MR-1 is embedded within a conductive 3D printed hydrogel matrix for pollutant 
removal from water. The hydrogel combines polymeric components with conductive 
carbon additive, allowing the printed construct to preserve bacterial viability while 
enhancing extracellular electron transfer. The 3D bioprinting process enables precise 
control of filament size and spatial arrangement, which in turn regulates diffusion, 
hydrodynamics, and degradation efficiency. As a result, the material functions as a 
designed living bioremediation platform for water purification. 


water purification, which aligns more directly with Bioremediation than with 
the broader category of biotechnology/bioprocessing.

engineered E. coli Nissle 1917 with a DOX-loaded tannic-acid/Fe³⁺ coating. The 
bacteria selectively colonize hypoxic tumor regions, while the coating protects the cells 
and carries chemotherapeutic cargo for stimulus-responsive release. A hypoxia-
activated lysis circuit improves biosafety by limiting bacterial persistence in vivo and 
simultaneously releases bacterial antigens that promote macrophage polarization toward 
an anti-tumor phenotype. The resulting construct functions as an integrated living 
therapeutic platform coupling targeted delivery, immune activation, and controlled 
bacterial self-elimination.

delivery and immunotherapy.

a separate coating/encapsulation fabrication route. 
 
Integrated interpretation of the paper 
The full paper clearly describes two closely related ELMs: the coated bacterial living material 
D@T@E and the engineered, hypoxia-lytic derivative D@T@E-PL. The main ELM 
emphasized by the study is D@T@E-PL, because it is presented as the final intelligent living 
material with improved biosafety and stronger combined chemo-immunotherapeutic 
performance in vivo.

Integrated concept description 
The paper describes a family of hybrid engineered living materials in which genetically 
engineered microbial biosensors are embedded inside mechanically robust, diffusion-permissive 
polymeric core-shell hydrogels. These ELMs are three-dimensional living sensing units rather 
than free-cell assays, and they are designed for rapid, portable, multiplexed chemical detection. 
The fungal and bacterial variants are combined at the platform level through spatial encoding in 
a microfluidic chip, where each bead population acts as a discrete analyte-responsive module. 
Ontologically, the work is best represented as separate fungal and bacterial hybrid polymeric 
3D ELMs dedicated to biosensing and environmental monitoring.

Result: ELM 
The paper describes one main engineered ELM: an engineered Escherichia coli biofilm with a 
genetically appended metal-binding domain (MBD) on extracellular amyloid fibers, used as a 
living anti-corrosion surface on steel in seawater-like conditions. The article explicitly frames 
this as an engineered living biofilm/ELM and shows coexistence of living cells, extracellular 
matrix, and an abiotic interfacing surface, with additional calcite biomineralization during 
operation.

Engineered Escherichia coli MG1655 derivatives, specifically the OmpR234

binding region of Pseudomonas aeruginosa, but the living ELM population itself is 
engineered E. coli. 
 
Material(s): 
The living matrix is a polymeric extracellular amyloid biofilm based on curli/CsgA-
derived fibers and associated EPS. During seawater exposure, the system additionally 
forms a mineralized calcite calcium-carbonate layer, giving the mature protective 
interface both organic/polymeric and ceramic/mineral character. 


A 2D surface-associated biofilm/coating formed on planar metal coupons, specifically 
X70 carbon steel and 304 stainless steel. The ontology definition for 2D explicitly 
includes biofilms and planar extracellular matrices. 


The document’s explicit end use is corrosion protection of metallic surfaces in 
seawater/simulated seawater. Within the provided ontology, the closest supported 
application code is SLS, because the construct functions as a living 
surface/biointerface that improves interaction with the surrounding environment by 
protecting the metal substrate. A dedicated corrosion-protection application code is not 
present in the ontology/graph. 
 
Production method(s): 
ECP: the cells are genetically engineered to alter curli production and display the MBD 
peptide. 
BFF: the material is produced by deliberate biofilm formation and extracellular matrix

BTI: the engineered biofilm is intentionally grown as an interface on inorganic 
metallic surfaces. 
BMN: the protective system is strengthened by biofilm-induced calcite 
biomineralization during immersion. 
 
Concept description: 
This ELM is an engineered bacterial living coating in which genetically modified E. 
coli forms a curli-based biofilm with enhanced affinity for metal surfaces through an 
appended metal-binding peptide. Once established on steel, the living biofilm acts as a 
planar biointerface that suppresses corrosion and also promotes formation of a calcite-
rich mineral barrier. The resulting construct therefore combines a living polymeric 
matrix with an inorganic mineralized layer at the metal interface. In functional terms, it 
behaves as a hybrid living surface for anti-corrosion protection in seawater 
environments, reducing corrosion current and improving electrochemical resistance on 
both X70 carbon steel and 304 stainless steel. 


ontology logic for hybrid living materials and the BTI production category.

Full text 
Result: ELM 
The full paper clearly describes two distinct ELMs: 
ELM A, an engineered Bacillus subtilis biofilm used as a sensing and adsorption material, and 
ELM B, a hybrid biofilm@biochar (BBC) material formed by combining that engineered 
biofilm with biochar for soil remediation. The article explicitly presents both the engineered 
biofilm and the hybrid BBC as engineered living materials for heavy-metal sensing/remediation. 
ELM A

The living chassis is engineered Bacillus subtilis, specifically derivatives of strain 1935

the living material itself is populated by B. subtilis cells.

The matrix is a biofilm extracellular matrix built around engineered TasA amyloid

This ELM is best classified as a biofilm-type 2D structure. The paper repeatedly 
describes it as an engineered biofilm (BF), even though the matrix contains fibrous 
TasA structures.

The biofilm is used for biosensing Pb²⁺, Cu²⁺, and Hg²⁺, and for bioremediation by

 
Production method(s): 
The ELM is produced by genetic engineering of cells to install promoter-reporter and 
adsorption circuits, followed by biofilm formation in culture to grow the functional 
extracellular matrix. The sensing circuit uses metal-responsive promoters controlling 
fluorescent reporters, while the adsorption function is achieved by TasA-MT display on 
biofilm fibers. 
 
Concept description: 
This ELM is an engineered Bacillus subtilis biofilm that integrates heavy-metal 
detection and heavy-metal capture in a single living material platform. The cells carry 
metal-responsive genetic circuits for Pb²⁺, Cu²⁺, and Hg²⁺ sensing, while the 
extracellular biofilm architecture presents TasA-metallothionein fibers that bind those 
ions. Functionally, the material behaves as both a whole-cell biosensor and a 
remediation matrix in aqueous environments. In ontology terms, it is a bacterial, 
biofilm-based, polymeric bio-ELM with sensing and remediation roles. 


2D: selected because the ontology explicitly associates biofilms with the 2D phylum,

Result: ELM. The paper explicitly describes “probiotic cellulose” as a living material 
composed of living probiotics integrated within a bacterial cellulose matrix, which fits the 
ontology’s definition of an ELM. 
ELM A 
 
Ontology code: 
BAC-BIO-EUB-3D-LPO-BMD-EMG 
 
Decoded slots: 
BAC = bacterial ELM; BIO = biological engineered living material; EUB = eubacterial 
ELM; 3D = three-dimensional ELM; LPO = living polymer; BMD = biohybrid medical 
devices; EMG = extracellular matrix grown in culture. 
 
Organisms: 
Komagataeibacter xylinus (Kx), the cellulose-producing bacterium, and Lactobacillus 
fermentum (Lf), the probiotic that proliferates through the cellulose matrix. The paper 
states that Kx produces the bacterial cellulose film and that Lf progressively invades the 
matrix during incubation. 
 
Material(s): 
The matrix is bacterial cellulose, described as a nonliving cellulose matrix formed by 
bacterial biosynthesis. Within the ontology, cellulose is best classified under living 
polymers (LPO). 
 
Geometry: 
The material is best read as a 3D cellulose membrane/pellicle: the article describes a 
thick gel-like membrane, reports a thickness of about 1.5 ± 0.2 mm, and shows by


## Page 125


cross-sectional FESEM on page 3 that probiotics progressively invade the full interior 
of the matrix rather than remaining only on the surface. 
 
Applications: 
The best-supported ontology application is BMD because the paper presents the 
material as a tunable living bio-ink of interest for 3D printing and states that it could be 
used to obtain health devices; it also highlights antimicrobial potential relevant to 
biomedical use. 
 
Production method(s): 
EMG is the best-supported production code because the cellulose matrix is grown in 
culture by Kx and the final living material is obtained by co-culture and subsequent 
probiotic proliferation within that biologically produced extracellular matrix. 
 
Concept description: 
This ELM is a bacterial cellulose-based living polymer in which a biologically 
produced cellulose matrix hosts living probiotic bacteria. Kx generates the cellulose 
pellicle, and subsequent proliferation of Lf inside that matrix tunes the viscoelastic 
response from more fluid-like to more solid-like behavior. The FESEM series on page 3 
shows increasing volumetric colonization of the matrix, while the photographs on page 
5 illustrate the corresponding macroscopic shift from a transparent gel-like pellicle to a 
more opaque solid-like material. In ontology terms, it is a bacterial, biological, three-
dimensional living polymer intended toward biomedical-device-oriented use as a 
tunable living bio-ink. 
 
Rationales for ambiguous slots (if any): 
o 
BIO rather than HYB: the matrix is bacterial cellulose grown by cells, not a 
synthetic scaffold or synthetic abiotic support. 
o 
3D rather than 2D: although the material is membrane-like, the paper analyzes 
it as a thick volumetric pellicle with internal bacterial invasion across the cross-
section. 
o 
BMD: this is assigned from the authors’ stated intended use in health devices 
and bio-inks, not from a demonstrated finished medical device in the paper. 
o 
EMG: this is the closest ontology-listed production route because the key 
structural matrix is extracellular cellulose grown in culture; the article does not 
map more specifically to another listed manufacturing category.

## Page 129


 
Production method(s): 
Cells were mixed directly into the photocurable bioink, extrusion-printed onto pre-
treated glass, and then photocured with 405 nm light to form a covalently crosslinked 
hydrogel. The biological–abiotic integration mechanism is physical entrapment of living 
cyanobacterial cells inside the polymer matrix, with matrix crosslinking and glass 
attachment mediated by methacrylate chemistry. 
 
Concept description: 
This ELM is a hybrid photosynthetic biocatalyst built by embedding engineered 
Synechocystis cells into a 3D-printed photocurable polymer matrix. The 
alginate/GGMMA/LAP scaffold provides a mechanically stable hydrogel environment 
that retains living cells while permitting light-driven metabolism and gas exchange. 
Within this construct, the entrapped cyanobacteria convert inorganic carbon into 
ethylene under illumination. The material therefore functions as a solid-state living 
catalytic platform for renewable chemical production. 


rather than constituting a cells-only material.

three-dimensional printed constructs, even though the printed format is a thin layer. 
CAT: selected because the ELM is used as a biocatalyst for ethylene production in a 
chemical-production process; this is more directly supported than broader application 
labels.

This ELM is a eukaryotic hybrid living material in which engineered Chlamydomonas 
cells are immobilized inside a 3D-printed photocurable hydrogel. The polymer matrix 
creates a stable structural environment while preserving photosynthetic viability after 
recovery from printing stress. Functionally, the construct operates as a whole-cell 
catalytic material that converts cyclohexanone into ε-caprolactone. In ontology terms, it 
is a protist-based living polymer designed for chemical biotransformation.

ontology places eukaryotic organisms that are not animal, plant, or fungal cells under 
protists.

rather than forming a cells-only matrix. 
CAT: selected because the demonstrated function is a catalytic chemical conversion, 
cyclohexanone to ε-caprolactone. 
Integrated assessment 
The full paper clearly describes two distinct ELMs, not just one platform, because it applies the 
same 3D-printed photocurable polymer scaffold to two different engineered living systems with 
different taxa and different catalytic outputs: a bacterial cyanobacterial ethylene-producing 
material and a eukaryotic algal biotransformation material. Both satisfy the ontology-level ELM 
definition because they combine living cells with a scaffold-like biomaterial in a structured 
printed construct.

ELM A 


 
Organisms: 
Fungal mycelium is the living component. The optimized biocomposite was reported as 
compatible with Trametes versicolor, Pleurotus ostreatus, and an Ecovative fungal 
strain, while the subsequent main experiments used Ecovative mycelium for indirect 
inoculation and colonization. 
 
Material(s): 
The matrix is a 3D-printed biocomposite made from chitosan, cellulose fibers, and spent 
coffee grounds; the paper describes chitosan as the binding matrix, cellulose as the 
structural reinforcement, and coffee grounds as the nutrient source that supports fungal 
growth. This is best aligned with a polymer-dominant matrix in the ontology.


## Page 133


 
Geometry: 
The main ELM is a three-dimensional printed biocomposite colonized by fungal 
mycelium. The paper explicitly describes high-resolution 3D printing of complex 
geometries, full colonization of 10 cm tall structures, and demonstration objects 
including bio-welded containers and flexible textile-like prints, but the core platform 
remains a 3D myco-composite system. 
 
Applications: 
The strongest ontology-consistent application is multipurpose smart/living materials 
and structures, because the paper frames the platform as enabling advanced structural 
mycelium applications and demonstrates functional behaviors such as hydrophobicity, 
self-sealing, flexible hinge formation, and textile-like deformation. Packaging-like use 
is discussed, but “packaging” is not an ontology application code in the provided 
taxonomy. 
 
Production method(s): 
The paper uses high-resolution extrusion-based additive manufacturing of a 
biocomposite scaffold, followed by fungal indirect inoculation and colonization from a 
mycelium bed. Because the ontology options do not exactly name this combined 
workflow, the best-supported production slot is OPM rather than a narrower code. 
 
Concept description: 
This paper describes a hybrid fungal living material in which living mycelium colonizes 
a pre-formed, high-resolution 3D-printed biocomposite made of chitosan, cellulose, and 
coffee grounds. The printed scaffold provides structural form and mechanical 
robustness, while the mycelium contributes colonization, bridging, hydrophobic skin 
formation, and regenerative sealing behavior. The resulting myco-composite is 
presented as a versatile structural platform with improved mechanical performance 
relative to previously reported mycelium composites, alongside demonstrators such as 
self-sealing containers and flexible textile-like assemblies. As an ontology-grounded 
concept, it is best interpreted as a eukaryotic hybrid fungal 3D living polymer for 
multipurpose smart/living material functions, fabricated through a combined additive-
manufacturing-plus-colonization workflow. 
 
Rationales for ambiguous slots (if any): 
Application = SLM: The article demonstrates several functional structural behaviors 
and multiple end uses, but no single narrower ontology application code is stated as the 
definitive main one; SLM is the best-supported umbrella category. 
Production = OPM: The workflow is specifically “3D-printed biocomposite + indirect 
inoculation,” which does not map exactly to a single named ontology production 
category; OPM is therefore the most reproducible choice. 
A stricter alternative coding, if you want a more manufacturing-specific interpretation, would be 
to test EUK-HYB-FUN-3D-LPO-SLM-3DP, but I consider OPM better justified by the exact 
wording of the paper and the ontology boundaries.

tumor microenvironments and enhancement of effector and memory T-cell responses to 
reduce tumor relapse and metastasis in mice. Within the ontology, this supports BMD 
because the system is used for healthcare as an advanced therapeutic biohybrid device, 
and BMR because the paper explicitly frames it as a “photosynthetic micron robot.”

The paper first genetically engineers DC2.4 cells using lentiviral constructs so they 
overexpress OX40L, 4-1BBL, and CD70, then isolates their membranes, and finally 
coats C. vulgaris by a membrane-processing/mixing sequence including extraction, 
ultrasound smoothing, magnetic stirring, and collection of the coated algae. This fits 
ECP because artificially manipulated cells are used to produce the functional ELM-
enabling biological interface, and OPM because the membrane-coating/combination 
route is a hybrid assembly workflow not explicitly enumerated elsewhere in Table 2.

Concept description: 
This paper describes a biological engineered living material built from living Chlorella 
vulgaris cells whose surfaces are coated with genetically engineered dendritic-cell 
membrane displaying OX40L, 4-1BBL, and CD70. Under red-light irradiation, the 
living microalgae generate oxygen photosynthetically, alleviating tumor hypoxia, while 
the membrane-presented ligands stimulate T-cell activation and proliferation. 
Functionally, the construct behaves as a microrobotic therapeutic biointerface that 
couples oxygen generation with immune co-stimulation. In mouse models, especially 
when combined with anti-PD-1, it improves anti-tumor immunity and reduces relapse 
and metastasis. 


o 
BIO rather than HYB: the coded micron robot itself is described as living algae 
plus a biologically derived engineered cell membrane, not as cells combined 
with an abiotic synthetic scaffold.

/ freshwater green alga, but it does not explicitly place it in Plantae; under the 
ontology, the safer directly supported kingdom assignment is PRO. 
o 
UNK for class: the ontology class slot is defined by the predominant 
extracellular-matrix material class (alloy, carbon, ceramic, composite, 
polymer, cross-class), whereas this paper describes a membrane-coated living

cell construct rather than a clearly classifiable extracellular matrix. Not

justified because the article repeatedly frames the system as a micron robot. 
o 
ECP+OPM: ECP captures the genetically engineered DC source cells; OPM 
captures the subsequent membrane-isolation/coating combination process.

advanced healthcare solutions connected to tissue engineering and regenerative 
medicine, and the abstract is explicitly framed in regenerative medicine, with light-
controlled release of a pro-angiogenic factor that promotes endothelial network 
formation. 


OPM is the safest ontology-grounded production code. The ontology explicitly includes 
under OPM the encapsulation of cells within engineered constructs for generating 
bio-ELMs and HLMs, which matches secure bacterial encapsulation in bilayer hydrogel 
constructs.

This abstract describes a bacterial hybrid engineered living material in which 
optogenetically engineered bacteria are confined within a bilayer hydrogel scaffold. The 
living cells synthesize and secrete a collagen-binding VEGF peptidomimetic under light 
control, enabling tunable local delivery of a pro-angiogenic cue. The hydrogel provides


## Page 139


containment and structural support while preserving bacterial activity and preventing 
escape. Within the ontology, this is most consistently interpreted as a 3D living-
polymer hybrid ELM for tissue engineering and regenerative medicine, produced 
through an encapsulation-based route captured by OPM.

Rationales for ambiguous slots (if any): 
o 
HYB instead of BIO: the ontology reserves BIO for cells plus their own 
extracellular matrices without supporting synthetic biomaterials; here the 
bacteria are encapsulated in a hydrogel scaffold.

Concept description: 
This paper describes a bona fide engineered living material in which genetically 
engineered bacterial cells are combined with a synthetic hydrogel scaffold to create a 
remotely controllable therapeutic construct. The bacteria are programmed with a light-
responsive circuit to secrete a collagen-binding VEGF peptidomimetic (YCQ), while 
the bilayer PluDA hydrogel maintains bacterial viability and prevents escape. The 
construct operates as a 3D hybrid bacterial–polymeric ELM that enables tunable light-
regulated release of the pro-angiogenic factor over several days. The released protein 
binds collagen-containing matrices and promotes angiogenic network formation in 
HUVEC cultures, supporting its regenerative-medicine function. 
 
Rationales for ambiguous slots (if any): 
o 
Application = TER rather than BMD: the paper frames the system primarily 
as a regenerative, pro-angiogenic material for healing and vascular 
regeneration, not as an already configured medical device. TER is therefore the 
closest ontology-grounded fit. 
o 
Production = ECP rather than OPM: although the hydrogel encapsulation is 
important, the defining functional mechanism is that engineered bacteria 
produce and secrete the therapeutic agent in response to light; this aligns most 
directly with Engineering cells to produce ELMs. 
o 
Class = LPO rather than LCO: the matrix is described as Pluronic-based 
hydrogel, which is polymeric; no evidence is given that the ELM matrix is 
predominantly composite in the ontology sense. 
Result: ELM. The article explicitly describes an engineered living material composed of live 
engineered bacteria securely encapsulated in synthetic bilayer hydrogel constructs for light-
controlled therapeutic factor release.

activation for pyruvoyl tetrahydropterin production, showing that the construct 
functions as a programmable microbial bioreactor. In ontology terms, it is a 3D hybrid 
bacterial living polymer directed toward biotechnology and bioprocessing.



The ELM is described as hydrogel films, including a bilayer film format in which a 
bacteria-containing hydrogel layer is enclosed by a bacteria-free outer hydrogel layer. 
Although film-shaped, the bacteria are encapsulated within a volumetric hydrogel

 
Applications: 
The study presents the material as a platform for conversion of cinnamic acid into 
pinocembrin, which supports biotechnology and bioprocessing (BTP). The paper also 
explicitly frames the system as a prospective living drug-delivery / living medical 
device for production of a bioactive flavonoid in the body, supporting biohybrid 
medical devices (BMD). 
 
Production method(s): 
The bacteria were genetically engineered and then encapsulated in photocrosslinked 
PVA-VS hydrogels; bilayer films were fabricated by sequential deposition and 
crosslinking of a bacteria-containing inner layer and a bacteria-free enveloping layer. 
The ontology does not provide a more specific dedicated code for this exact hydrogel 
encapsulation workflow, so OPM is the best-supported production-method code. 
 
Concept description: 
This work describes an engineered living material composed of probiotic E. coli Nissle 
1917 encapsulated within a synthetic PVA-based hydrogel matrix, forming a hybrid 
bacterial living polymer. The engineered bacteria catalyze the conversion of the low-
cost precursor cinnamic acid into the higher-value flavonoid pinocembrin while 
remaining spatially contained in bilayer hydrogel films. The material therefore 
functions both as a microbial bioprocessing platform and as a prototype biohybrid 
therapeutic device concept. Its central design principle is the integration of 
metabolically active engineered bacteria with a polymeric containment matrix that 
permits nutrient and product diffusion while restricting bacterial escape. 


medical-device platform (BMD). 
o

photocrosslinking, which is clearly described in the paper but not more 
specifically codified elsewhere in the ontology tables provided.

 
Material(s): 
The material is a pure mycelium skin/pellicle with no synthetic scaffold, making it a 
bio-ELM rather than a hybrid material. The paper describes fungal tissue with structural

and also mentions a possible polysaccharide coating at the liquid interface; after 
growth, the skins are plasticized with 20% glycerol and dried.


## Page 151


 
Geometry: 
The geometry is best described as a 2D sheet-like membrane / pellicle / skin. The 
material is grown at the liquid–air interface as a floating mycelium skin and used as a 
leather-like sheet, even though it has measurable thickness. 
 
Applications: 
The paper frames the material as a leather substitute and points to fashionwear and 
automotive seats as explicit future product applications. A furniture use is also 
mentioned in the paper, but a furniture-specific application code is not present in the 
ontology/graph. 
 
Production method(s): 
The material is produced by liquid-state culture / fermentation, followed by 
homogenization of fungal biomass, transfer to wells, static growth into a planar skin 
at the liquid–air interface, then glycerol plasticization and drying. In ontology terms, 
the closest supported production codes are EMG+BFF. 
 
Concept description: 
This ELM is a living fungal mycelium skin made from Ganoderma lucidum and 
designed as a self-healing leather-like material. Its key functional element is the 
presence of chlamydospores embedded in the dried mycelium matrix; after hydration 
and nutrient exposure, these spores germinate into new hyphae that refill punctures and 
cuts and reconnect with the existing network. The material remains viable after drying 
at 40 °C and plasticization with 20% glycerol, and healed regions recover mechanical 
performance to the point that samples often fail outside the repaired zone. As an 
ontology-grounded concept, it is a 2D fungal bio-ELM with a polymeric matrix, 
targeted primarily toward wearable / leather-like product applications and secondarily 
toward vehicle-interior uses. 
 
Rationales for ambiguous slots (if any): 
2D: Chosen because the material is a planar skin/pellicle/sheet grown at the liquid–air 
interface; although it has thickness, the ontology’s 2D definition best matches the 
reported geometry. 
LPO: Chosen because the reported material is a pure, flexible fungal tissue with 
polymeric biological structure; the paper does not describe a predominantly metallic, 
ceramic, carbon, or cross-class matrix. 
FAS+TRI: Chosen because the paper explicitly mentions fashionwear and automotive 
seats as target applications; furniture is mentioned, but no furniture-specific ontology 
code is present in the ontology/graph. 
EMG+BFF: Chosen because the ontology has no dedicated “liquid-state mycelium 
pellicle fermentation” code; the closest supported match is ECM grown in culture plus 
biofilm-related ECM growth for a cultured planar skin formed at the interface.

Production method(s): 
3DB is the best-supported production code. The ontology defines it as additive 
manufacturing involving biomaterials and living cells to create living three-


## Page 153


dimensional constructs, which matches vat photopolymerization of aqueous BSA-
PEGDA resins containing engineered microbes. 
 
Concept description: 
This abstract describes a hybrid 3D engineered living material in which engineered 
microbes are directly encapsulated in a BSA-PEGDA hydrogel during additive 
manufacturing. The living cells continuously produce metabolites such as l-DOPA, 
naringenin, and betaxanthins, and these metabolites actively modify the surrounding 
matrix by increasing stiffness or resistance to degradation. The printed ELM 
components are then assembled into mechanically functional parts such as bolts and 
gears, showing that the living constructs can operate as functional elements of synthetic 
living machines. Within the ontology, this is most consistently interpreted as a 3D 
living-polymer hybrid ELM for materials biofunctionalization and biohybrid-
machine subsystems, produced by 3D bioprinting. 
 
Rationales for ambiguous slots (if any): 
o 
Domain = UNK: the abstract only states “engineered microbes” and does not 
explicitly identify whether they are bacterial, fungal, or mixed. 
o 
Kingdom = UNK: no taxonomic identity is given for the encapsulated 
microbes. 
o 
BMR+MAP instead of only MAP: matrix enhancement is central, but the 
abstract also explicitly demonstrates assembly into bolts and gears for 
synthetic living machines, which is directly covered by BMR. 
o 
3DB instead of 3DP: the cells are processed within the printable resin during 
fabrication, not colonizing a pre-printed scaffold afterward, so the ontology 
definition of 3D bioprinting of cells and biomaterials is the closer match. 
Full text 
Result: ELM 
Finding: The paper clearly describes multiple distinct ELMs, specifically ELM-EC-LDOPA, 
ELM-EC-NGN, and ELM-SC-BXN, all based on engineered microorganisms encapsulated in 
a BSA-PEGDA hydrogel and fabricated by vat photopolymerization / 3D printing. 
ELM A — ELM-EC-LDOPA 
 
Ontology code: 
BAC-HYB-EUB-3D-LPO-MAP+BMR-3DB 
 
Decoded slots: 
BAC = Bacterial ELM; HYB = Hybrid living material; EUB = Eubacterial; 3D = three-
dimensional ELM; LPO = living polymer; MAP = materials production and 
biofunctionalization; BMR = biohybrid machines and robots; 3DB = 3D bioprinting of 
cells and biomaterials. The ontology defines HYB for ELMs combining cells with 
abiotic synthetic biomaterials, 3D for cell-containing 3D structures, LPO for polymer-
dominant matrices including hydrogels, MAP for biofunctionalization of materials, 
BMR for machine/robot constituents, and 3DB for additive manufacturing where cells 
and biomaterials are processed together. 
 
Organisms: 
Engineered Escherichia coli were used. The l-DOPA-generating strain was created by


## Page 154


transforming a tyrosine-overproducing strain, and the culture conditions identify the 
working strain as E. coli eBL0430D for l-DOPA production. 
 
Material(s): 
The matrix is a BSA-PEGDA hydrogel, formed from bovine serum albumin and 
poly(ethylene glycol) diacrylate, with Ru(bpy)3Cl2 and sodium persulfate used for 
photopolymerization. In this ELM, the encapsulated bacteria produce l-DOPA, which 
then interacts with BSA and contributes to matrix stiffening. 
 
Geometry: 
This is a 3D hydrogel ELM. The paper reports 3D printed cylinders, lattices, tubular 
stents, and specifically a nut-and-bolt assembly fabricated from the l-DOPA-producing 
resin. 
 
Applications: 
Best-supported applications are materials production and biofunctionalization 
(MAP), because in situ l-DOPA production enhances matrix stiffness, and biohybrid 
machines and robots (BMR), because the printed material is demonstrated as 
mechanically functional nut-and-bolt hardware for prospective synthetic living 
machines. 
 
Production method(s): 
The best-supported production code is 3DB, because the living cells and the BSA-
PEGDA biomaterial were combined in the same aqueous resin and then directly 
processed by vat photopolymerization / SLA 3D printing into living constructs. 
 
Concept description: 
ELM-EC-LDOPA is a bacterial hybrid living material in which engineered E. coli are 
encapsulated within a BSA-PEGDA 3D hydrogel fabricated by vat 
photopolymerization. The embedded cells continuously produce l-DOPA, and the paper 
shows that this metabolite binds to the surrounding BSA-containing matrix and 
increases compressive stiffness relative to the cell-free control. The construct therefore 
functions as a bio-augmented living polymer whose biological component actively 
improves material performance rather than merely being passively enclosed. Its printing 
into mechanically functional nuts and bolts further supports its interpretation as a 
candidate material platform for synthetic living machines. 
 
Rationales for ambiguous slots (if any): 
Application = MAP+BMR: MAP was selected because the central demonstrated effect 
is matrix biofunctionalization by in situ l-DOPA production; BMR was also selected 
because the same resin was used to print mechanically functional bolt-and-nut 
components for living-machine proof-of-concept. 
Production = 3DB: The ontology does not name “vat photopolymerization” separately, 
so it is best mapped to 3DB because cells and biomaterial are printed together, not 
colonized afterward on a previously printed scaffold.



This ELM is a 3D hydrogel construct fabricated by the same vat-photopolymerization

The best-supported ontology application is BTP, because the paper presents this ELM

a finalized medical device. 
 
Production method(s): 
The best-supported production method is 3DB, since engineered bacteria and 
biomaterial resin were processed together in a 3D printing workflow. 
 
Concept description: 
ELM-EC-NGN is a bacterial hybrid living polymer in which engineered E. coli 
BL21(DE3) are embedded in a BSA-PEGDA 3D hydrogel and printed by vat 
photopolymerization. Its defining function is the continuous production of 
naringenin, which the paper uses as a comparative example of bio-augmentation 
through metabolite synthesis. Although naringenin interacts with BSA, the paper reports 
that it does not induce the same matrix-structure change or stiffness increase observed 
for l-DOPA. This makes ELM-EC-NGN best interpreted as a bioprocessing-oriented 
ELM rather than a mechanically reinforced one. 
 
Rationales for ambiguous slots (if any): 
Application = BTP: Although naringenin is described as a therapeutic compound, the 
paper does not present this construct as a concrete medical device; the clearest 
supported use is metabolite production within a living material, which fits 
biotechnology and bioprocessing. 
Production = 3DB: The same rationale applies as above: cells and biomaterial are 
printed together in one additive-manufacturing step.

The matrix is the same BSA-PEGDA hydrogel. The yeast produce betaxanthins,

The best-supported production method is 3DB, because the engineered yeast were

 
Concept description: 
ELM-SC-BXN is a fungal hybrid living polymer in which engineered S. cerevisiae are 
encapsulated within a BSA-PEGDA 3D hydrogel fabricated by vat 
photopolymerization. The embedded yeast continuously produce betaxanthins, and the 
paper shows that this metabolite alters interactions with the protein matrix and is 
associated with a strong increase in resistance to microbial degradation. Unlike a 
passive yeast-loaded scaffold, this ELM actively stabilizes its own surrounding material 
through ongoing biochemical production. Its fabrication into working gear assemblies 
further supports interpretation as both a biofunctionalized material and a biohybrid 
machine component. 
 
Rationales for ambiguous slots (if any): 
Application = MAP+BMR: MAP was selected because the principal demonstrated 
effect is material biofunctionalization via enhanced degradation resistance; BMR was 
also selected because the same living material was printed into assembled gears with 
transmitted rotational motion. 
Production = 3DB: As with the bacterial constructs, the cells were included in the 
printable biomaterial resin before 3D fabrication, so 3DB is a closer ontology match 
than scaffold-printing-plus-colonization.

ELM A 
 
Ontology code: 
BAC-HYB-EUB-3D-LPO-BRE-3DB 
 
Decoded slots: 
o 
BAC = Bacterial ELM 
o 
HYB = Hybrid living material 
o 
EUB = Eubacterial ELM 
o 
3D = Three-dimensional ELM 
o 
LPO = Living polymers 
o 
BRE = Bioremediation 
o 
3DB = 3D bioprinting of cells and biomaterials 
 
Organisms: 
Engineered Synechococcus elongatus PCC 7942 cyanobacteria. The main functional 
construct in the paper is the laccase-producing strain with inducible lysis, denoted 
Laccase+-riboF-Lysis+; the article also includes reporter/control variants built on the 
same alginate-hydrogel platform. 
 
Material(s): 
The abiotic matrix is an alginate hydrogel, described as a natural 
polymer/polysaccharide scaffold, crosslinked with calcium salts and used as a cell-laden 
printable bioink. 
 
Geometry: 
The material is a volumetric 3D-printed hydrogel scaffold produced by direct-ink-
writing. The paper emphasizes grid-type printed geometries with high surface-area-to-


## Page 159


volume ratio and explicitly frames the construct as a three-dimensional hydrogel 
composite, not a 2D film or a 4D morphing system. 
 
Applications: 
The clearest ontology-supported application is bioremediation: the engineered laccase-
producing hydrogel decolorizes the textile dye indigo carmine. The inducible cell-death 
module is a biocontainment feature, but the paper’s stated end-use application is 
remediation of chemical pollutants. 
 
Production method(s): 
The best-supported production code is 3DB, because the study uses direct-ink-writing / 
3D printing of a cell-laden alginate bioink, meaning cells and biomaterial are 
processed together during fabrication. The paper does not describe a separately printed 
empty scaffold followed by later colonization as the primary route. 
 
Concept description: 
This paper describes a bona fide engineered living material because genetically 
engineered cyanobacterial cells coexist with an abiotic alginate hydrogel scaffold and 
produce regulated functional outputs from within that material. The platform uses 3D 
bioprinting to shape porous, photosynthetic hydrogel constructs containing S. elongatus 
PCC 7942, enabling external chemical control through a theophylline-responsive 
riboswitch. In its main functional form, the material expresses CotA laccase to oxidize 
and decolorize indigo carmine, establishing a clear bioremediation use case. The same 
platform also integrates an inducible lysis circuit to reduce environmental 
contamination by escaped cells, making the ELM both functional and partially self-
limiting. 
 
Rationales for ambiguous slots (if any): 
o 
HYB rather than BIO: the cells are embedded in an abiotic alginate hydrogel 
scaffold, so the construct is scaffold-based rather than cell/ECM-only. 
o 
LPO rather than LCO: although the paper uses the word “biocomposite,” the 
matrix itself is predominantly a polymeric hydrogel, and the ontology explicitly 
places hydrogels under living polymers. 
o 
BRE only: bioremediation is the only explicit end-use application clearly 
claimed and experimentally demonstrated at the material level. The YFP and 
lysis modules support regulation and containment but do not define a separate 
primary application slot more strongly than BRE. 
o 
3DB rather than 3DP: the study prints a cell-containing bioink directly, which 
matches “3D bioprinting of cells and biomaterials” better than “printed scaffold 
followed by cell colonization.” 
Identification decision: ELM. The article explicitly describes genetically engineered 
cyanobacteria integrated into an alginate hydrogel scaffold to form a stimuli-responsive 
functional material, which matches the ontology’s ELM definition. 
Scope note: The paper includes several engineered strain variants within the same alginate-
hydrogel platform. I coded the main application-bearing construct (Laccase+-riboF-Lysis+) 
because it is the clearest fully specified ELM with a defined ontology-supported end use; the 
reporter/control variants are better interpreted as validation variants of the same platform rather 
than separate end-use ELM classes.

Concept description: 
This abstract describes a fungal biological engineered living material formed directly 
from programmable Aspergillus niger mycelial pellets into self-supporting membrane-
like sheets. The living fungal network remains viable, self-regenerative, and functional 
over long storage, and its visible output can be tuned through inducible melanin 
biosynthesis. Within the ontology, the material is most consistently interpreted as a 2D 
fungal living-polymer bio-ELM, because it is a planar membrane grown entirely from 
fungal biomass without an abiotic scaffold. Its clearest validated function is biosensing, 
specifically color-based detection of xylose levels relevant to industrial wastewater 
monitoring. 
 
Rationales for ambiguous slots (if any): 
o 
BIO instead of HYB: no supporting synthetic biomaterial or synthetic structure 
is described in the abstract. 
o 
2D instead of 3D: “living membrane materials” and “large-area self-supporting 
structures” are more consistent with a planar material than a bulk 3D scaffold. 
o 
BSE instead of BSE+ENV: the abstract explicitly supports detection of 
xylose; environmental monitoring is plausible from the wastewater context, but 
the demonstrated ontology fit is more directly biosensing. 
o 
ECP+EMG: the fabrication includes both programmed gene control and 
growth of the fungal material itself; a single narrower production code would 
miss one of those two explicitly supported aspects. 
Full text 
Result: ELM. The paper explicitly presents a fungi-derived engineered living material grown 
from programmable Aspergillus niger mycelial pellets, forming self-supporting living 
membrane materials that remain alive, self-regenerative, and functional. 
 
Ontology code: 
EUK-BIO-FUN-2D-LPO-BSE+ENV-ECP+EMG 
 
Decoded slots: 
o 
EUK = Eukaryotic ELMs 
o 
BIO = Biological engineered living materials (cells plus their own extracellular 
matrices, without synthetic scaffold) 
o 
FUN = Fungal ELMs 
o 
2D = Planar / membrane-like ELMs 
o 
LPO = Living polymers 
o 
BSE+ENV = Biosensing + Environmental monitoring 
o 
ECP+EMG = Engineering cells to produce ELMs + Extracellular matrix grown 
in culture 
 
Organisms: 
The biological chassis is Aspergillus niger, specifically wild-type ATCC1015 and


## Page 162


engineered derivatives including the melanin-auxotrophic SΔpptA strain and the xylose-
inducible Spxyn-pptA strain. 
 
Material(s): 
The material is built from dense A. niger mycelial pellets / hyphae as the sole raw 
material. The paper states that the hyphal cell walls contain polysaccharides including 
chitin and α/β-glucans plus glycosylated proteins; in the engineered sensing version, 
melanin is additionally produced as a tunable pigment. This best supports a polymer-
dominated matrix. 
 
Geometry: 
The final construct is a large-area, self-supporting, flexible living membrane obtained 
by filtering pellet slurry and drying it on a glass slide. Although the fungus itself forms 
a 3D hyphal network, the ELM described and tested is a membrane / sheet-like material, 
which best matches the ontology’s 2D phylum. The figures on pages 4–6 visually show 
a flat membrane and its tunable color response. 
 
Applications: 
The directly demonstrated application is a xylose-responsive colorimetric biosensor for 
detecting xylose concentration in papermaking / industrial wastewater, which matches 
BSE and ENV. The article also mentions fabrics and packaging as future opportunities, 
but those are not as directly validated as the wastewater-sensing use case. 
 
Production method(s): 
The material is produced by genetically programming A. niger to control melanin 
synthesis, culturing the fungus to form mycelial pellets / hyphal biomass, then vacuum 
filtration, washing, and ambient drying to form the membrane. Ontology-wise, this is 
best captured by ECP+EMG: engineered cells are used to produce the material, and the 
extracellular/cell-associated matrix is grown in culture. The biological–abiotic 
integration mechanism is minimal: this is a bio-ELM made from fungal cells and their 
own matrix, without a synthetic scaffold. 
 
Concept description: 
This work describes a fungal engineered living material based on programmable 
Aspergillus niger mycelial pellets that are processed into self-supporting living 
membranes. The fungus provides both the living cellular component and the structural 
matrix, yielding a scaffold-free bio-ELM with a polymer-rich hyphal architecture. By 
genetically tuning melanin biosynthesis through a xylose-inducible circuit, the 
membrane acquires a visible, concentration-dependent color response that enables 
biosensing of xylose in wastewater. The material remains viable, self-regenerative, and 
functional after storage, making it a robust example of a responsive fungal ELM 
platform. 
 
Rationales for ambiguous slots (if any): 
o 
2D rather than 3D: the organism forms a 3D hyphal network, but the actual 
ELM decoded in the paper is consistently presented as a membrane / sheet-like 
material, so the final construct is best coded as 2D. 
o 
LPO rather than LCO: the described matrix is dominated by chitin, glucans, 
glycoproteins, and melanin-associated biopolymers rather than a conventional 
composite with a separate abiotic phase, so living polymers is the best-
supported class.


## Page 163


o 
BSE+ENV rather than broader application combinations: the paper directly 
demonstrates sensing of xylose concentration in wastewater, while fabrics and 
packaging are presented as future possibilities; therefore biosensing plus 
environmental monitoring is the most defensible application code. 
o 
ECP+EMG rather than adding further production codes: the central 
ontology-supported process is genetic engineering of the fungal chassis plus 
growth of the material-forming matrix in culture; filtration and drying are 
experimentally important but are not more specifically resolved by a better-
fitting ontology code than these two core categories.

Iteration 1 
Below, I treat novelty only on the first five slots 
Domain–Superkingdom–Kingdom–Phylum–Class, and I complete each hypothesis with 
Application and Production method only as compatible enablers. 
I write the 7-slot codes with dashes only for readability. 
All retained hypotheses were screened against your desert-building constraints: contained 
biology, desert-stress tolerance or realistic shielding, construction compatibility, field 
repairability, and low maintenance. 
 
Retained ELM hypotheses 
 
1) Mineralizing repair / sand-stabilization bioceramic 
Gap-defining 5-slot combination: 
BAC–BIO–EUB–FR–LCE 
Full 7-slot hypothesis: 
BAC–BIO–EUB–FR–LCE–SLB–BFF+BMN 
Concept description 
A branched bacterial mineralizing bioceramic layer that grows inside cracks or in near-
surface sand networks to seal defects and stabilize wind-erodible ground around desert 
buildings. 
Why this is a strong application-conditioned gap 
 
Biological plausibility 
Spore-forming or mineralizing eubacteria are compatible with intermittent activation, 
dormancy, and reactivation after rare moisture inputs, which fits desert operation 
better than continuously metabolically active systems. 
 
Structural / material plausibility 
The FR phylum matches irregular crack paths and branched pore/sand networks, 
while LCE is coherent with mineral deposition forming a stiff ceramic-like repair 
phase rather than a soft biofilm only. 
 
Technological feasibility and practical implications 
This can be integrated as a low-maintenance repair grout, dry powder patch, or 
perimeter stabilization treatment activated by recycled water or brief wetting events; 
it is construction-compatible and field-repairable without complex hardware. 
 
2) Sealed archaeal solar-modulating living film 
Gap-defining 5-slot combination: 
ARC–HYB–ARA–2D–LPO


## Page 165


Full 7-slot hypothesis: 
ARC–HYB–ARA–2D–LPO–SLS–BTI+OPM 
Concept description 
A thin, sealed archaeal living film inside a transparent polymer laminate that modulates 
incoming light and thermal load on façades or louvers under extreme heat and radiation. 
Why this is a strong application-conditioned gap 
 
Biological plausibility 
Halophilic archaea are among the most naturally compatible cells for high salt, high 
irradiation, and high temperature environments, especially when maintained in 
contained saline microchambers. 
 
Structural / material plausibility 
The 2D phylum fits a continuous interfacial film, and LPO is coherent with a 
polymeric laminate or membrane architecture that also provides biological 
containment and dust shielding. 
 
Technological feasibility and practical implications 
The concept is realistic as a replaceable façade insert or shutter cartridge rather than 
an exposed coating; this reduces abrasion risk, simplifies containment, and supports 
low-maintenance building integration. 
 
3) Protist-based living ceramic membrane for greywater polishing and cooling 
Gap-defining 5-slot combination: 
EUK–HYB–PRO–2D–LCE 
Full 7-slot hypothesis: 
EUK–HYB–PRO–2D–LCE–SLB–BTI+OPM 
Concept description 
A contained protist-derived living ceramic membrane, for example using silicifying diatom-
like cells on a supported thin layer, for greywater polishing coupled to evaporative or 
radiative cooling subsystems. 
Why this is a strong application-conditioned gap 
 
Biological plausibility 
Protists are a plausible choice for wet, shielded recirculating loops where biological 
activity is desired for water treatment or nutrient uptake, while remaining physically 
separated from the external desert environment. 
 
Structural / material plausibility 
The 2D phylum matches a membrane or surface-active treatment layer, and LCE is 
coherent with silica-rich or mineral-associated architectures rather than bulk soft 
biomass. 
 
Technological feasibility and practical implications 
This is better suited to enclosed utility cartridges than exposed façades; as a module 
in a water-recycling loop it is scalable, serviceable, and compatible with building 
systems that already require periodic filter replacement. 
 
4) Filamentous fungal mineralized repair tape 
Gap-defining 5-slot combination: 
EUK–HYB–FUN–1D–LCE 
Full 7-slot hypothesis: 
EUK–HYB–FUN–1D–LCE–SLB–FBT+BMN 
Concept description 
A fiber-based fungal living ceramic tape or liner that bridges small cracks, reinforces joints, 
and triggers localized mineral hardening inside sheltered construction interfaces. 
Why this is a strong application-conditioned gap 
 
Biological plausibility 
Filamentous fungi are naturally compatible with 1D growth patterns through hyphae 
and can colonize fibrous supports using limited nutrient reservoirs in a controlled, 
immobilized format.


## Page 166


 
Structural / material plausibility 
The 1D phylum fits woven or nonwoven reinforcement geometries, and LCE is 
justified when the fungal network is used as a template or mediator for mineral 
stiffening rather than only soft polymeric mass formation. 
 
Technological feasibility and practical implications 
This is attractive as a prefabricated repair consumable: a roll, strip, or insert that can 
be stored dry, applied locally, activated on demand, and replaced in the field without 
specialized bioprocess equipment. 
 
5) Cross-domain fiber cartridge for dust capture and local CO₂ conditioning

CRD–HYB–CRK–1D–LPO 
Full 7-slot hypothesis: 
CRD–HYB–CRK–1D–LPO–SLB–FBT+OPM
