A replaceable cross-domain fiber cartridge, for example a fungal–cyanobacterial or fungal–
microalgal capillary network, that traps dust, buffers humidity locally, and contributes to 
localized CO₂ control in air-intake or courtyard ventilation systems. 
Why this is a strong application-conditioned gap 
 
Biological plausibility 
Cross-domain consortia are plausible where one partner provides structural 
colonization and the other contributes gas exchange or nutrient cycling, provided the 
system is immobilized and physically contained in a sheltered cartridge. 
 
Structural / material plausibility 
The 1D phylum fits capillary fibers and woven filter media, while LPO is coherent 
with hydrogel-coated or polymer-supported fibrous architectures designed for air 
handling rather than load-bearing structure. 
 
Technological feasibility and practical implications 
This is much more credible as a serviceable building subsystem than as an exposed 
façade skin: it can be swapped like a filter, isolated from occupants, protected from 
wind abrasion, and integrated into low-pressure ventilation hardware. 
 
6) Longer-horizon, high-containment synthetic-cell water-recycling cartridge

Full 7-slot hypothesis:

A 3D polymeric cartridge populated by synthetic or artificial cells for tightly contained

The ontology explicitly admits synthetic-cell ELMs, and this use case benefits from

The 3D phylum suits porous cartridge volumes, and LPO is coherent with hydrogel

Screening decisions: gaps I did not retain


## Page 167


I excluded several ontology-valid regions from the final shortlist because they fit the 
ontology but fit your boundary conditions poorly. 
Rejected pattern A: exposed plant-dominant living skins 
I did not prioritize open PLN façade or roof systems that require continuous hydration and 
direct environmental exposure. Even if ontology-valid, they are hard to reconcile with scarce 
freshwater, low maintenance, and desert abrasion unless moved into highly shielded modules, 
at which point other kingdoms become more practical. 
Rejected pattern B: highly exposed 0D particulate ELMs 
I did not prioritize 0D candidates for desert exteriors because wind-driven sand and dust 
make particle loss, abrasion, and containment more problematic. They may still be useful 
inside sealed cartridges, but they are less compelling than 1D, 2D, FR, or 3D architectures for 
building subsystems. 
Rejected pattern C: open 4D morphing concepts 
I did not prioritize 4D building-envelope hypotheses because shape-morphing living systems 
add control and maintenance burdens that are difficult to justify under desert field conditions 
unless the system is very well protected and modular. 
Overall recommendation 
For near- to mid-term discovery, the strongest directions are: 
1. BAC–BIO–EUB–FR–LCE for self-healing and sand stabilization 
2. ARC–HYB–ARA–2D–LPO for solar/thermal façade control under extreme radiation 
3. CRD–HYB–CRK–1D–LPO for serviceable dust and air-management cartridges 
These three best satisfy your combined requirements of containment, desert robustness, 
construction compatibility, and low-maintenance deployment while still occupying genuinely 
weakly explored ontology regions.

while keeping the biology shielded and contained.

Deprioritized gap families 
I did not prioritize the following, despite ontology validity: 
 
Animal-cell ELM gaps: rejected for this use case because hydration, containment, 
sterility, and maintenance burdens are poorly aligned with desert buildings. 
 
Open, highly hydrated 2D biofilm/hydrogel concepts: rejected because low humidity, 
UV, and wind-driven abrasion make them hard to sustain without excessive shielding 
and water input. 
 
Synthetic-cell-led construction concepts: deprioritized because field repair, scale-up, 
and deployment readiness are weaker than for bacterial, fungal, protist, or archaeal 
routes. 
These five hypotheses are the strongest ontology-valid gaps I would carry forward for first-
pass experimental screening in the stated desert-building context.

contained hybrid systems rather than exposed bio-ELMs,

1) Archaeal ceramic humidity-buffering façade module 
Gap status: absent on the 5-slot core 
Core 5-slot gap: ARC–HYB–ARA–3D–LCE 
7-slot hypothesis: ARC–HYB–ARA–3D–LCE–SLB–3DP 
Concept description 
A sealed porous ceramic façade cartridge colonized by halophilic archaea, designed to buffer 
humidity, tolerate intense UV, and contribute to passive thermal regulation in desert 
buildings. 
Plausibility justification 
 
Biological plausibility 
Halophilic archaea such as Haloferax volcanii can tolerate high salinity, strong 
irradiation, and repeated dehydration–rehydration cycles; confinement in brine-filled 
microreservoirs also improves biological containment and reduces contamination 
risk. 
 
Structural / material plausibility 
A 3D living ceramic is coherent here because a porous aluminosilicate or geopolymer 
ceramic can provide thermal mass, capillary storage, abrasion resistance, and UV-
stable housing for the cells. 
 
Technological feasibility (non-prescriptive) 
Porous ceramic modules are construction-compatible and scalable; post-fabrication 
inoculation of protected internal pore networks is feasible in principle, and the 
cartridge format supports replacement and low-maintenance operation.

A cross-kingdom pairing such as Sporosarcina pasteurii with Aureobasidium

while the fungus contributes surface adhesion, extracellular binding, and crack-

Technological feasibility (non-prescriptive)

controlled nutrient access; that constraint is acceptable in desert buildings, where

ELM candidate 1: BAC–BIO–EUB–FR–LCE–SLB–BFF+BMN 
Iteration 1 
0) Code integrity check 
Code provided: BAC–BIO–EUB–FR–LCE–SLB–BFF+BMN 
Ontology validity: Valid as a 7-slot ELM code with a combined production slot. In the 
ontology, BAC is bacterial, BIO is biological engineered living material, EUB is eubacterial, 
FR is fractal, LCE is living ceramics, SLB is smart and living buildings, and BFF+BMN is a 
valid combined production description because the ontology explicitly allows category 
combinations with “+”.  
Ambiguity and resolution: 
There is one relevant tension: BFF is described as biofilm-based trapping or anchoring of 
inorganic particles and is noted as usually leading to living composites, mainly HLMs, 
whereas BIO excludes supporting synthetic biomaterials or synthetic structures. I resolve this 
without changing the code by interpreting the inorganic phase as cell-generated or cell-
localized mineral matter formed in situ and retained within the bacterial biofilm/extracellular 
matrix, not as an externally imposed synthetic scaffold. That interpretation remains consistent 
with BIO, LCE, and BMN.  
Graph support: 
The exact full 8-code combination was not present in the graph when checked directly. The 
graph does contain nearby bacterial mineralizing building-related precedents, including 
crack-healing calcite-precipitating concrete and soil-biocementation ELMs, but not this exact 
FR + BFF full hypothesis. Therefore, this candidate is ontology-valid and graph-adjacent, but 
not present in the graph as an exact instantiation. 
 
1) Slot-by-slot decoding 
Domain — BAC 
Meaning: Bacterial ELMs.  
Design constraints 
 
Biological constraints: the active component must be bacterial cells.  
 
Material constraints: extracellular matrix and mineral phase must be bacterial-derived 
or bacterial-mediated.


## Page 175


 
Structural/geometric constraints: architecture must support bacterial colonization, 
dormancy, reactivation, and local mineral precipitation.  
 
Process/production constraints: cultivation, sporulation, deposition, and activation 
steps must be compatible with bacteria rather than eukaryotic tissue culture.  
Super-kingdom — BIO 
Meaning: Biological engineered living materials made only of cells and their extracellular 
matrices, without supporting synthetic biomaterials or synthetic structures.  
Design constraints 
 
Biological constraints: cells and their ECM must supply the living component and 
functional matrix.  
 
Material constraints: no permanent synthetic scaffold can define the ELM.  
 
Structural/geometric constraints: the structural phase must emerge from 
biofilm/ECM/mineral accretion in situ.  
 
Process/production constraints: formulation may include temporary nutrients or salts, 
but the final functional material cannot rely on a synthetic support architecture.  
Kingdom — EUB 
Meaning: Eubacterial ELMs.  
Design constraints 
 
Biological constraints: candidate organisms must be true bacteria, not archaea or 
fungi.  
 
Material constraints: mineral nucleation pathways should be consistent with known 
bacterial biomineralization or biofilm-mediated deposition.  
 
Structural/geometric constraints: bacterial biofilms and microcolonies must be the 
operative cell organization.  
 
Process/production constraints: standard bacterial culture, sporulation, and 
inoculation workflows are appropriate.  
Phylum — FR 
Meaning: Fractal ELMs with irregular, fractured, or self-similar structures that cannot be 
defined by integer dimensional values.  
Design constraints 
 
Biological constraints: cells must remain viable in irregular crack and pore networks 
with heterogeneous nutrient and moisture fields.  
 
Material constraints: the mineralizing matrix must conform to branched, tortuous, 
multiscale voids.  
 
Structural/geometric constraints: the target architecture is not a uniform slab; it is a 
crack network or sand-pore branching topology.  
 
Process/production constraints: infiltration and growth must occur through capillary-
driven or moisture-triggered percolation rather than mold-defined shaping.  
Class — LCE 
Meaning: Living ceramics; extracellular matrices made predominantly of ceramics, including 
oxides, silicates, carbides, and nitrides.  
Design constraints 
 
Biological constraints: cells must drive ceramic-like mineral deposition and tolerate 
the local alkalinity and ionic environment.  
 
Material constraints: the final dominant non-cellular phase should be 
mineral/ceramic, not polymer-dominant.  
 
Structural/geometric constraints: the mineral must stiffen and bridge defects while 
preserving at least limited living niches.  
 
Process/production constraints: mineralizing chemistries must be compatible with 
bacterial viability during formation and dormancy after curing.  
Application — SLB 
Meaning: Smart and living buildings.  
Design constraints 
 
Biological constraints: the system must survive building-envelope conditions such as 
desiccation, temperature swings, UV exposure, and intermittent wetting.


## Page 176


 
Material constraints: the ELM must bond to masonry-like crack walls or sand grains 
and contribute to stabilization.  
 
Structural/geometric constraints: it must function in near-surface crack networks, 
façade defects, or soil/building interfaces.  
 
Process/production constraints: deployment must be field-compatible, low-
maintenance, and implementable as patch, grout, or infiltrant.  
Production — BFF 
Meaning: Biofilm-based trapping or anchoring of inorganic particles/materials and biofilms 
interfacing with inorganic materials.  
Design constraints 
 
Biological constraints: organisms must produce robust ECM/biofilm capable of 
adhesion to mineral surfaces.  
 
Material constraints: trapped or anchored inorganic matter must become integrated 
into the living ceramic phase.  
 
Structural/geometric constraints: biofilm must bridge crack walls and bind granular 
substrates.  
 
Process/production constraints: colonization must start on an inorganic substrate such 
as masonry fines, silica-rich sand, or crack-wall minerals.  
Production — BMN 
Meaning: Biomineralization.  
Design constraints 
 
Biological constraints: cells must actively induce mineral nucleation and growth.  
 
Material constraints: mineral phase should be calcium carbonate or a related ceramic 
deposition consistent with bacterial pathways.  
 
Structural/geometric constraints: mineral deposition must occur throughout the 
branched defect network, not only at the exposed surface.  
 
Process/production constraints: ion supply, alkalinity generation, nucleation control, 
and moisture pulses must be part of the workflow.  
 
2) Biological instantiation 
Candidate 1 — Sporosarcina pasteurii 
Functional role: ureolytic biomineralization; rapid CaCO₃ precipitation for crack sealing and 
sand biocementation; biofilm-assisted mineral anchoring. 
Viability and growth requirements 
 
Oxygen: aerobic to facultatively tolerant field conditions.  
 
Nutrients: low-complexity medium; urea and calcium source during activation.  
 
Temperature: practical activity in warm built environments.  
 
pH: tolerates alkaline microenvironments generated during ureolysis.  
 
Moisture: intermittent wetting compatible; can be delivered as spores.  
 
Mechanical stress tolerance: suitable for immobilization in pores and crack walls.  
Biocontainment strategy 
 
Spore-based dormant formulation.  
 
Nutrient-limited activation only after wetting.  
 
Encapsulation in a temporary mineral nutrient granule that dissolves on activation.  
 
No continuous feed, reducing uncontrolled spread.  
Why preferred 
 
Directly aligned with mineralizing repair and sand stabilization.  
 
Strong precedent for biocementation and crack repair.  
 
Spore formation suits desert cyclic wetting.  
Candidate 2 — Bacillus subtilis mineralizing strain 
Functional role: biofilm production, surface adhesion, and biomineralization support through 
alkaline metabolism and ECM-mediated nucleation. 
Viability and growth requirements 
 
Oxygen: aerobic.  
 
Nutrients: simple carbon/nitrogen input.


## Page 177


 
Temperature: robust across fluctuating ambient conditions.  
 
pH: tolerates alkaline ranges better than many non-spore-formers.  
 
Moisture: survives desiccation better than many vegetative bacteria due to 
sporulation capacity.  
 
Mechanical stress tolerance: strong biofilm former on mineral substrates.  
Biocontainment strategy 
 
Sporulation-controlled dormant inoculum.  
 
Nutrient-limited one-shot activation.  
 
Optional auxotrophic laboratory strain for pilot deployment.  
Why preferred / not preferred 
 
Better biofilm architecture than many ureolytic specialists.  
 
Less direct and less efficient mineralization than S. pasteurii for fast ceramic infill.  
Recommended candidate 
Recommended organism: Sporosarcina pasteurii 
Justification against alternatives 
 
Compared with B. subtilis, it is more directly specialized for high-yield calcite 
precipitation and therefore better matches LCE and BMN.  
 
Its sporulation-compatible lifecycle better fits rare moisture pulses and long dry 
intervals.  
 
It is more efficient for achieving measurable defect filling per activation cycle.  
Trade-offs 
 
Ureolytic systems can generate ammonium as a by-product, requiring dosage control. 
 
B. subtilis offers stronger native biofilm versatility, but lower mineral productivity for 
the same activation event.  
 
3) Matrix/material candidates 
Candidate 1 — In situ bacterially precipitated calcite (CaCO₃) with bacterial ECM 
Exact composition: calcite-dominant calcium carbonate mineral phase integrated with 
bacterial extracellular polymeric substances. 
Compatibility with biology 
 
Direct product of bacterial biomineralization.  
 
ECM provides nucleation loci and adhesion to crack walls and sand grains.  
Interface mechanisms 
 
Electrostatic binding of Ca²⁺ to EPS.  
 
Nucleation on cell walls and extracellular polymers.  
 
Mineral bridge formation between grains and crack faces.  
Why selected 
 
Strongest match to LCE and BMN.  
 
Compatible with BIO because the ceramic phase is biologically generated in situ.  
Candidate 2 — CaCO₃–silica microcomposite formed in situ from local sand fines plus 
bacterial biofilm 
Exact composition: bacterial EPS/mineral network integrating precipitated CaCO₃ with native 
silica-rich fines or sand particles already present in the defect environment. 
Compatibility with biology 
 
Bacteria colonize silica-rich granular substrates well.  
 
Sand provides abundant inert mineral interfaces for attachment.  
Interface mechanisms 
 
Biofilm adhesion to silicate surfaces.  
 
EPS-mediated particle trapping.  
 
Calcite overgrowth that cements adjacent grains.  
Why selected 
 
Better for sand stabilization than pure crack filling.  
 
Preserves BIO because the inorganic granular substrate is the deployment 
environment, not a synthetic scaffold.  
Preferred material system


## Page 178


Recommended material: calcite-dominant CaCO₃/ECM ceramic phase with optional 
incorporation of native silica fines where present. 
Justification relative to alternatives 
 
Pure silica gel, polymer grout, or externally added hydrogel would move the concept 
toward HYB or away from LCE.  
 
A calcite-dominant system most faithfully matches a bacterial living ceramic for 
construction repair.  
 
Environmental silica is useful as a local substrate, but should not dominate the matrix 
if LCE is to remain the primary class.  
 
4) Geometry and structural instantiation 
Phylum-derived architecture: multiscale fractal mineralizing network occupying branched 
cracks and near-surface sand pore paths. 
Quantitative geometry 
 
Primary crack aperture target: ~50 µm to 2 mm.  
 
Secondary branch penetration: ~10 µm to 500 µm.  
 
Mineralized bridge thickness: ~20–200 µm per activation cycle, accumulating over 
repeated wetting events.  
 
Sand pore-scale binding domain: grain-to-grain contacts in the ~50–500 µm scale for 
fine-to-medium sand.  
 
Colonized depth: first 1–10 mm from the exposed surface for realistic passive 
infiltration.  
How geometry supports function 
 
Cell viability and function: irregular branching retains protected microniches with 
higher local humidity than the exposed exterior.  
 
Mass transport and diffusion: capillary infiltration during wetting distributes ions and 
dormant spores through tortuous pathways.  
 
Mechanical performance: mineral bridges across hierarchical branches reduce crack 
connectivity and increase near-surface soil cohesion.  
Why this structure is optimal 
A planar film would not reach defect depth; a bulk 3D scaffold would violate BIO; a simple 
linear geometry would not match crack branching or sand pore networks. The fractal 
architecture is the most faithful translation of FR because sealing performance depends on 
occupying irregular multiscale pathways rather than forming a uniform shell. 
 
5) Application instantiation 
Deployment scenario: a dry powder or concentrated spore-salt treatment is brushed, injected, 
or sprayed onto cracked earthen/mineral building surfaces or perimeter desert sands adjacent 
to foundations. Rare wetting events or controlled recycled-water activation trigger bacterial 
germination, biofilm formation, and ceramic precipitation within near-surface branched 
defects. 
Measurable success metrics 
 
Crack sealing efficiency: percentage reduction in connected crack volume.  
 
Air/water permeability reduction: before/after permeability of treated zones.  
 
Surface erosion resistance: reduction in wind-driven particle loss.  
 
Unconfined compressive or penetrometer gain: near-surface strength increase in 
treated sand.  
 
Mineral deposition rate: mass or volume of CaCO₃ formed per activation cycle.  
 
Durability after dry/wet cycles: retained sealing after repeated desert weathering.  
Main failure modes and mitigation strategies 
 
Failure: sealing limited to the surface crust. 
Mitigation: low-viscosity infiltrant and repeated short wetting pulses.  
 
Failure: bacterial death from desiccation/UV. 
Mitigation: spore formulation and shaded/subsurface targeting.


## Page 179


 
Failure: salt efflorescence or pore clogging too early. 
Mitigation: staged ion dosing and lower initial calcium loading.  
 
Failure: ammonia-related by-products from ureolysis. 
Mitigation: controlled dosage, drainage management, and pilot-scale environmental 
monitoring.  
 
6) Production instantiation 
Step-by-step production process 
1. Material preparation 
 
Prepare a dry mineralizing formulation containing urea, calcium salt, buffering salts, 
and optional micronized limestone seed crystals.  
 
Adjust particle size so the powder can enter fine cracks and coat sand grains.  
 
Keep no permanent synthetic binder in the final formulation to preserve BIO.  
2. Biological component preparation 
 
Produce a spore-rich culture of Sporosarcina pasteurii.  
 
Wash and concentrate spores.  
 
Blend spores with the dry mineralizing salts under low-moisture conditions.  
3. Integration strategy 
 
Apply as dry powder patch, low-viscosity aqueous infiltrant, or sprayable slurry for 
immediate infiltration.  
 
Upon wetting, spores germinate locally.  
 
Bacteria attach to mineral surfaces, form biofilm, and initiate biomineralization.  
 
Biofilm traps fines and anchors the growing mineral phase to the inorganic substrate.  
4. Post-processing and stabilization 
 
Use one or more brief wetting cycles with recycled water to trigger deposition.  
 
Allow passive drying to return the system to a dormant, stabilized state.  
 
Avoid sterilization after biological integration because viability is required.  
 
Store the pre-application product dry and cool for shelf stability.  
Manufacturability 
 
Spore production is scalable using conventional fermentation.  
 
Dry blending and powder packaging are construction-compatible.  
 
Field application is compatible with spray, brush, or crack injection methods.  
Scalability 
 
Suited to batch production in sacks/cartridges.  
 
Can be deployed over large areas using surface spray or perimeter trench treatment.  
 
Reactivation by natural or scheduled wetting reduces maintenance complexity.  
Quality control and reproducibility 
 
Spore count per gram.  
 
Urease activity assay after rehydration.  
 
Standardized CaCO₃ precipitation test.  
 
Crack penetration test in representative masonry coupons.  
 
Erosion resistance test in treated sand beds.  
Why this route is feasible and preferred 
It satisfies BFF+BMN directly: bacteria form biofilm on inorganic building/sand substrates 
while driving biomineralization. It also preserves BIO because the functional architecture 
emerges from cells, ECM, and mineral formed in situ rather than from a synthetic scaffold.

C. Integrated paragraph 
BAC–BIO–EUB–FR–LCE–SLB–BFF+BMN defines a bacterial living ceramic for defect 
sealing and ground stabilization in smart and living buildings. The recommended biological 
system is spore-forming Sporosarcina pasteurii, selected for high-yield calcite precipitation, 
dormancy, and reactivation after intermittent wetting. The material phase is a calcite-
dominant CaCO₃/ECM ceramic, optionally integrating native silica fines at the treated 
interface. The FR slot is instantiated as a multiscale branched network occupying cracks and 
near-surface sand pores. Deployment consists of applying a dry or infiltrating spore-
mineralization formulation to building or perimeter sand defects, where BFF+BMN is 
realized by substrate-adherent biofilm growth and in situ biomineralization, yielding 
permeability reduction, crack closure, and erosion-resistant biocementation.

Iteration 2 
0) Code integrity check 
Code: BAC–BIO–EUB–FR–LCE–SLB–BFF+BMN 
The 7-slot code is ontology-valid. The ontology explicitly defines BAC as bacterial ELMs, 
BIO as biological engineered living materials made only of cells and their extracellular 
matrices, EUB as eubacterial, FR as fractal ELMs, LCE as living ceramics, SLB as smart and 
living buildings, BFF as biofilm-based trapping/anchoring of inorganic particles or 
interfacing with inorganic materials, and BMN as biomineralization.  
Interpretive note on ambiguity: 
There is a real ontological tension between BIO and BFF. BIO excludes supporting synthetic 
biomaterials or synthetic structures, whereas BFF is described as usually involving biofilms 
trapping/anchoring inorganic particles or interfacing with inorganic materials, often leading 
mainly to HLMs. The most coherent reading here is therefore: the system remains bio-ELM 
because the dominant material phase is generated and organized by the bacterial 
biofilm/extracellular matrix itself, while inorganic precursors are transient feedstocks or 
environmentally available particulates incorporated into a biologically produced ceramic 
extracellular phase rather than a pre-fabricated scaffold.  
 
1) Slot-by-slot decoding 
Domain — BAC 
Meaning: Bacterial ELMs.



colonization, biofilm growth, and mineral deposition.  


be bacterial-process compatible.  
Super-kingdom — BIO 
Meaning: Biological engineered living materials made only of cells and their extracellular 
matrices, without supporting synthetic biomaterials or synthetic structures.  
Design constraints 
 
Biological constraints: cells and their secreted matrix are the primary constructive 
agents.  
 
Material constraints: no external permanent synthetic scaffold is allowed as the



colonization, biofilm formation, and extracellular mineral/matrix accumulation



Kingdom — EUB 
Meaning: Eubacterial ELMs.

 
Biological constraints: candidate organisms must be true bacteria; archaea, fungi, 
plants, and animal cells are excluded.  
 
Material constraints: extracellular products must be consistent with eubacterial cell



Design constraints 


2) Biological instantiation 
Candidate 1 — Sporosarcina pasteurii

Primary mineralizing chassis for ureolysis-driven calcium carbonate deposition; produces 
biofilm on mineral surfaces and promotes crack bridging and sand-grain cementation.

Viability and growth requirements 




 
Temperature: mesophilic; suitable for warm desert daytime operation if protected in



Moisture: tolerant of drying between wetting events; suited to pulse activation.

Mechanical stress tolerance: acceptable within protected crack and pore

Biocontainment strategy 
 
Dry spore formulation before deployment.  


Nutrient-limited field medium to confine growth to treated zones.

It is strongly aligned with the required combination of bacterial identity, biofilm-mediated 
inorganic interfacing, and ceramic mineral deposition. Its main strength is direct coherence

Candidate 2 — Bacillus subtilis (mineralizing/spore-forming strain) 
Functional role in the ELM: 
Biofilm-forming and spore-forming bacterial chassis with strong EPS production; supports



 
Nutrients: minimal media compatible; can function under nutrient pulse feeding.  
 
Temperature: mesophilic, broadly robust.  
 
pH: tolerates mildly alkaline environments.  
 
Moisture: excellent dormancy/reactivation via spores.  


 
Sporulation-based storage and transport.  
 
Nutrient gating for localized activation.

It offers stronger stress tolerance and better dry-state handling than many non-spore-formers,

Recommended candidate 
Recommended biological system: Sporosarcina pasteurii 
Justification against alternatives 
For this ontology-grounded concept, the dominant functional requirement is not generic 
survival but efficient ceramic-phase generation inside fractal crack/sand networks. S. 
pasteurii is therefore preferred because biomineralization is its primary engineering role, 
whereas B. subtilis is stronger as a robust biofilm/spore platform but weaker as a direct 
mineralizing chassis. 
Trade-offs 
 
S. pasteurii offers higher mineralization efficiency and faster crack/sand 
consolidation.  
 
B. subtilis offers better dry handling and broad biofilm robustness.  
 
The chosen balance favors repair rate and mineral yield over maximum 
environmental robustness.  
 
3) Matrix/material candidates


## Page 184


Because the code is BIO, the material system must be interpreted as a biologically produced 
extracellular ceramic phase, not an external scaffold. The candidates below are therefore 
extracellular mineral systems generated and organized by the bacteria within the substrate. 
Candidate 1 — Calcium carbonate bioceramic phase 
Exact composition: predominantly calcite with possible vaterite/aragonite transient fractions 
during early precipitation. 
Compatibility with the biological component 
Highly compatible with ureolytic mineralizing bacteria; the biofilm EPS provides nucleation 
sites and stabilizes early mineral clusters. 
Interface mechanisms 
 
EPS-mediated adhesion to crack walls and sand grains.  
 
Bacterial cell walls and EPS act as nucleation loci.  
 
Crystal growth bridges grain contacts and crack asperities.  
Why selected relative to alternatives 
This is the most coherent interpretation of LCE plus BMN for crack sealing and sand 
stabilization in a desert-building context. 
Candidate 2 — Calcium phosphate / apatite-like ceramic phase 
Exact composition: poorly crystalline calcium phosphate progressing toward hydroxyapatite-
like mineral. 
Compatibility with the biological component 
Bacterial biofilms can nucleate phosphate minerals, though the process is usually slower and 
more sensitive to local chemistry than carbonate-based mineralization. 
Interface mechanisms 
 
Ionic interaction between EPS functional groups and calcium/phosphate ions.  
 
Heterogeneous nucleation on bacterial envelopes and adsorbed organic matrix.  
Why selected relative to alternatives 
It remains compatible with LCE, but for desert construction sealing it is less directly practical 
than carbonate mineralization because precursor management is more demanding and field 
kinetics are generally slower. 
Recommended material system 
Recommended material: calcium carbonate bioceramic extracellular matrix 
Justification against alternatives 
It best matches the combined requirements of LCE, BFF, BMN, and the stated 
repair/grout/sand-stabilization scenario. It is also the clearest ceramic phase for bridging 
irregular crack networks and cementing loose grains under intermittent wetting. 
 
4) Geometry and structural instantiation 
Fractal physical architecture derived from FR 
The ELM is instantiated as a ramified mineralizing biofilm network occupying: 
1. microcracks and crack side branches in masonry or cementitious surfaces, and  
2. near-surface branched pore networks among sand grains around desert buildings.  
Quantitative geometry 
 
Primary crack width target: 50–800 µm  
 
Secondary side-branch width: 10–150 µm  
 
Biofilm coating thickness on walls/grains: 10–80 µm before full mineral 
densification  
 
Mineralized bridge thickness across narrow defects: 50–300 µm per wetting cycle 
cluster  
 
Sand grain size window: 100–500 µm  
 
Effective mineralized penetration depth from treated surface: 5–30 mm depending on 
capillary access  
How geometry supports function 
 
Cell viability and function: branching pore/crack networks create sheltered 
microhabitats that retain transient moisture and reduce UV exposure.


## Page 185


 
Mass transport and diffusion: capillary wetting distributes dissolved ions and 
nutrients through multiscale branches better than through dense bulk volumes.  
 
Mechanical performance: mineral deposition at constrictions, asperity contacts, and 
grain bridges creates a distributed ceramic locking network rather than a single brittle 
plug.  
Why this structure is optimal 
A planar biofilm would not stabilize loose sand depth-wise, and a regular 3D scaffold would 
violate the BIO interpretation and the substrate-led geometry. The fractal architecture is the 
most coherent because the living ceramic grows along the actual defect topology of cracks 
and branched pore networks, which is precisely the structural meaning of FR.  
 
5) Application instantiation 
Deployment scenario 
The ELM is deployed as a dry bacterial mineralizing repair formulation or wettable 
mineralizing slurry applied to: 
 
near-surface cracks in earthen, mortar, or cement-based desert building envelopes,  
 
perimeter soil/sand zones around foundations and walls exposed to aeolian erosion.  
After application, short wetting events activate the bacteria. The cells form a biofilm on 
mineral surfaces, trap fine inorganic particles already present in the defect environment, and 
precipitate a ceramic extracellular phase that seals crack branches and cements sand contacts. 
Measurable success metrics 
 
Crack sealing efficiency: percentage reduction in connected crack aperture or 
permeable crack length  
 
Mineral deposition rate: mg mineral per cm² or per cm³ treated zone  
 
Surface consolidation depth: mm of stabilized near-surface layer  
 
Compressive or scratch resistance increase: relative gain versus untreated substrate  
 
Wind erosion resistance: mass loss reduction under controlled airflow/sand abrasion  
 
Water permeability reduction: flux reduction across repaired crack network  
Main failure modes and mitigation strategies 
 
Failure mode: incomplete penetration into fine crack branches 
Mitigation: low-viscosity first wetting cycle and repeated pulse infiltration  
 
Failure mode: superficial crusting with poor depth consolidation 
Mitigation: staged precursor delivery with lower initial ion concentration  
 
Failure mode: loss of viability from extreme desiccation/UV 
Mitigation: spore-based formulation and application into sheltered crack/pore spaces  
 
Failure mode: brittle over-mineralization and local debonding 
Mitigation: gradual multi-cycle mineral growth rather than one-step precipitation  
 
6) Production instantiation 
Step-by-step production process 
1. Material preparation 
 
Prepare a dry mineralizing precursor pack containing urea, calcium salt, and a fine 
mineral fraction compatible with the target substrate.  
 
Keep the inorganic fraction as feedstock or nucleation aid, not as a permanent 
synthetic scaffold, to remain coherent with BIO.  
2. Biological component preparation 
 
Culture Sporosarcina pasteurii to high cell density.  
 
Induce or preserve a stable dormant/spore-rich formulation for dry storage.  
 
Blend cells with protective excipients compatible with later rehydration.  
3. Integration strategy 
 
Mix dormant cells with the dry precursor formulation to obtain a powder patch, 
injectable paste, or brushable slurry generated immediately before use.  
 
Apply directly into crack networks or onto sand perimeter zones.  
 
On wetting, the bacteria adhere to mineral surfaces, establish biofilm patches, 
trap/environmentally anchor fine inorganic particles, and initiate biomineralization.

4. Post-processing and stabilization 
 
Apply controlled wetting cycles over hours to days.  
 
Allow ambient curing and repeated mineral growth episodes.  
 
Avoid heat sterilization after inoculation; viable handling must be aseptic before 
formulation instead.  
5. Manufacturability 
 
Uses standard bacterial fermentation, concentration, drying, and powder blending.  
 
Compatible with field mixing and low-skill application.  
 
No complex hardware or scaffold fabrication is required.  
6. Scalability 
 
High scalability through batch fermentation and dry-pack distribution.  
 
Suitable for repair cartridges, sachets, or bulk stabilization treatment kits.  
7. Quality control and reproducibility 
 
Viable cell/spore count before packaging  
 
Urease activity assay  
 
Mineral precipitation test in standardized coupons  
 
Flowability / injectability of final formulation  
 
Crack sealing benchmark on representative substrates  
 
Erosion resistance test on treated sand specimens  
Why this production route is feasible and preferred 
It is the most coherent route for BFF+BMN because it couples surface colonization and 
particle/inorganic interfacing with in situ biomineralization under mild conditions. It also 
respects BIO better than any scaffold-based manufacturing method, since the final structural 
phase is generated in place by the bacteria and their extracellular matrix rather than by a 
preformed synthetic construct.

C. Integrated paragraph 
BAC–BIO–EUB–FR–LCE–SLB–BFF+BMN defines a bacterial bio-ELM instantiated as a 
living ceramic mineralizing biofilm for desert building repair and perimeter stabilization. The 
recommended biological system is Sporosarcina pasteurii, with Bacillus subtilis as a 
secondary option, because the primary function is biofilm-mediated inorganic interfacing 
followed by biomineralization. The material phase is a predominantly calcium carbonate 
extracellular ceramic, with calcium phosphate as an alternative. The FR slot is realized as a 
ramified network within branched cracks and near-surface sand pores. In SLB, deployment 
occurs as a repair grout or dry patch activated by intermittent wetting. BFF+BMN is 
implemented through field application of dormant cells plus mineral precursors, followed by 
in situ biofilm growth and cyclic ceramic deposition to seal defects and consolidate erodible 
ground.


## Page 188


Iteration 3 
0) Code integrity check 
Ontology validity: confirmed. 
The uploaded ontology explicitly contains BAC (Bacterial ELMs), BIO (biological 
engineered living materials), EUB (Eubacterial ELMs), FR (Fractal ELMs), LCE (Living 
ceramics), SLB (Smart and living buildings), BFF (Biofilm based trapping or anchoring of 
inorganic particles or materials and biofilms interfacing with inorganic materials), and BMN 
(Biomineralization). The ontology also states that application and production categories may 
be combined using “+”.  
Interpretive note on ambiguity. 
The only relevant tension is that BFF is described as a route that “usually” leads to living 
composites and is “mainly” associated with HLMs, whereas BIO excludes synthetic 
scaffolds. I therefore interpret BFF here in its narrow ontology-consistent sense: biofilm-
mediated anchoring/interfacing with native inorganic crack walls and sand grains, while 
BMN provides the ceramic extracellular phase. This remains consistent with BIO, because 
the system does not require a synthetic support.

2) Biological instantiation 
Candidate 1 — Sporosarcina pasteurii 
Functional role 
Primary mineralizing bacterium for urease-driven CaCO₃ precipitation, crack bridging, 
intergranular biocementation, and formation of a stiff ceramic extracellular phase. 
Viability and growth requirements 
Aerobic; best deployed as dormant spores or desiccation-tolerant resting cells; activated by 
intermittent moisture; requires soluble calcium plus a carbonate-generating route; performs 
well under mildly alkaline conditions; operationally compatible with episodic wetting and 
drying in sun-exposed mineral substrates.


## Page 190


Biocontainment strategy 
Use a non-GMO spore-based formulation with activation only after water and 
nutrient/mineral precursor delivery. Containment is primarily achieved by dormancy outside 
wetted zones and by strong dependence on localized mineralization chemistry. For 
engineered variants, auxotrophy could be added, but it is not required for a baseline field-
deployable concept. 
Why it is strong 
It gives the clearest path to a cell-centric living ceramic, because the mineral phase is not 
incidental: it is the dominant functional product. 
 
Candidate 2 — Bacillus subtilis mineralizing biofilm-forming strain 
Functional role 
Biofilm establishment, ECM secretion, surface adhesion on mineral substrates, and secondary 
support to mineral nucleation and defect sealing. 
Viability and growth requirements 
Aerobic surface-associated bacterium; highly compatible with sporulation, intermittent 
hydration, nutrient limitation, and temperature fluctuation typical of exposed building 
envelopes. Works best when brief moisture pulses are followed by drying periods that 
consolidate the mineralized layer. 
Biocontainment strategy 
Again, a spore-first deployment is preferred. Growth is spatially restricted by localized 
wetting and nutrient availability. Field deployment is simplest with a non-engineered strain; 
engineered kill-switches are possible but not necessary for the baseline concept. 
Why it is strong 
It is superior when the priority is robust biofilm architecture and adhesion on irregular 
mineral surfaces rather than maximum mineralization rate. 
 
Why these are preferred over plausible alternatives 
 
Cyanobacteria are less aligned with the stated desert repair scenario because they 
require persistent light/water management and do not match the spore-based 
intermittent-reactivation logic as directly.  
 
Fungal mineralizers fall outside BAC–EUB.  
 
Non-spore-forming bacteria are less robust for low-maintenance desert deployment 
with rare wetting events.  
 
Recommended candidate 
Recommended candidate: Sporosarcina pasteurii 
Justification 
For BAC–BIO–EUB–FR–LCE–SLB–BFF+BMN, the central requirement is not merely 
adhesion but generation of a mineral-dominant ceramic ECM under intermittent field 
activation. S. pasteurii is the more direct fit because its value proposition is exactly that: rapid 
extracellular CaCO₃ formation that converts irregular crack and sand networks into a stiff 
bioceramic continuum. 
Trade-offs 
 
Advantage over B. subtilis: stronger and faster mineralization, better fit to LCE.  
 
Disadvantage: weaker native biofilm engineering flexibility and more chemistry-
sensitive operation.  
 
When B. subtilis would be preferable: when persistent interfacial biofilm robustness 
matters more than peak mineral deposition rate.  
 
3) Matrix/material candidates 
Candidate 1 — Calcite-dominant living ceramic 
Exact composition 
Predominantly CaCO₃ (calcite) precipitated extracellularly, integrated with a thin bacterial 
ECM composed of polysaccharides, proteins, and cell-wall-derived organic nucleation sites.


## Page 191


Compatibility with the biological component 
Directly compatible with ureolytic or otherwise carbonate-generating eubacteria. The living 
cells create the local supersaturation and nucleation conditions that generate the ceramic 
phase. 
Interface mechanisms 
Mineral nucleation on cell walls, EPS, and adhered biofilm patches; growth of mineral 
bridges between opposing crack faces and between adjacent sand grains; progressive infilling 
of pore throats and microcracks. 
Why it is strong 
This is the cleanest interpretation of BIO + LCE: the ceramic matrix is biologically produced 
in situ and no synthetic scaffold is required. 
 
Candidate 2 — Calcite-rich biocemented mineral crust with trapped native silica-rich fines 
Exact composition 
Biologically precipitated CaCO₃ as the continuous ceramic phase, with native quartz/silica 
fines or sand grains from the treated substrate mechanically trapped and mineral-bridged by 
the biofilm-mineral network. 
Compatibility with the biological component 
Compatible with both candidate bacteria because the native mineral substrate supplies the 
inorganic interface required for BFF, while the bacteria provide the ceramic growth phase 
through BMN. 
Interface mechanisms 
Biofilm adhesion to silicate-rich grains and crack walls; capture of local fines; mineral 
necking and grain-to-grain cementation through extracellular CaCO₃ deposition. 
Why it is strong 
It better matches the sand-stabilization branch of the use case, because it converts the existing 
granular mineral substrate into the stabilized architecture instead of creating only a surface 
mineral skin. 
 
Recommended material system 
Recommended material system: 
A calcite-dominant living ceramic composed of bacterially precipitated CaCO₃ integrated 
with bacterial ECM, optionally incorporating native silica-rich fines from the treated 
substrate where sand stabilization is required. 
Why this is preferred 
 
It preserves BIO because no synthetic scaffold is introduced.  
 
It satisfies LCE because the dominant extracellular phase is ceramic.  
 
It operationalizes BFF through mineral-surface anchoring and BMN through 
extracellular mineral growth.  
 
It is more coherent for building cracks and desert sands than alternatives such as 
hydroxyapatite or silica-only routes, which are less direct for ambient field 
precipitation.  
 
4) Geometry and structural instantiation 
Phylum-driven architecture: FR = fractal ELM 
The physical architecture is a hierarchical branched mineralizing network that follows 
irregular crack paths and near-surface sand pore networks rather than a regular sheet or 
lattice. 
Quantitative structural definition 
 
Target crack width range: ~50–800 µm for effective repeated-cycle sealing.  
 
Active biofilm thickness on mineral surfaces: ~5–50 µm.  
 
Mineral bridge span per activation cycle: ~20–200 µm across pore throats or 
microcrack gaps.  
 
Near-surface treatment depth in sand or porous façade material: ~2–20 mm, 
depending on capillary penetration.


## Page 192


 
Hierarchy of occupied features: from ~10 µm wall asperities and pore throats to mm-
scale branched crack segments.  
How geometry supports cell viability and function 
 
The branched topology maximizes surface area for adhesion.  
 
Thin hydrated films along crack walls and grain contacts retain water longer than 
bulk-exposed surfaces.  
 
Local branching creates protected microniches where spores can reactivate during 
brief wetting events.  
How geometry supports mass transport and diffusion 
 
Capillary pathways in cracks and sand deliver water, calcium, and soluble precursors 
directly along the defect network.  
 
Thin interfacial colonization reduces long-distance diffusion barriers.  
 
Repeated wetting/drying cycles naturally distribute mineralization fronts through 
connected branches.  
How geometry supports mechanical performance 
 
Fractal propagation creates many short mineral bridges instead of one thick brittle 
plug.  
 
Distributed intergranular necking improves erosion resistance in sand.  
 
Branched ceramic infill reduces crack-tip stress concentration by blunting and 
bridging secondary crack branches.  
Why this structure is optimal 
A regular 2D film would seal only the surface. A conventional 3D scaffold would violate BIO 
if pre-fabricated synthetically and would not match the native defect geometry. The fractal 
architecture is optimal because it is the only geometry that naturally conforms to irregular 
crack trees and sand pore networks while remaining fully cell-grown in situ. 
 
5) Application instantiation 
Deployment scenario 
The ELM is deployed as a dry spore-mineral precursor powder, low-viscosity aqueous 
infiltrant, or brushable/slurry repair treatment onto: 
 
cracked earthen envelopes,  
 
porous mineral façades,  
 
mortar or render defects,  
 
and wind-erodible perimeter sands adjacent to desert buildings.  
After application, brief wetting activates spores/cells, promotes adhesion to mineral surfaces, 
and initiates biomineralization cycles that progressively seal cracks and cement near-surface 
grains. 
Measurable success metrics 
 
Crack sealing efficiency: closed crack area or sealed crack volume.  
 
Mineral deposition: CaCO₃ mass per treated area or treated mass.  
 
Mechanical improvement: increase in surface hardness, unconfined compressive 
strength, or shear strength of treated sand.  
 
Erosion resistance: reduction in wind-driven mass loss.  
 
Hydraulic performance: reduction in water permeability through treated cracks.  
 
Biological persistence: viable spore/cell count after repeated dry-wet cycles.  
Main failure modes 
1. Surface crusting too early, blocking deeper penetration.  
2. Salt accumulation or over-rapid precipitation, causing brittle superficial layers.  
3. Loss of viability under intense UV, heat, or prolonged desiccation.  
4. Incomplete crack bridging in cracks that are too wide for capillary retention.  
5. Chemistry imbalance, causing weak mineral yield.  
Mitigation strategies 
 
Use staged low-volume wetting cycles instead of one large application.  
 
Separate attachment phase and mineralization phase.  
 
Favor spore-based delivery over vegetative-cell delivery.


## Page 193


 
Limit treatment to crack widths and surface depths compatible with capillary 
infiltration.  
 
Use repeated moderate mineralization pulses rather than a single high-concentration 
pulse.  
 
6) Production instantiation 
Production logic: BFF + BMN 
The production route is an in situ mineralizing biofilm process: first the bacteria attach and 
establish an interfacial biofilm on native inorganic surfaces (BFF), then they generate the 
ceramic phase by extracellular biomineralization (BMN). 
Step-by-step production process 
1. Biological component preparation 
 
Grow the selected bacterium under conditions that maximize sporulation and later 
mineralization competence.  
 
Harvest, wash, and concentrate spores.  
 
Dry-stabilize into a powder or keep as a concentrated inoculum depending on field 
logistics.  
2. Mineralization chemistry preparation 
 
Prepare an aqueous precursor package supplying calcium ions, minimal nutrients, 
and the required carbonate-generation chemistry for the selected bacterium.  
 
Keep precursor concentration moderate to avoid premature surface plugging.  
3. Substrate preparation 
 
Remove loose debris from the crack mouth or sand surface.  
 
Pre-wet lightly to establish capillary access without waterlogging the substrate.  
4. Integration strategy: attachment / biofilm establishment 
 
Apply the spore suspension or inoculum by spray, brush, drip, or low-pressure 
injection.  
 
Allow a short adhesion phase so cells attach to crack walls, grains, and native fines.  
 
This step operationalizes BFF.  
5. Integration strategy: biomineralization phase 
 
Deliver mineralization solution in repeated pulses.  
 
The cells reactivate, form interfacial biofilm, and nucleate extracellular CaCO₃ on 
cell/EPS surfaces.  
 
Mineral necks and bridges grow progressively through the crack/sand network.  
 
This step operationalizes BMN.  
6. Post-processing and stabilization 
 
Allow controlled drying between cycles to consolidate the ceramic network.  
 
No terminal sterilization is performed after biological integration, because viability 
must remain.  
 
Shelf-stable products should therefore be stabilized before deployment, not after in 
situ growth.  
7. Quality control and reproducibility 
 
Biological QC: spore viability, reactivation efficiency, mineralization activity.  
 
Material QC: CaCO₃ yield, phase composition, adhesion to mineral substrate.  
 
Process QC: penetration depth, crack closure uniformity, treated-sand strength 
increase.  
 
Reproducibility: standardized precursor concentration, moisture pulse schedule, and 
application volume per treated area.  
Manufacturability and scalability 
This route is manufacturable because it relies on: 
 
standard bacterial fermentation and sporulation,  
 
dry-powder or liquid concentrate formulation,  
 
and repair-compatible field application methods rather than complex hardware.  
It is scalable because the living component is produced centrally, while the final ceramic 
structure is grown in place on the building defect network.


## Page 194


Why this production route is preferred 
It is the most coherent route for BAC–BIO–EUB–FR–LCE–SLB–BFF+BMN because it: 
 
avoids synthetic scaffolds,  
 
exploits the native inorganic substrate as the anchoring interface,  
 
generates the ceramic phase only where needed,  
 
and matches irregular branched defects better than any ex situ fabricated geometry.

BAC–BIO–EUB–FR–LCE–SLB–BFF+BMN defines a spore-forming eubacterial living 
ceramic for mineral repair and sand stabilization in desert buildings. The recommended 
biological system is Sporosarcina pasteurii, with Bacillus subtilis as a biofilm-centered

alternative. Its material phase is a calcite-dominant CaCO₃ extracellular ceramic integrated 
with bacterial ECM. Derived from FR, the construct develops as a branched multiscale 
network along crack walls and sand pore throats rather than as a regular film or scaffold. In 
SLB deployment, it is infiltrated into façade defects or perimeter sands and activated by brief 
wetting. Production follows BFF+BMN: interfacial biofilm anchoring on native mineral 
substrates followed by repeated extracellular biomineralization cycles to achieve crack 
closure and intergranular biocementation.

2) Biological instantiation 
Candidate A — Haloferax volcanii (nonpathogenic model haloarchaeon) 
 
Functional role (cell-centric):  
o 
Heterotrophic uptake and conversion of dissolved organics (greywater COD

o 
EPS/biofilm formation to create a stable living layer that captures 
particulates/microdroplets and enhances residence time at ceramic surfaces.  
 
Viability & growth requirements:  
o 
Salinity: Requires high salt (brine conditions; supports containment-by-
environment).  
o 
Temperature: Mesophilic–moderately thermotolerant growth window (aligns 
with “hot” service-core loops).  
o 
Oxygen: Typically aerobic respiration (design should provide oxygenation or

o 
pH: Near-neutral to mildly alkaline (compatible with detergent-influenced

o 
Shear tolerance: Biofilm state improves retention under flow; design uses 
protected pores to reduce detachment.  
 
Biocontainment strategy:  
o 
Primary: Sealed cartridge + downstream microfiltration membrane barrier 
(physical retention).  
o 
Secondary (environmental): Dependence on high salinity—cells lose 
viability outside brine conditions (practical containment by physiology).  
o 
Operational: Periodic thermal/UV sanitation of the housing during cartridge 
replacement (cells remain internal; spent cartridges treated as bioactive 
waste).  
Why preferred vs alternatives: strong lab tractability, robust growth at high ionic strength, 
good candidate for stable colonization of inorganic surfaces in brines.

Cylindrical or prismatic stack of 3D ceramic modules: e.g., Ø 50–70 mm × 150–250

Honeycomb channels provide predictable hydraulics + cleanability, while micro-
porosity supplies protected ecological niches for archaeal colonization and stable

How geometry supports key functions 
 
Cell viability/function: protected pores reduce washout; high surface area increases 
contact time for uptake of dissolved organics.  
 
Mass transport: convection in channels + diffusion into pores; oxygen delivery can be

upstream aeration (microbubble injection), or

Mechanical performance: monolith resists compression; ceramic stability supports 
hot brine and periodic thermal sanitation of the housing.  
 
5) Application instantiation 
Application = SLB+BRE: building-service subsystem + bioremediation (waste processing).  
Deployment scenario (smart/living building greywater “polishing” cartridge) 
 
Where/how used: installed in a desert-building service core as a swap-in cartridge 
after coarse filtration (hair/lint) and before storage/reuse loop.  
 
Influent envelope (operational target):  
o 
Hot-to-warm greywater with elevated salinity/brackish concentration 
(service-core concentrated streams).  
o 
Controlled brine buffering in a recirculation tank to maintain archaeal 
viability.  
Measurable success metrics (operational + cell-centric) 
 
Organic polishing: reduction in soluble organics (COD proxy) across cartridge at 
defined flow and residence time.  
 
Stability: sustained performance over a target service interval (e.g., weeks) without 
catastrophic washout.  
 
Hydraulics: pressure drop maintained below a set threshold (clogging indicator).  
 
Biological retention: negligible archaeal cell escape downstream (verified by 
filtration integrity/biomonitoring).  
Main failure modes and mitigations


## Page 200


1. Fouling/clogging (biofilm overgrowth + particulates):  
o 
Mitigate via graded porosity, upstream coarse filtration, scheduled backflush, 
and modular replacement.  
2. Salinity drift (dilution) → archaeal loss of viability:  
o 
Mitigate via brine-buffer reservoir and conductivity control.  
3. Biofilm sloughing under flow transients:  
o 
Mitigate with protected pore habitats + controlled ramp-up of flow after 
maintenance.  
4. Cross-contamination by non-halophiles (biofouling community shift):  
o 
Mitigate using high-salinity operating regime (selective pressure) and 
periodic sanitation during cartridge swap.  
 
6) Production instantiation 
Production = CMM: composite-manufacturing-style scaffold fabrication + cell colonization 
step.  
Step-by-step workflow (implementable) 
1. Define ceramic scaffold spec (QC-first)  
o 
Target porosity %, pore-size distribution, compressive strength, permeability, 
and channel geometry.  
2. Composite-style scaffold fabrication (CMM-aligned)  
o 
Create a porous/formable preform (e.g., sacrificial template or ceramic fiber 
architecture) to enable woven/porous/graded features.  
o 
Infiltrate with ceramic slurry (Al₂O₃ or SiC precursor system) using vacuum-
assisted impregnation to achieve controlled strut thickness and gradients.  
o 
Cure / set green body; then debinding/burnout of organics and sintering to 
obtain a ceramic-dominant structure (keeps class = LCE).  
3. Post-fabrication conditioning  
o 
High-salinity wash to remove residual fines.  
o 
Surface conditioning to improve wettability and initial attachment (e.g., 
controlled oxidation for SiC or hydroxylated oxide surface for alumina) 
without adding non-ceramic bulk phases.  
4. Biological component preparation (archaea)  
o 
Expand Haloferax volcanii culture in high-salt medium to a defined cell 
density.  
o 
Prepare inoculum with controlled EPS induction conditions (biofilm-
favoring).  
5. Integration strategy: perfusion colonization  
o 
Place ceramic scaffold in sterile housing.  
o 
Recirculate inoculum through the module at low shear for 12–48 h to 
establish attachment inside pores/channels.  
o 
Switch to conditioned greywater feed gradually (stepwise load increase) to 
stabilize metabolism and prevent shock washout.  
6. Stabilization & packaging  
o 
Seal cartridge; integrate a downstream physical retention barrier (microfilter) 
in the housing design (system-level containment).  
o 
Define storage mode:  
 
“Wet-brine standby” (maintain viability), or  
 
controlled dry storage is not guaranteed for viability (if needed: Not 
present in the ontology/graph regarding preservation protocols).  
7. Manufacturability / scalability / QC  
o 
Manufacturability: batch ceramic scaffold production with repeatable 
molding/infiltration.  
o 
Scalability: parallel colonization stations (standard bioreactor/perfusion rigs). 
o 
QC tests (minimum):

 
Porosity/permeability (flow test), mechanical integrity 
(compression), salt/thermal cycling durability.  
 
Biological loading (biomass proxy), retention (effluent monitoring), 
and performance benchmark (COD drop in standardized test water).  
Why this is feasible & preferred (within ontology): 
 
It directly matches HYB (cells + scaffold) , enforces 3D colonization , keeps matrix 
class as LCE , and uses CMM as the manufacturing paradigm with post-fabrication 
colonization.

ARC–HYB–ARA–3D–LCE–SLB+BRE–CMM: Haloarchaeal biofilm–colonized porous 
ceramic (3D) cartridge for hot, saline building greywater polishing and bioremediation. The

biological system is a perfusion-established Haloferax volcanii biofilm (alternative 
Halobacterium salinarum) that retains viability in brine and removes soluble organics 
through archaeal uptake and metabolism while generating EPS for stable adhesion. The 
material system is a living-ceramic scaffold, preferably porous alumina or SiC. The 3D 
architecture combines millimeter-scale flow channels with 50–300 µm interconnected pores. 
Deployed as a sealed, swappable service-core module (SLB+BRE), it is manufactured via 
composite-style porous ceramic fabrication and post-fabrication cell colonization (CMM) to 
achieve sustained polishing performance with controlled hydraulics and containment.

2) Biological instantiation 
Candidate 1 — Haloferax volcanii (nonpathogenic model haloarchaeon) 
 
Functional role: aerobic heterotrophic DOC/COD polishing in saline greywater; 
stable biofilm formation within ceramic pores; nutrient assimilation into archaeal 
biomass (cell-centric “nutrient cycling” via uptake + biomass turnover).  
 
Viability / growth requirements (operational setpoints):  
o 
Salinity: ~1.7–2.5 M NaCl equivalent (high-salt dependence used as 
containment lever).  
o 
Temperature: ~30–45 °C (compatible with “hot” service-core fluids).  
o 
pH: near neutral to mildly alkaline (target 7–8.5).  
o 
Oxygen/nutrients: requires dissolved O₂ (passive aeration or microbubble 
injection); carbon from greywater organics; trace N/P.  
 
Biocontainment strategy:  
o 
Physical: sealed cartridge + downstream 0.2 µm-rated microfiltration at 
outlet; internal immobilization in tortuous porosity.  
o 
Ecological: obligate high-salt dependence (effluent dilution reduces 
survival), plus periodic high-salinity maintenance flush.  
Candidate 2 — Halobacterium salinarum (extreme halophilic archaeon; nonpathogenic lab 
model) 
 
Functional role: robust saline-tolerant biofilm communities for organic polishing 
under very high salinity; tolerance to desiccation/UV stress can support wet–dry duty 
cycles.  
 
Viability / growth requirements (operational setpoints):  
o 
Salinity: typically higher than Haloferax (very high NaCl brines).  
o 
Temperature: ~30–45 °C.  
o 
Oxygen/nutrients: generally aerobic heterotroph; requires organics + O₂.  
 
Biocontainment strategy:  
o 
Strong brine dependence + sealed cartridge + outlet microfiltration; thermal 
regeneration option (ceramic allows high-temp sanitation when cartridge is 
removed/offline).  
Why these are preferred over plausible alternatives 
 
Under the concept’s “hot + saline + low water activity” constraints, haloarchaea 
provide higher intrinsic tolerance than most conventional bacterial chassis (cell-
centric viability constraint under salinity/heat). This aligns with ARC/ARA and with 
the stated desert-building greywater conditions.


## Page 205


 
Within archaea, Haloferax volcanii is favored for controllable growth and biofilm 
formation under moderately extreme salinity, reducing the need for brine levels that 
could accelerate scaling/precipitation in plumbing.  
Recommended candidate: Haloferax volcanii 
 
Selection rationale: achieves the saline/heat robustness goal while enabling operation 
at moderately high salinity, easing integration into a greywater loop (less aggressive 
brine handling than extreme-halophile-only operation).  
 
Trade-offs: may be less tolerant than H. salinarum at the highest salinities; mitigated 
by maintaining a controlled brine window and using modular cartridge swap-outs.  
 
3) Matrix/material candidates (Class = LCE) 
Material candidate 1 — Porous alumina (α-Al₂O₃) ceramic foam/monolith 
 
Composition: α-Al₂O₃ (optionally with a thin silica washcoat to tune surface 
hydroxylation).  
 
Bio-compatibility mechanism: rough, hydrophilic oxide surfaces support archaeal 
biofilm anchoring via EPS adsorption + mechanical interlocking into micro-porosity.  
 
Interface mechanisms: (i) micro-roughness-driven adhesion; (ii) electrostatic 
interactions on hydroxylated oxide surfaces; (iii) stable wetting in saline.  
 
Justification: chemically inert, high compressive strength, tolerates heat sanitation 
and wet–dry cycling (strong fit to “hot desert service cartridge”).  
Material candidate 2 — Cordierite honeycomb ceramic (2MgO·2Al₂O₃·5SiO₂) 
 
Composition: cordierite silicate ceramic honeycomb (monolithic channel structure).  
 
Bio-compatibility mechanism: silicate surface chemistry + engineered wall micro-
porosity enabling protected colonization while maintaining low pressure drop.  
 
Interface mechanisms: silanol-rich surface regions + porous wall for internal 
attachment; optional thin ceramic washcoat for controlled roughness.  
 
Justification: widely used as durable monolith architecture; scalable modular 
fabrication with tight geometric tolerances.  
Preferred material: Porous alumina for maximum chemical/thermal robustness and high 
internal surface area for colonization, with cordierite as a lower-cost, high-throughput 
monolith alternative. 
 
4) Geometry and structural instantiation (Phylum = 3D) 
3D reactor cartridge architecture: a stacked porous-ceramic monolith cartridge (replaceable 
module) enabling flow-through polishing plus protected internal niches for archaeal biofilms. 
Quantitative geometry (implementable starting spec): 
 
Module form: cylindrical or prismatic monoliths, e.g., Ø 50–80 mm × 80–150 mm 
per module; 2–4 modules per cartridge.  
 
Flow channels (macro): parallel channels 0.8–1.5 mm hydraulic diameter to limit 
clogging.  
 
Wall micro-porosity: open porosity 35–55% with pore throat scale 10–80 µm for 
sheltered colonization (cells retained; convective refresh at pore entrances).  
 
Specific surface targeting: maximize internal surface while keeping pressure drop 
within building pump limits.  
How geometry supports function 
 
Cell viability: interior micro-porosity provides shear protection; macro-channels 
deliver oxygenated flow.  
 
Mass transport: convective flow in channels + diffusion into porous walls creates a 
stable nutrient/oxygen gradient sustaining biofilms.  
 
Mechanical performance: monolith architecture resists compression and handling 
during cartridge swaps; ceramics tolerate thermal cycling.  
Why 3D monolith over alternatives 
 
Compared to purely 2D coatings, the 3D monolith multiplies active area and provides 
protected microenvironments; compared to packed beds, it reduces channeling and 
simplifies containment/replaceability (SLB integration constraint).

5) Application instantiation (SLB + BRE) 
Deployment scenario (smart building + bioremediation): 
 
Installed as a service-core greywater polishing cartridge downstream of a coarse 
screen and upstream of reuse (e.g., flushing/utility water), tailored for hot, 
saline/brackish greywater in desert buildings.  
Measurable success metrics (examples suitable for verification): 
 
Organic polishing: % reduction in COD or TOC across cartridge (influent vs 
effluent).  
 
Solids/clarity: turbidity reduction and stable effluent clarity at target flow.  
 
Hydraulics: pressure drop maintained below an operational ceiling (cartridge health 
KPI).  
 
Biological stability: retained archaeal biomass within cartridge (no detectable cells in 
effluent beyond limit of detection after outlet filtration).  
Main failure modes + mitigations 
 
Biofouling / pore clogging: mitigate with macro-channel sizing, periodic backflush, 
and cartridge swap schedule.  
 
Salt crystallization during dry cycles: mitigate with controlled drain-down, brine 
maintenance window, and geometry avoiding dead zones.  
 
Loss of viability due to salinity drift: mitigate by inline conductivity control and 
scheduled saline conditioning pulses.  
 
Microbial contamination (non-archaeal growth): mitigate via salinity selection 
pressure + periodic thermal sanitation (offline) enabled by ceramic robustness.  
 
6) Production instantiation (CMM) 
Production route consistent with CMM (composite manufacturing + colonization): fabricate a 
porous ceramic composite monolith using composites-style preforms/infiltration and graded 
porosity control, then colonize with haloarchaea.  
Step-by-step workflow 
1. Preform preparation (composites-style): form a woven or felted ceramic-fiber 
preform (e.g., alumina fiber felt) into monolith geometry (outer shell + internal 
channel former).  
2. Ceramic slurry formulation: alumina (or cordierite precursor blend) + binder + 
sacrificial pore formers (controlled size distribution) to target dual-scale porosity.  
3. Infiltration/impregnation: vacuum-assisted impregnation of the preform with slurry 
(analogous to resin infusion in composites) to yield a porous/functionally graded 
green body.  
4. Curing + burnout: binder cure → controlled thermal burnout to remove organics/pore 
formers.  
5. Sintering: sinter to final ceramic composite scaffold (QC: porosity, pore throat 
distribution, compressive strength).  
6. Surface conditioning: aqueous rinse + mild surface activation (increase hydroxylated 
oxide sites); optional thin ceramic washcoat to tune roughness.  
7. Sterilization (scaffold-only): heat sterilize/autoclave compatible with ceramics.  
8. Biological preparation: culture Haloferax volcanii in defined high-salt medium to 
mid-log phase; prepare inoculum at controlled cell density.  
9. Colonization/seeding: recirculate inoculum through monolith under controlled 
salinity/temperature with aeration for 24–72 h to establish stable biofilm loading.  
10. Cartridge assembly + containment: integrate monolith modules into sealed cartridge 
housing; add outlet microfiltration barrier; leak/pressure test.  
11. Quality control (release tests):  
o 
Geometry/porosity & pressure-drop curve (per module lot).  
o 
Biofilm loading proxy (e.g., protein/DNA content per module) and effluent 
sterility downstream of filter.


## Page 207


o 
Functional bench test on synthetic saline greywater (COD/TOC reduction at 
specified flow).  
Manufacturability/scalability 
 
Composite-style preforms and infusion are batch-scalable; monolith modules can be 
standardized and swapped, aligning with SLB maintenance logic.

assembly with outlet microfiltration, targeting measurable organic-load reduction at bounded 
pressure drop with strict biocontainment.

 
Many non-halophilic archaea would be less compatible with detergent-rich, saline 
building streams (higher risk of washout or loss of activity).  
Recommended candidate 
Recommended: Haloferax volcanii 
 
Rationale vs H. salinarum: tends to be a more tractable, lab-robust haloarchaeal 
chassis for controlled colonization and stable operation; it supports a practical 
development pathway for repeatable cartridge seeding and performance tuning.  
 
Trade-offs: may be less tolerant than H. salinarum to extreme salinity spikes; 
mitigate with controlled salinity window in the building greywater loop.  
 
3) Matrix/material candidates 
Material A — Porous α-alumina (Al₂O₃) honeycomb/foam (ceramic) 
 
Compatibility with biology: chemically stable in hot brines; supports surface 
attachment and biofilm anchoring via microscale roughness and hydroxylated 
ceramic surfaces.  
 
Interface mechanisms: archaeal adhesion/biofilm retention via surface roughness + 
ionic interactions; internal porosity provides protected niches against shear.  
 
Why strong for LCE: alumina is a canonical oxide ceramic, matching “living 
ceramics.”  
Material B — Open-cell silicon carbide (SiC) foam (ceramic) 
 
Compatibility with biology: exceptional thermal and abrasion robustness; chemically 
resistant in challenging aqueous environments.  
 
Interface mechanisms: high-surface-area foam struts promote attachment; open-cell 
network reduces clogging risk at a given porosity.  
 
Why strong for LCE: SiC is explicitly within ceramic families (carbides) described 
under LCE.  
Material selection justification (relative): 
 
Al₂O₃ is simpler, widely manufacturable, and cost-efficient for modular building 
cartridges.  
 
SiC is premium-grade for harsh duty cycles (abrasive particulates, higher 
temperatures), at higher cost.  
 
4) Geometry and structural instantiation 
3D architecture (Phylum: 3D): a flow-through ceramic reactor cartridge with multi-scale 
porosity. 
 
Macro-transport geometry (primary flow):  
o 
Extruded/formed honeycomb channels: 0.8–2.0 mm hydraulic diameter (flow 
distribution + low pressure drop).  
o 
Channel wall thickness: 200–400 µm (structural stiffness while preserving 
area).  
 
Colonization geometry (biological niches):  
o 
Interconnected macropores: 50–300 µm (biofilm residence + protected 
microenvironments).  
o 
Target open porosity: 60–80% (balance between surface area and clogging 
tolerance).  
 
How geometry supports function  
o 
Viability: convective delivery in channels + diffusive penetration into 
macropores maintains nutrient/oxygen access to cells while reducing shear at 
the attachment sites.  
o 
Mass transport: short diffusion lengths from channel lumen to pore network; 
enables consistent substrate exposure and waste removal.


## Page 212


o 
Mechanical performance: monolithic ceramic geometry withstands handling 
and cyclic wet–dry operation as a cartridge.  
Why this geometry vs other plausible ones 
 
Compared to 2D biofilms on plates, a 3D porous monolith provides orders-of-
magnitude higher retained biomass per footprint and better integration as a swappable 
building-service module (matches 3D + SLB).  
 
5) Application instantiation 
Deployment scenario (SLB+BRE): 
A replaceable “service-core” cartridge installed in a smart building greywater loop handling 
hot, saline/brackish greywater (e.g., from showers/laundry in desert buildings using saline 
source water). The unit sits after coarse screening and upstream of storage/disinfection. 
Measurable success metrics 
 
COD reduction: % COD decrease per pass and per day (influent vs effluent).  
 
Turbidity / suspended microdroplet capture: NTU reduction (biofilm-assisted capture 
in pores).  
 
Stability: time-to-performance decay (days/weeks) under steady salinity and 
temperature.  
 
Hydraulics: pressure drop at nominal flow; fouling rate (ΔP/day).  
 
Biocontainment: viable cell count in effluent below a defined threshold.  
Main failure modes + mitigations 
 
Salt dilution event → archaeal loss: mitigate with salinity-buffered equalization tank 
and conductivity monitoring (smart-building control).  
 
Biofouling/clogging: mitigate with graded porosity (larger upstream pores), periodic 
brine backflush, replaceable prefilter.  
 
Detergent/toxic shock: mitigate with upstream dilution/equalization and adsorption 
guard bed if needed.  
 
Mineral scaling (hot brines): mitigate with antiscalant dosing upstream or periodic 
acidified brine cleaning (performed offline, cartridge removed).  
 
6) Production instantiation 
Production route (CMM): composite-style porous/graded scaffold fabrication + post-
colonization  
Step-by-step workflow 
1. Design & tooling  
o 
Define cartridge envelope (e.g., 100–150 mm length) with honeycomb 
channel layout and target pore gradients.  
2. Composite-style ceramic preform formation (CMM-consistent)  
o 
Create a ceramic fiber preform (e.g., alumina fiber felt / SiC fiber network) 
using textile/composite handling (layup/needling/braiding) to establish a 
porous, shape-stable skeleton and enable functional grading.  
3. Ceramic slurry infiltration  
o 
Vacuum-infiltrate the preform with an Al₂O₃ (or SiC-compatible) ceramic 
slurry containing fugitive porogens (polymer beads) to set macropore size 
targets.  
4. Debinding + partial sintering  
o 
Thermal debind to remove organics and porogens; partial sinter to lock in 
interconnected porosity while maintaining high open volume.  
5. Final ceramic stabilization  
o 
Final heat treatment to reach mechanical integrity for handling and flow, 
while preserving the designed porosity window.  
6. Pre-use conditioning  
o 
Rinse with sterile high-salinity brine to equilibrate surface chemistry and 
remove fines.  
7. Sterilization (pre-seeding)


## Page 213


o 
Apply sterilization compatible with ceramics (e.g., dry heat or steam) before 
biological loading.  
8. Biological preparation  
o 
Grow the selected haloarchaeon (recommended Haloferax volcanii) in 
controlled high-salt medium to a defined cell density; prepare an inoculum 
for attachment.  
9. Colonization / maturation (HYB requirement)  
o 
Seed the sterile cartridge in a recirculating bench bioreactor: controlled flow, 
salinity, temperature, and oxygenation to drive biofilm establishment inside 
pores.  
10. Packaging for deployment  
 
Seal cartridge in a housing that maintains brine wetness and includes a downstream 
microfiltration screen; label batch metadata.  
Manufacturability, scalability, QC 
 
Manufacturability: CMM-style preforms + infiltration scale from lab to pilot 
(repeatable porosity and geometry).  
 
Scalability: parallel production of identical cartridges; standardized seeding rigs.  
 
Quality control  
o 
Scaffold QC: pore-size distribution (micro-CT/mercury porosimetry), open 
porosity, compressive strength, pressure-drop curve.  
o 
Biological QC: attached biomass proxy (ATP/protein), effluent viable cell 
counts, baseline COD-removal rate in a standard test brine.

C. Integrated paragraph 
ARC–HYB–ARA–3D–LCE–SLB+BRE–CMM: a haloarchaeal biofilm–colonized 3D living-
ceramic cartridge for smart-building bioremediation of hot saline greywater. The biological 
system uses Haloferax volcanii (alt. Halobacterium salinarum) retained as a pore-anchored 
biofilm to drive cell-centric uptake and metabolism of soluble organics, reducing COD while 
stabilizing biomass against washout. A porous α-Al₂O₃ (or SiC) ceramic scaffold provides a 
3D flow-through honeycomb with interconnected macropores for protected colonization and 
convective–diffusive mass transport. Production follows composite-style ceramic 
preform/infiltration and post-fabrication archaeal colonization, yielding a swappable, 
contained reactor module with defined COD-removal and hydraulic performance targets.

1) Slot-by-slot decoding 
Slot 1 — Domain: CRD 
 
Meaning: Cross-domain ELMs (cells from different cellular domains).  
 
Design constraints  
o 
Biological: at least two domains → co-culture compatibility, differential 
stress tolerance, interspecies antagonism control.  
o 
Material: must provide segregated micro-niches (e.g., pore gradients) to 
buffer domain-specific requirements.  
o 
Structural/geometric: needs multi-compartment micro-architecture to prevent 
competitive exclusion.  
o 
Process: staged inoculation likely (one domain establishes first, then the 
other).  
Slot 2 — Super-kingdom: HYB 
 
Meaning: Hybrid living materials (cells + abiotic synthetic biomaterial/structure 
“scaffold”).  
 
Design constraints  
o 
Biological: survival under scaffold chemistry (pH/ions), adhesion to abiotic 
interfaces, dormancy strategy.  
o 
Material: scaffold is mandatory; must support colonization and mass 
transport.  
o 
Structural: must include connected porosity or interfacial area for cell 
retention.  
o 
Process: scaffold fabrication and cell colonization are separable steps (fits 
“colonization” framing).  
Slot 3 — Kingdom: CRK 
 
Meaning: Cross-kingdom ELMs (cells from different kingdoms).  
 
Design constraints  
o 
Biological: at least two kingdoms → functional division of labor; manage 
metabolite exchange and competition.  
o 
Material: interfaces must enable both cell wall types (e.g., bacterial adhesion 
+ fungal hyphal anchoring).  
o 
Structural: benefit from both microcolonies (bacteria) and filament networks 
(fungi).  
Slot 4 — Phylum: CP 
 
Meaning: Cross-phylum ELMs (structures combining 0D/1D/2D/3D/4D/fractal; 
multi-scale not necessarily fractal).  
 
Design constraints  
o 
Structural/geometric: multi-scale architecture required (e.g., 0D capsules + 
3D pores + 2D coating).  
o 
Mass transport: must maintain water/oxygen access while limiting wash-out.  
o 
Mechanical: must handle cracking/abrasion via hierarchical reinforcement.  
Slot 5 — Class: LCO
