 
Meaning: Living composites (ECM/matrix predominantly composite; e.g., polymer-, 
ceramic-, metal-matrix composites).  
 
Design constraints  
o 
Material: composite matrix must dominate (binder + fillers/fibers); living 
fraction is embedded/colonizing.  
o 
Interface: must promote mineral nucleation + polymer/EPS bonding.  
o 
Mechanical: composite must resist erosion/sand abrasion while permitting

Slot 6 — Application: SLB 
 
Meaning: Smart and living buildings (ELMs as subsystems for improved building

o 
Deployment: façade/exterior layer integration, maintainability, serviceability. 
o 
Performance: dust suppression + surface stabilization + self-healing

 
Meaning: CMM: composite manufacturing methods + cell colonization; BMN:

o

o 
QC: must quantify composite properties and biomineral output (mass gain,

2) Biological instantiation 
Candidate set A (mineralizer + adhesive/bridging fungus) 
1. Sporosarcina pasteurii (bacterium; urease-driven CaCO₃ precipitation)  
 
Functional role: biomineralization to seal microcracks and cement near-surface sand 
grains (BMN).  
 
Viability needs: intermittent moisture; carbon/nitrogen source; Ca²⁺ availability; 
tolerates moderate temperature swings; benefits from protected pores.  
 
Biocontainment: encapsulate as spores or dried cells in mineral-compatible 
microcapsules; nutrient-limited activation only during service misting; physical 
confinement within the render porosity.  
2. Aureobasidium pullulans (fungus/yeast-like; strong adhesion/EPS production)  
 
Functional role: surface adhesion and extracellular polymer (e.g., pullulan-like EPS) 
for dust binding; hyphae/filaments can bridge microcracks and anchor into pores.  
 
Viability needs: episodic moisture; low nutrient requirement; tolerates desiccation; 
benefits from shaded/porous microhabitats.  
 
Biocontainment: immobilization in the pore network; low free-water availability 
limits dispersion; periodic “on-demand” feeding (minimal) via façade service cycles.  
Why preferred (cell-centric): clear division of labor aligned to the concept brief: bacteria 
provide mineral (stiff crack sealing + sand cementation) while fungus provides 
binding/bridging (EPS + filament reinforcement) without requiring speculative genetic 
engineering. 
 
Candidate set B (spore-former mineralizer + robust filament former) 
1. Lysinibacillus sphaericus (spore-forming bacterium; mineral precipitation capability 
in alkaline niches)  
 
Functional role: mineral precipitation for cohesion and crack filling; sporulation 
supports desert cycling.  
 
Viability needs: moisture pulses; protected pores; alkaline-tolerant 
microenvironment.


## Page 217


 
Biocontainment: spores in microcapsules; activation only with dosed nutrient 
solution.  
2. Trichoderma harzianum (filamentous fungus; strong colonization and hyphal growth) 
 
Functional role: hyphal crack spanning; matrix reinforcement; surface bio-binding 
via secreted polymers/proteins.  
 
Viability needs: humidity pulses; protected porosity; moderate temperature.  
 
Biocontainment: physical immobilization within near-surface porosity; nutrient 
limitation.  
Trade-offs vs A: potentially stronger filament reinforcement, but higher risk of 
overgrowth/bioload if nutrients are not tightly controlled. 
 
Recommended candidate 
Recommended: Sporosarcina pasteurii + Aureobasidium pullulans 
 
Why: best-aligned to BMN-driven self-healing + EPS-driven dust suppression with 
controllable, moisture-triggered activation (spores + desiccation tolerance).  
 
Key trade-off: requires careful control of the “mineralization feed” (Ca²⁺/substrate 
dosing) to avoid surface efflorescence and uneven deposition.  
 
3) Matrix/material candidates 
Material candidate 1 — Polymer-modified mineral render composite (LCO-compatible) 
 
Composition: lime-based mineral binder (Ca(OH)₂ / CaCO₃ system) + silica 
sand/mineral fillers + small fraction of waterborne acrylic/latex polymer modifier + 
short microfibers (cellulose or glass).  
 
Bio-compatibility: porous mineral phase provides protected niches; polymer fraction 
improves adhesion and reduces dusting; compatible with fungal EPS anchoring.  
 
Interface mechanisms: CaCO₃ fillers serve as nucleation sites for bacterial mineral 
deposition; fungal EPS provides additional particle binding and interfacial “glue” to 
polymer-modified domains.  
Material candidate 2 — Magnesium phosphate cement (MPC)–polymer composite 
 
Composition: MgO–KH₂PO₄ cementitious binder + silica/mineral fillers + polymer 
modifier + microfibers.  
 
Bio-compatibility: faster set and potentially milder local chemistry than highly 
alkaline Portland-type systems; supports post-curing colonization.  
 
Interface mechanisms: mineral binder provides rigid scaffold; polymer + EPS 
improves abrasion resistance and dust capture; biomineralization adds secondary 
CaCO₃ sealing in cracks/pores.  
Preferred material: Candidate 1 (polymer-modified lime render composite) because it 
naturally supports CaCO₃-based biomineral sealing while remaining a clear living composite 
(binder + fillers + polymer) consistent with LCO and staged colonization consistent with 
HYB/CMM.  
 
4) Geometry and structural instantiation 
CP → multi-scale (cross-phylum) render architecture  
Quantitative geometry (serviceable façade “living render”) 
 
2D layer (sheet-like): 2–5 mm thick exterior render coat over 
masonry/concrete/adobe.  
 
3D porous scaffold: interconnected porosity ~10–25 vol% with characteristic pore 
throats ~20–200 µm (micro-habitat for bacteria/fungal fragments; diffusion paths).  
 
0D features: mineral-compatible microcapsules (50–300 µm) preloaded with 
bacterial spores and minimal nutrients (activated only when wetted).  
 
1D reinforcement: dispersed microfibers (length 1–5 mm; diameter 10–30 µm) + in 
situ fungal hyphal filaments to span microcracks.  
 
Surface microtexture: roughness scale 50–500 µm to trap dust/sand fines and retain 
transient moisture films.  
Why this geometry is optimal (cell-centric + performance)


## Page 218


 
Cell viability: pores and capsules buffer desiccation and UV exposure; staged wetting 
activates metabolism only when needed.  
 
Mass transport: thin layer + connected porosity supports oxygen diffusion and 
episodic water transport while limiting washout.  
 
Mechanical: hierarchical reinforcement (fibers + hyphae + mineral infill) targets 
crack arrest and abrasion resistance better than a single-scale coating.  
 
5) Application instantiation 
Deployment scenario (SLB) 
A protected, serviceable exterior render on desert-building façades and sand-exposed 
perimeter surfaces, designed to (i) suppress dust release, (ii) stabilize near-surface sand 
grains, and (iii) self-heal microcracking driven by thermal cycling and abrasion.  
Measurable success metrics 
 
Dust suppression: % reduction in airborne particulate release under standardized air-
jet abrasion.  
 
Sand stabilization: increase in near-surface shear strength / erosion threshold (wind 
tunnel or impingement tests).  
 
Self-healing: crack closure fraction (%) for 50–300 µm cracks after N wetting cycles; 
recovery of flexural strength (%).  
 
Mineralization: CaCO₃ mass gain (mg/cm²) per activation cycle; pore-blocking 
bounded to preserve breathability.  
Main failure modes + mitigations 
 
Over-mineralization/efflorescence: mitigate via low-dose, pulsed feeding and limiting 
soluble salts.  
 
Desiccation kill-off: mitigate via spore-based storage + sheltered porosity + 
activation only during controlled misting.  
 
Bio-overgrowth/bioload: mitigate via nutrient limitation and short activation 
windows.  
 
UV/thermal degradation: mitigate with shallow “active zone” (top 1–2 mm) as 
sacrificial layer, replaceable in maintenance cycles.  
 
6) Production instantiation 
Production route consistent with CMM+BMN: composite manufacture + post-colonization + 
mineralization cycles.  
Step-by-step workflow 
1. Composite scaffold formulation (CMM)  
o 
Mix mineral binder + graded mineral fillers + polymer modifier.  
o 
Add microfibers and controlled porogen/air-entrainment to target 10–25% 
porosity.  
2. Render application  
o 
Apply 2–5 mm coat by trowel/spray onto primed façade substrate 
(serviceable outer skin design).  
3. Cure (non-living stage)  
o 
Standard curing to reach handling strength while keeping connected porosity 
open (avoid high-temperature curing).  
4. Staged colonization (CMM “cell colonization”)  
o 
Post-seed the cured porous layer by spraying a thin aqueous carrier 
containing:  
 
bacterial spores (mineralizer) + fungal inoculum,  
 
plus a minimal, quickly exhaustible carbon source.  
o 
Allow 12–48 h humid incubation (temporary cover film) to anchor cells into 
pores; then dry to dormancy.  
5. Biomineralization activation (BMN)  
o 
In controlled maintenance cycles, mist with a low-dose mineralization feed 
(Ca²⁺ source + compatible substrate) for 1–3 short wetting events, then dry.


## Page 219


o 
Repeat cycles only when crack/dust metrics indicate need (condition-based 
maintenance).  
Manufacturability, scalability, QC 
 
Manufacturability: standard façade render processing; biology added post-cure to 
avoid viability loss.  
 
Scalability: compatible with spray/trowel at building scale; inoculum delivery by 
maintenance systems.  
 
Quality control  
o 
Composite: porosity, adhesion strength, abrasion loss.  
o 
Biology: retained viable counts after drying; urease/mineralization activity 
proxy; fungal adhesion/coverage in the active zone.  
o 
Function: standardized crack-healing and erosion tests after defined 
activation cycles.

C. Integrated paragraph 
CRD–HYB–CRK–CP–LCO–SLB–CMM+BMN: a cross-domain, cross-kingdom bacterial–
fungal living composite façade render for smart-building envelopes that self-heals and 
stabilizes sand-exposed surfaces. The biological system pairs a biomineralizing bacterium 
(e.g., Sporosarcina pasteurii) with an adhesive, EPS-forming fungus (e.g., Aureobasidium 
pullulans) to couple CaCO₃ crack sealing with dust-binding and filament reinforcement. A CP 
multi-scale architecture combines a 2–5 mm coating, interconnected micro-porosity, and 
microcapsule/fiber features to retain cells and enable diffusion. Deployed as a serviceable 
outer skin, it is produced via composite scaffold fabrication and post-colonization, then 
activated by controlled biomineralization cycles to reduce erosion and close microcracks.

Biological: Must support multi-scale colonization (microcolonies + filament 
networks).  
o 
Material: Must be architected across scales (microcapsules + fibers + porous 
render).  
o 
Structural: Must explicitly combine at least two structural “phyla” (e.g., 2D 
render layer + 0D capsules + 1D filaments + 3D pore network).  
o 
Process: Composite manufacturing must preserve the designed multi-scale 
architecture (avoid capsule rupture, preserve porosity).  
Slot 5 — Class: LCO 
 
Meaning: Living composites = extracellular matrices made predominantly of 
composites (e.g., polymer-, ceramic-, metal-matrix composites).  
 
Design constraints  
o 
Biological: Cells must function within/against a composite matrix (interfaces, 
nucleation sites, anchoring).  
o 
Material: Must specify at least two phases (e.g., polymeric binder + mineral 
aggregate/filler; plus biogenic mineral).  
o 
Structural: Load transfer + erosion resistance should arise from the 
composite architecture, not a single phase.  
o 
Process: Composite mixing/layup/spray/trowel approaches are appropriate.  
Slot 6 — Application: SLB 
 
Meaning: Smart and living buildings = ELM subsystems used in buildings, typically 
for improved sensing/actuation and healthier functionality.  
 
Design constraints  
o 
Biological: Must remain safe and stable in a built-environment context; 
containment is central.  
o 
Material: Weathering, UV, sand abrasion, salt, thermal cycling constraints 
dominate.  
o 
Structural: Must be deployable as façade render / surface skin / serviceable 
layer.  
o 
Process: Must be manufacturable at building-material scale (kg–ton), and 
installable by common construction methods.  
Slot 7 — Production: CMM+BMN 
 
Meaning:  
o 
CMM: Composite manufacturing methods + cell colonization 
(woven/porous/graded scaffolds as synthetic scaffolds for cells).  
o 
BMN: Biomineralization = cells generate minerals; applicable across 
kingdoms; relevant to HLMs and bio-ELMs.  
 
Design constraints  
o 
Biological: Must include a mineral-forming cell function (bacterial or fungal 
biomineralization; here primarily bacterial).  
o 
Material: Must provide mineral precursors (e.g., Ca²⁺ source; carbonate 
generation pathway) and nucleation surfaces.  
o 
Structural: Must enable mineral deposition where it matters (crack faces; 
sand–binder interfaces).  
o 
Process: Must describe a feasible composite workflow + a post-installation 
activation route for biomineralization.  
 
2) Biological instantiation 
Note: The ontology/graph do not specify organism species; explicit organism selection is Not 
present in the ontology/graph. The candidates below are engineering instantiations consistent 
with CRD–HYB–CRK–CP–LCO–SLB–CMM+BMN. 
Candidate 1 (bacteria–fungus consortium) 
Bacterium: Sporosarcina pasteurii (spore-forming, ureolytic biomineralizer) 
Fungus: Aureobasidium pullulans (EPS/adhesion-focused fungus; biofilm-forming) 
 
Functional role


## Page 223


o 
S. pasteurii: moisture-triggered germination → ureolysis → carbonate 
availability → CaCO₃ precipitation at crack and sand-grain contacts (self-
healing + sand binding).  
o 
A. pullulans: adhesive extracellular polymers (EPS) + surface colonization 
→ particle binding, microcrack “bridging” by biomass/EPS, and improved 
retention of bacterial micro-niches.  
 
Viability / growth requirements  
o 
Aerobic; moisture pulses required (activation events).  
o 
Nutrients must be micro-dosed (embedded micro-reservoirs) to avoid 
uncontrolled proliferation.  
o 
Temperature: designed for exterior façade range (e.g., warm climates); 
desiccation tolerance handled via dormancy/encapsulation.  
 
Biocontainment strategy  
o 
Physical containment: microencapsulation of bacterial spores (and optionally 
fungal propagules) in semi-permeable polymer–silica capsules embedded in 
render.  
o 
Nutrient limitation: internal “single-shot” nutrient beads (finite urea + Ca-
salt) so activity self-terminates when depleted.  
o 
Spatial separation: bacteria primarily inside capsules; fungus preferentially in 
near-surface microvoids to reduce direct competition.  
Why preferred vs plausible alternatives 
 
Strong division of labor: bacterial mineralization + fungal adhesion/EPS, matching 
the concept’s “mineralize + reinforce” logic.  
 
Both can be handled as dormant/contained components compatible with building-
material deployment constraints.  
Candidate 2 (bacteria–fungus consortium) 
Bacterium: Bacillus subtilis (spore-forming; biofilm/EPS; mineralization-capable strains) 
Fungus: Trichoderma reesei (filamentous fungus; hyphal network reinforcement) 
 
Functional role  
o 
B. subtilis: spore-based persistence; biofilm/EPS to retain fines; contributes 
to mineral nucleation (secondary biomineralization support).  
o 
T. reesei: hyphal (1D) reinforcement network within microcracks and pores; 
improves toughness and crack-spanning capacity.  
 
Viability / growth requirements  
o 
Aerobic; moisture pulses; nutrients must be limited.  
o 
Filamentous growth requires connected microvoids and water activity above 
a threshold during activation windows.  
 
Biocontainment strategy  
o 
Encapsulation of spores; fungal growth constrained by restricted pore 
connectivity and nutrient access.  
Why preferred vs plausible alternatives 
 
More explicit hyphal reinforcement (mechanical crack-bridging) than yeast-like 
fungi.  
 
Trade-off: filamentous growth is harder to control; greater risk of visible growth if 
containment fails.  
Recommended candidate (selected) 
Recommended: Candidate 1 — Sporosarcina pasteurii + Aureobasidium pullulans 
 
Selection justification: Maximizes controllable BMN-driven self-healing (bacterial 
CaCO₃) while using fungal EPS primarily for adhesion/retention rather than 
aggressive hyphal expansion (lower “escape/visibility” risk in façades).  
 
Trade-offs: Less “fiber-like” reinforcement than a strongly filamentous mycelium; 
mitigation is provided structurally (microfibers + graded porosity) and materially 
(composite binder toughening).  
 
3) Matrix/material candidates


## Page 224


The ontology defines LCO (living composites) but does not prescribe specific binders; 
explicit compositions are Not present in the ontology/graph. The candidates below satisfy 
HYB + LCO + SLB and enable CMM+BMN. 
Material candidate A — Breathable polymer–mineral render composite 
 
Composition (explicit):  
o 
Water-based siloxane-modified acrylic binder (polymer matrix)  
o 
Graded silica sand (150–600 µm) + silica fume (microfiller) as mineral 
phases  
o 
Ca-source microreservoirs (e.g., Ca-lactate or Ca-acetate beads) for 
biomineralization feedstock  
o 
Polymer–silica microcapsules (50–300 µm) containing bacterial spores (and 
optionally fungal propagules)  
 
Bio-compatibility:  
o 
Polymer binder is comparatively mild vs high-alkaline cement; capsules 
shield cells from shear and desiccation.  
 
Interface mechanisms:  
o 
CaCO₃ precipitates preferentially on silica/filler surfaces (nucleation) and 
within crack voids.  
o 
Fungal EPS increases adhesion to sand grains and anchors to polymer matrix. 
Why select vs alternatives: Best fit when façade requirements prioritize dust suppression + 
abrasion resistance with controlled living activity in a thin layer. 
Material candidate B — Mineral-rich geopolymer–latex composite render 
 
Composition (explicit):  
o 
Metakaolin-based geopolymer (alkali-activated aluminosilicate) as primary 
mineral binder  
o 
Acrylic latex modifier (polymer phase) for toughness + crack resistance  
o 
Silica sand + fine aluminosilicate fillers  
o 
Encapsulated spores in alkali-resistant microcapsules (e.g., silica-shell 
capsules)  
 
Bio-compatibility:  
o 
Fresh geopolymer alkalinity is challenging; viability requires robust 
encapsulation and delayed activation (after cure/carbonation).  
 
Interface mechanisms:  
o 
Mineral matrix provides abundant nucleation surfaces; biomineralization 
preferentially seals microcracks and pores.  
Why select vs alternatives: Best fit when façade requirements prioritize mineral durability; 
comes with higher burden on encapsulation and activation control. 
Recommended material system: Candidate A (polymer–mineral breathable composite) for 
better compatibility with controlled biological activation in building skins. 
 
4) Geometry and structural instantiation 
Phylum = CP (Cross-phylum / multi-scale) requires a combined architecture across scales.  
Proposed CP architecture (quantified, multi-scale) 
1. 2D layer (render skin):  
o 
Thickness: 3–6 mm on exterior substrate (masonry/earthen façade).  
o 
Surface texture: micro-roughness 50–300 µm to retain fines and reduce wind 
shear slip.  
2. 3D porous micro-network (within the layer):  
o 
Target open porosity: 15–30 vol%  
o 
Interconnected pore throats: 20–150 µm (supports transient moisture 
percolation and gas diffusion).  
3. 0D living microreactors (capsules):  
o 
Capsule diameter: 50–300 µm (bacterial spores + finite nutrient/Ca source 
co-located).  
o 
Areal density: 10⁶–10⁷ capsules/m² (tunable by desired healing capacity).


## Page 225


4. 1D reinforcement (biological + abiotic):  
o 
Biological: fungal biomass/EPS in microvoids and crack faces (candidate-
dependent).  
o 
Abiotic: short mineral microfibers (e.g., basalt fibers) 3–6 mm length, 10–20 
µm diameter to provide crack-bridging that does not depend on uncontrolled 
fungal hyphae.  
How geometry supports function 
 
Cell viability: Capsules create protected microenvironments; pores enable O₂ 
diffusion; activation occurs during moisture pulses.  
 
Mass transport: Moisture ingress carries dissolved Ca²⁺/carbonate precursors to crack 
tips; pores avoid complete sealing before healing.  
 
Mechanical performance: Composite layer resists abrasion; fibers control shrinkage 
cracking; biomineralization densifies microcrack zones.  
Why this CP structure vs simpler geometries: A single-phase 2D coating lacks protected 
niches and healing reservoir; a purely 3D bulk stabilizer is unnecessary for façade skins. CP 
enables targeted, serviceable, thin-layer performance. 
 
5) Application instantiation 
Application = SLB (Smart and living buildings).  
Deployment scenario 
 
Where: Exterior façade zones exposed to wind-driven sand, dust, and thermal cycling 
(desert/peri-desert buildings).  
 
How used: A protected living render skin applied as a serviceable outer layer 
(replaceable “sacrificial” coat). Moisture events (rain, fog, scheduled misting) trigger 
episodic self-healing and sand binding.  
Measurable success metrics 
 
Erosion resistance: mass loss under standardized sand abrasion (g/m² per test 
interval).  
 
Dust suppression: PM10/PM2.5 emission reduction from surface under wind shear 
(µg/m³ near-surface plume).  
 
Self-healing efficiency: % crack closure for 50–500 µm cracks after N wetting 
cycles; regained surface tensile strength (pull-off adhesion).  
 
Biomineral output: CaCO₃ mass fraction or mineral deposition rate localized to crack 
region (mg/cm² per activation cycle).  
Failure modes + mitigations 
 
Desiccation kill-off: mitigate via spore-based biology + microcapsules + activation-
by-pulse.  
 
Uncontrolled growth/visibility: mitigate via nutrient limitation + physical 
containment + breathable topcoat.  
 
Salt crystallization/efflorescence: mitigate via controlled ion dosing, capillary-break 
design, and limiting water residence time.  
 
Loss of adhesion to substrate: mitigate via primer layer and graded modulus (softer 
outer skin, tougher inner interface).  
 
6) Production instantiation 
Production = CMM+BMN requires composite manufacturing + biomineralization.  
Step-by-step implementable workflow (CMM+BMN) 
1. Microcapsule fabrication (living fraction packaging)  
o 
Prepare dry bacterial spores (and optional fungal propagules) and blend with 
finite nutrient + Ca-source microbeads.  
o 
Encapsulate into polymer–silica microcapsules (50–300 µm) with semi-
permeable shell (water/ions pass; cells retained).  
2. Composite render base formulation (abiotic scaffold)  
o 
Mix siloxane-acrylic binder + graded silica sand + silica fume (target 
rheology for trowel/spray).


## Page 226


o 
Add short reinforcement fibers (basalt) for shrinkage/crack control.  
3. Integration (low-shear mixing)  
o 
Add capsules at the final mixing stage under low shear to reduce rupture.  
o 
Confirm capsule integrity by sieve/optical sampling (QC gate).  
4. Application to substrate  
o 
Apply primer (compatibility layer), then apply render 3–6 mm.  
o 
Tool surface texture to target roughness (dust retention + lower wind 
erosion).  
5. Cure + stabilization  
o 
Allow binder cure (polymer film formation) under ambient conditions.  
o 
Optional breathable protective overcoat with controlled permeability 
(reduces UV damage while preserving gas/moisture exchange).  
6. Activation protocol (BMN)  
o 
Commissioning activation via brief wetting (misting) to initiate localized 
biomineralization.  
o 
Re-activation occurs only during subsequent moisture events; nutrients are 
finite → activity tapers.  
Manufacturability / scalability 
 
Compatible with existing construction workflows: premixed bags (dry fillers + 
capsules) + on-site addition of binder/water.  
 
Capsule production is the main scale driver; can be industrialized as a discrete supply 
chain component.  
Quality control (reproducibility) 
 
Capsule size distribution + rupture rate (microscopy/sieving).  
 
Viability after mixing (CFU/spore germination checks from sacrificial samples).  
 
Standardized crack-healing test coupons (controlled crack width; wetting cycles; 
strength recovery).  
 
Mineral phase verification in healed zones (simple acid reaction screening + 
microscopy; advanced methods optional).  
Why this route is feasible/preferred: It directly matches CMM (composite formulation + 
graded microstructure) and realizes BMN through protected, moisture-triggered 
mineralization without requiring continuous cultivation infrastructure.

C. Integrated paragraph (~100 words) 
CRD–HYB–CRK–CP–LCO–SLB–CMM+BMN describes an encapsulated cross-domain, 
cross-kingdom hybrid living composite render for sand-exposed building skins, in which 
bacterial biomineralization and fungal adhesion jointly drive self-healing and dust 
suppression. A protected Sporosarcina pasteurii spore fraction mineralizes CaCO₃ within 
microcracks and sand-grain contacts, while Aureobasidium pullulans contributes EPS-
mediated anchoring and retention in near-surface microvoids. A CP multi-scale structure 
combines a 3–6 mm 2D render layer, a 3D porous micro-network, 0D living microcapsules, 
and 1D reinforcement fibers. Composite manufacturing plus biomineralization enables 
episodic, moisture-triggered stabilization and crack sealing in smart building façades.

Iteration 3 
0) Code integrity check 
 
Ontology validity: All 7 codes CRD–HYB–CRK–CP–LCO–SLB–CMM+BMN are 
present as defined ontology codifications for the 7-slot model (Domain, Super-
kingdom, Kingdom, Phylum, Class, Application, Production).  
 
Multi-label validity (Production): The ontology explicitly allows combining 
categories using “+”, so CMM+BMN is a valid production codification.  
 
Ambiguity handling (no code changes):  
o 
CRD (cross-domain) does not specify which domains; CRK (cross-kingdom) 
does not specify which kingdoms. The input concept explicitly targets a 
bacterial–fungal pairing, which is consistent with cross-domain (Bacteria + 
Eukarya) and cross-kingdom. (No ontology slots are modified.)  
 
1) Slot-by-slot decoding 
Slot 1 — Domain: CRD 
 
Meaning: Cross-domain ELMs (populated by different cellular domains).  
 
Design constraints  
o 
Biological: Must include cells from ≥2 domains (e.g., bacteria + eukaryotes).  
o 
Material: Microenvironments must support distinct cell physiologies (e.g., 
different osmotic/pH tolerances).  
o 
Structural/geometric: Architecture must provide segregated niches 
(microcompartments) to avoid competitive exclusion.  
o 
Process: Assembly/activation must allow staged introduction or differential 
protection of each domain.  
Slot 2 — Super-kingdom: HYB 
 
Meaning: Hybrid living materials (HLMs): cells + an abiotic synthetic 
biomaterial/structure (scaffold).  
 
Design constraints  
o 
Biological: Cells must tolerate contact with an abiotic scaffold and its 
curing/aging chemistry.  
o 
Material: Must explicitly include a non-living scaffold/matrix (render 
composite).  
o 
Structural/geometric: Must preserve mass transport (water/oxygen) through 
an abiotic phase.  
o 
Process: Requires a manufacturable route that integrates living components 
without lethal curing steps.  
Slot 3 — Kingdom: CRK 
 
Meaning: Cross-kingdom ELMs (combining cells from different kingdoms).  
 
Design constraints  
o 
Biological: Must include ≥2 kingdoms; for this concept: eubacteria + fungi is 
coherent.  
o 
Material: Interfaces must support both bacterial biofilms/microcolonies and 
fungal hyphae/EPS anchoring.  
o 
Structural/geometric: Must allow both microcolony-scale and filament-scale 
occupancy.  
Slot 4 — Phylum: CP 
 
Meaning: Cross-phylum ELMs: structures combining multiple phyla 
(0D/1D/2D/3D/4D/FR) and/or multi-scale non-fractal structures.  
 
Design constraints  
o 
Biological: Must host multiple structural modes (e.g., 0D encapsulated cells 
+ 1D fibers + 2D biofilm patches + 3D porosity).  
o 
Material: Requires graded or hierarchical porosity/roughness.  
o 
Structural/geometric: Multi-scale architecture is mandatory (not a single-
scale biofilm or a single 3D scaffold).


## Page 229


o 
Process: Manufacturing must reproducibly control multi-scale features.  
Slot 5 — Class: LCO 
 
Meaning: Living composites (extracellular matrices predominantly composites; e.g., 
polymer-, ceramic-, or metal-matrix composites).  
 
Design constraints  
o 
Biological: Cells must function in a composite microenvironment with 
fillers, fibers, and polymer modifiers.  
o 
Material: Must be explicitly a composite (not a pure polymer, ceramic, 
carbon, or alloy matrix).  
o 
Structural/geometric: Must include reinforcement/second phase 
(fibers/particles) supporting crack bridging and erosion resistance.  
o 
Process: Composite processing and quality control (dispersion, porosity, 
adhesion) are central.  
Slot 6 — Application: SLB 
 
Meaning: Smart and living buildings (ELMs as subsystems of buildings for improved 
sensing/actuation, healthier environments, etc.).  
 
Design constraints  
o 
Biological: Must survive building envelope stressors (desiccation cycles, UV, 
temperature swings).  
o 
Material: Must be compatible with façade substrates and durability 
expectations.  
o 
Structural/geometric: Must maintain surface integrity while enabling periodic 
biological activation.  
o 
Process: Must be serviceable (repair/refresh cycles) and safe for occupants.  
Slot 7 — Production: CMM+BMN 
 
Meaning:  
o 
CMM: Composite manufacturing methods and cell colonization (composites-
industry processes creating woven/porous/graded scaffolds for subsequent 
cell colonization).  
o 
BMN: Biomineralization (cells generate minerals for stiff mineralized 
tissues/protection/storage; applicable to HLMs and bio-ELMs).  
o 
“+” combination allowed.  
 
Design constraints  
o 
Biological: Requires a mineral-forming cellular role (biomineralizing 
bacteria) plus a co-functioning cross-kingdom partner (fungus).  
o 
Material: Composite must present nucleation sites and ion access for mineral 
precipitation.  
o 
Structural/geometric: Must allow mineral deposition specifically in 
cracks/pores without blocking all transport.  
o 
Process: Must include (i) composite fabrication + (ii) colonization/activation 
+ (iii) mineralization phase.  
 
2) Biological instantiation 
Candidate Set 1 (recommended) 
Bacterium: Sporosarcina pasteurii (spore-forming, mineralizing) 
Fungus: Aureobasidium pullulans (EPS/adhesion, biofilm-like growth) 
 
Functional role  
o 
S. pasteurii: localized mineral precipitation within microcracks/pores to 
restore stiffness and seal pathways (BMN).  
o 
A. pullulans: EPS-mediated dust binding + surface adhesion + 
hyphal/filamentous crack spanning to stabilize the repair zone (CRK 
synergy).  
 
Viability & growth requirements (engineering-relevant)  
o 
Moisture: episodic wetting triggers metabolic activity; dormancy between 
events (bacterial spores strongly support this).


## Page 230


o 
Oxygen: aerobic/oxygen-permeable near-surface niches; avoid fully 
waterlogged conditions.  
o 
pH/ions: requires protected microdomains if bulk render is alkaline; buffered 
microcapsules or locally conditioned pores.  
o 
Mechanical stress: tolerates micro-shear in pores; fungus benefits from 
protected roughness/anchoring sites.  
 
Biocontainment strategy  
o 
Spore-based dosing for bacteria; microencapsulation (e.g., hydrogel 
microbeads within the composite) to localize growth.  
o 
Nutrient limitation by design (only short activation solutions), and 
serviceable top-layer replacement as the primary containment layer.  
Why preferred over alternatives: robust dormancy/activation cycle aligns with façade wet–
dry cycles; clear division of labor (mineralize vs. bind/span) reduces cross-kingdom 
competition in limited nutrients. 
 
Candidate Set 2 (alternative) 
Bacterium: Lysinibacillus sphaericus (spore-forming; mineral-associated pathways used in

Fungus: Trichoderma harzianum (robust filamentous growth; strong surface colonizer) 
 
Functional role  
o 
L. sphaericus: mineral precipitation/ion capture in pores and crack tips 
(BMN).  
o 
T. harzianum: filamentous crack spanning + EPS/proteinaceous adhesion that 
increases dust binding and erosion resistance.  
 
Viability & growth requirements  
o 
Similar wet–dry activation concept; may require tighter nutrient control to 
prevent excessive biomass.  
 
Biocontainment strategy  
o 
Same as above: microencapsulation + nutrient-gated activation; optional 
post-event “dry-out” as a biological off-switch.  
Why not primary: filamentous fungi can become too aggressive in growth if nutrients are not 
tightly metered, increasing variability in façade aesthetics and porosity. 
 
Recommended biological system (final) 
Recommended: Sporosarcina pasteurii + Aureobasidium pullulans 
 
Trade-offs: Highest controllability via spore-driven activation and EPS-mediated 
stabilization; requires careful microenvironment design to avoid high-alkalinity 
lethality and to prevent pore clogging from over-mineralization.

Must satisfy HYB (abiotic scaffold present) and LCO (composite matrix dominant).

 
Compatibility with biology  
o 
Microcapsules create buffered, hydrated niches in an otherwise harsh mineral 
matrix.  
 
Interface mechanisms  
o 
Fungal EPS increases adhesion to mineral/polymer microtexture.  
o 
Bacterial mineral nucleation occurs preferentially at crack surfaces and filler

 
Composition (explicit): low-alkali cementitious binder (or blended cement) +

Compatibility with biology  
o 
Requires stronger buffering/encapsulation than Candidate 1 due to alkaline

Interface mechanisms  
o 
Porous fillers act as “cell refugia” (protected colonization pockets) while

4) Geometry and structural instantiation 
Must express CP (cross-phylum) as multi-structure, multi-scale architecture.  
Quantitative, CP-consistent architecture (final) 
 
2D layer (surface render): 2–5 mm thick façade coating (planar macroform).  
 
3D porous network: 10–25% interconnected porosity with throat sizes ~20–200 µm 
(supports water/oxygen pulses and microcolony residence).  
 
0D microreservoirs: microcapsules/microbeads 100–500 µm diameter containing 
spores + minimal nutrients/buffer (localized activation points).  
 
1D reinforcement: dispersed microfibers 3–12 mm length (crack deflection/bridging) 
to reduce crack opening and provide hyphal anchoring tracks.  
 
Crack-guided mineralization zones: engineered preferential crack paths (via filler 
grading) so BMN occurs where it restores sealing rather than clogging all pores.  
How geometry supports function 
 
Cell viability/function: protected 0D capsules + 3D pores limit desiccation severity 
and stabilize local chemistry; surface-access enables oxygen diffusion.  
 
Mass transport: interconnected pores allow brief wetting solution ingress and then 
rapid drying (biological “on/off” gating).  
 
Mechanical performance: fibers + mineral–polymer composite provide baseline 
toughness; BMN adds stiffness/closure at crack tips; fungal spanning stabilizes the 
repair scaffold during mineral precipitation.  
Why CP geometry is optimal here 
A single 2D biofilm would be erosion-prone; a purely 3D scaffold would be hard to service 
on façades. The CP multi-scale system enables serviceable 2D deployment with 3D 
functional microenvironments and 0D/1D reinforcement elements for repeatable crack 
healing. 
 
5) Application instantiation 
Must remain within SLB (smart and living buildings).  
Deployment scenario (final) 
 
Where/how used: Exterior façade render layer on sand-exposed building envelopes 
(desert/perimeter conditions). The coating is installed as a serviceable, replaceable 
outer skin or sacrificial top layer.  
 
Activation: Periodic, controlled wetting (maintenance spray or rare rainfall events) 
triggers short biological activity windows for crack sealing and dust binding.  
Measurable success metrics 
 
Crack sealing efficiency: % reduction in water/air permeability through controlled 
microcracks (before vs. after activation cycles).  
 
Mineral deposition localization: mineral mass gain concentrated at crack paths (vs. 
bulk pore clogging).  
 
Dust suppression: reduction in dust release rate under standardized airflow abrasion.  
 
Erosion resistance: mass loss under sand-blast/abrasion tests compared with non-
living control render.


## Page 232


 
Mechanical retention: coating adhesion strength and flexural toughness retention 
after repeated wet–dry cycles.  
Main failure modes + mitigations 
 
Over-mineralization clogging pores → limit ion/nutrient dosing; use graded porosity 
and localized capsules.  
 
Loss of viability from alkaline/UV/desiccation → encapsulation + UV-stable topcoat 
that remains vapor-permeable; keep active niches sub-surface.  
 
Uncontrolled fungal overgrowth/aesthetics drift → nutrient gating (short pulses only) 
+ physical confinement to microcavities.  
 
Salt/ion accumulation → periodic rinse protocols; replaceable outer layer design.  
 
6) Production instantiation 
Must implement CMM + BMN: composite manufacturing + colonization + 
biomineralization.  
Step-by-step workflow (implementable) 
1. Composite material preparation (CMM)  
o 
Dry blend mineral binder + fillers + microfibers; prepare polymer modifier 
dispersion separately.  
2. Biological component preparation  
o 
Produce bacterial spores (bulk spore prep) and fungal propagules (e.g., 
spores or robust cells), then encapsulate both in buffered hydrogel 
microbeads (100–500 µm) containing minimal nutrient payload for one 
activation cycle.  
3. Integration strategy  
o 
Mix microbeads into the polymer-modified mineral render at low shear 
(preserve bead integrity).  
4. Coating application  
o 
Apply 2–5 mm render layer to façade substrate; cure under conditions that do 
not dissolve/rupture capsules (controlled humidity/temperature).  
5. Cell colonization conditioning (post-cure)  
o 
Controlled “priming” wetting to hydrate pores and establish initial 
microcolonies/hyphal anchoring in near-surface porosity.  
6. Biomineralization activation (BMN)  
o 
Deliver a short, controlled activation solution that provides mineralization 
precursors to the bacterial niches; terminate by allowing rapid drying 
(biological off-switch).  
7. Stabilization  
o 
Optional breathable protective topcoat to manage UV/water flux while 
preserving oxygen diffusion.  
8. Quality control & reproducibility  
o 
Composite QC: porosity distribution, adhesion strength, permeability.  
o 
Biology QC: viability in capsules (CFU/viability assay), spatial localization 
(surface vs. bulk).  
o 
Function QC: standardized crack-healing tests across repeated activation 
cycles.  
Manufacturability, scalability, feasibility justification 
 
Composite mixing/coating is aligned with established façade render workflows 
(CMM).  
 
Biological integration is compatible with HYB by keeping living components 
isolated during harsh curing steps and only activating post-cure.  
 
BMN is implemented as a controlled post-installation phase, consistent with building 
maintenance cycles.  
 
7) Layered integrated summary of the fully instantiated ontology-derived ELM

under sand exposure. The biological system pairs a mineralizing bacterium (recommended:

Iteration 1 
Below are five dataset-derived (graph) ELM candidates whose core 5-slot ontology definition 
is already present in the graph. I only complete Application and Production method to fit the 
Titan habitat + infrastructure boundary conditions (cryogenic exterior, hydrocarbon-rich 
environment, low solar, no surface liquid water, remote/low-maintenance). All candidates are 
framed as sealed, modular subsystems (cartridges/panels/bioreactors) integrated into 
habitats/ISRU units. 
 
1) Organic-solvent biocatalyst hydrogel for hydrocarbon ISRU chemistry 
1) Origin & selection rationale (dataset-derived) 
Graph candidate explicitly targets biocatalysis in pure organic solvents, which is unusually 
aligned with Titan’s hydrocarbon-dominant environment, provided operation occurs in a 
shielded, temperature-controlled cartridge. 
2) Completed ontology code 
BAC–HYB–EUB–3D–LPO–CAT+BTP–OPM 
(core 5 slots unchanged) 
3) Application-oriented concept (1 sentence) 
A sealed alginate hydrogel biocatalyst module hosting genetically modified Escherichia coli 
to catalyze targeted conversions of hydrocarbon-derived feedstocks into useful precursors for 
habitat/ISRU chemical loops under Titan-relevant handling constraints. 
4) Justification (3–6 bullets) 
 
Biological suitability: E. coli is stated as genetically modified and embedded in 
alginate hydrogel, enabling controlled biocatalysis while physically confining cells.  
 
Titan boundary conditions: Direct compatibility with hydrocarbon media (entry states 
“pure organic solvents”); operation still assumed inside a thermally managed unit 
(cryogenic exterior otherwise incompatible with active metabolism).  
 
Water constraint: Hydrogel provides a microaqueous niche while the external process 
stream can be hydrocarbon-rich; this supports Titan deployment where bulk liquid 
water is not available at the surface.  
 
Low-maintenance integration: Cartridge-style, replaceable biocatalyst bed fits remote 
operation + limited resupply (swap modules vs. servicing open cultures).  
 
Containment note: Genetic safeguard specifics are Not present in the ontology/graph; 
only physical encapsulation is directly supported by the entry.

biocatalysis” is already explicit).

Brief note on what was not selected (and why) 
Several graph entries are photosynthesis-dependent (cyanobacteria/microalgae). Under 
Titan’s extremely low solar energy and cryogenic exterior, they are generally less compatible 
unless paired with substantial artificial lighting/thermal support; such Titan-operational 
details are Not present in the ontology/graph, so they were not prioritized here.

How geometry supports function:

5) Application instantiation 
Deployment scenario (Titan-relevant handling): 
 
A sealed, thermally managed ISRU chemistry skid inside a habitat/industrial bay 
processes a hydrocarbon-rich stream (e.g., purified solvent/fraction from upstream 
separation). The ELM cartridge provides selective bioconversion to a targeted 
intermediate (oxygenated or functionalized precursor) that feeds downstream 
synthesis (polymer/resin/additive loops).  
Measurable success metrics 
 
Conversion (%): substrate → target product per pass and at steady state.  
 
Selectivity (%): target vs byproducts.  
 
Space–time yield: g product L⁻¹ h⁻¹ (reactor volume basis).  
 
Operational half-life: time to 50% activity loss under continuous solvent exposure.  
 
Containment: CFU detected downstream = 0 (or below detection), plus 
endotoxin/bioburden thresholds as required.  
Main failure modes + mitigations 
 
Hydrogel dehydration / shrinkage: use solvent-resistant outer membrane + internal 
humidity/water management; schedule bead rehydration cycles.  
 
Solvent toxicity / membrane breach: multi-layer barrier + pressure relief + leak 
detection.  
 
Channeling / pressure rise: graded bead sizes, inlet distributors, periodic 
backflushing.  
 
Genetic drift / loss of function: operate as resting cells with short campaign length; 
swap cartridges; genetic safeguards Not present in the ontology/graph (can be added 
as an engineering layer).  
 
6) Production instantiation 
Production method: OPM (encapsulation and combined processes allowed).  
Step-by-step workflow 
1. Strain construction (bio): integrate pathway/enzyme cassette; define induction/control 
strategy (specific genetic safeguard recipes: Not present in the ontology/graph).  
2. Seed train & biocatalyst prep: cultivate under defined conditions; switch to “resting 
cell” state (wash + resuspend in buffered saline with required cofactors).  
3. Alginate bead encapsulation (matrix integration):  
o 
Mix cell suspension with sterile sodium alginate (e.g., 1.5–2.5% w/v).  
o 
Drip into CaCl₂ bath to form beads; cure 10–30 min; rinse to remove excess 
Ca²⁺.  
4. Optional bead shelling: add thin polyelectrolyte coating to reduce cell escape 
(implementation detail; not ontology-defined).  
5. Cartridge assembly (hybrid module):  
o 
Pack beads into solvent-compatible housing.  
o 
Install solvent-resistant, microporous barrier (retain beads/cells; allow 
molecular transport).  
o 
Add inlet/outlet distributors + pressure/temperature sensors.  
6. Conditioning: equilibrate with internal aqueous buffer, then introduce solvent stream 
gradually (ramp protocol to avoid osmotic shock).  
7. Quality control (release tests):  
o 
Bead size distribution, mechanical integrity.  
o 
Cell leakage test (CFU in effluent under flow).


## Page 249


o 
Baseline catalytic activity assay in representative solvent conditions.  
o 
Pressure drop vs flow characterization.  
8. Scalability strategy: scale by numbering-up cartridges (parallel skids) rather than 
large single beds; standardized bead production (nozzle arrays).

3) Matrix/material candidates 
Material candidate A — Ca²⁺-crosslinked alginate hydrogel (primary, input-consistent) 
 
Composition: sodium alginate (2–3 wt%) ionically crosslinked with CaCl₂ to form 
mm-scale beads or a monolith.

Bio-compatibility: mild gelation conditions maintain high cell viability; tunable

 
Composition: PEGDA (e.g., 5–15 wt% with biocompatible photoinitiator) or PVA 
(10–15 wt%) freeze–thaw cryogel.  
 
Bio-compatibility: both can be tuned for lower swelling and higher mechanical 
robustness than alginate in long runs.  
 
Interface mechanisms: covalent network (PEGDA) or crystalline physical crosslinks 
(PVA) with cell entrapment; surface functionalization (e.g., charged groups) to 
manage biofilm tendency.  
 
Why preferred: improved mechanical durability and reduced deformation under 
flow/pressure cycling.  
Selected matrix/material system for this instantiation: Alginate hydrogel (A), with an optional 
thin stabilizing outer coat if cell leakage or bead abrasion is observed. 
 
4) Geometry and structural instantiation 
Chosen 3D architecture: packed-bed hydrogel bead biocatalyst cartridge (true 3D bioreactor) 
 
Quantitative geometry (baseline prototype):  
o 
Bead diameter: 0.8–1.5 mm (narrow distribution for predictable pressure 
drop)  
o 
Packed-bed volume: 0.1–2 L per replaceable cartridge (lab → pilot scaling)  
o 
Bed void fraction: ~0.35–0.45 (typical packed spheres; defines residence 
time)  
o 
Retainers: dual mesh screens (upstream/downstream) with pore size <200 
µm to prevent bead escape  
 
How geometry supports cell viability/function  
o 
Mass transport: substrate partitions from the process stream into the bead 
surface; diffusion into bead interior supports distributed catalysis while 
limiting shear stress on cells.  
o 
Cofactor regeneration: cells remain metabolically active in the microaqueous 
bead interior; low nutrient “maintenance dosing” avoids runaway biomass.


## Page 254


o 
Mechanical integrity: beads act as sacrificial, swappable units; abrasion 
mitigated by gentle flow regime and optional bead coating.  
 
Why this is optimal vs other plausible 3D geometries  
o 
Compared with a monolithic hydrogel block: packed beads improve 
controllability (replaceability, lower cracking risk, easier QC per batch).  
o 
Compared with free-cell slurry: packed-bed maximizes containment and 
simplifies downstream separation (critical for Titan-style closed-loop 
handling).  
 
5) Application instantiation 
Deployment scenario (CAT+BTP) 
A sealed, replaceable biocatalyst cartridge integrated into an ISRU chemical skid that 
processes hydrocarbon-derived, pre-activated intermediates (generated upstream by non-
biological steps) to produce value-added precursors (oxygenated or functionalized organics) 
used in habitat material synthesis, consumables manufacturing, or closed-loop chemical 
management. 
Measurable success metrics 
 
Catalytic performance: conversion per pass (%) and product selectivity (%) at 
defined residence time.  
 
Space–time yield: g L⁻¹ h⁻¹ of target product from the cartridge.  
 
Operational stability: half-life of activity under continuous run (hours–weeks) and 
number of reuse cycles before replacement.  
 
Containment: cell leakage below detection threshold (CFU/mL or qPCR) in the 
external stream.  
 
Hydraulic stability: pressure drop remains within spec (no bed clogging/collapse).  
Main failure modes + mitigations 
 
Solvent intrusion / dehydration of beads: enforce barrier layers and strict water-
activity control inside the cartridge; schedule “rehydration/refresh” cycles.  
 
Freeze risk / thermal gradients: operate only inside thermally regulated enclosure; 
add thermal buffering and sensors.  
 
Biofilm overgrowth → clogging: nutrient limitation strategy; periodic low-shear 
backflush; bead surface chemistry to reduce adhesion.  
 
Genetic drift / loss of function: use short run-times with replacement, or 
cryopreserved standardized bead lots; QC enzyme activity prior to deployment.  
 
6) Production instantiation (OPM) 
OPM-consistent manufacturing principle: combine practical methods and encapsulation 
within engineered constructs into a reproducible cartridge workflow.  
Step-by-step production process 
1. Strain build (biological prep)  
o 
Construct GM E. coli expressing the target enzyme module + cofactor 
regeneration support.  
o 
Bank as master cell stock; verify genotype and baseline activity (bench 
assay).  
2. Biomass production  
o 
Seed fermentation to mid-log phase; harvest and concentrate cells (e.g., 
gentle centrifugation) to a defined wet-cell density.  
3. Hydrogel formulation  
o 
Prepare sterile sodium alginate solution (2–3 wt%) in buffered saline; adjust 
viscosity for bead formation.  
4. Encapsulation (core OPM action)  
o 
Mix concentrated cells into alginate under aseptic conditions.  
o 
Form beads by droplet extrusion into CaCl₂ gelling bath; cure to target 
modulus.


## Page 255


o 
Optional: apply a thin stabilizing outer layer (e.g., polyelectrolyte or 
secondary alginate coat) to reduce leakage/abrasion.  
5. Cartridge assembly (hybrid scaffold integration)  
o 
Load beads into a sterile, solvent-compatible housing (e.g., stainless steel or 
high-performance polymer).  
o 
Install upstream/downstream retainers and a defined flow distributor to avoid 
channeling.  
6. Conditioning  
o 
Perfuse with buffered aqueous maintenance medium to equilibrate beads; 
then transition to process operation with controlled partitioning of substrates.  
7. Quality control and reproducibility  
o 
Bead size distribution; compressive modulus; permeability proxy (tracer 
diffusion test).  
o 
Biological QC: viability staining and specific activity assay (per gram wet 
beads).  
o 
Containment QC: challenge test for cell leakage under flow.  
8. Packaging for deployment  
o 
Store hydrated at 4 °C short-term or cryopreserve as standardized bead lots 
(if validated), then assemble cartridges on demand.  
Manufacturability/scalability 
 
Scale by parallel cartridge numbering (“number-up”) rather than single huge beds, 
maintaining QC consistency.

3) Matrix/material candidates 
Material option 1 — Calcium alginate hydrogel beads (ionically crosslinked polysaccharide 
hydrogel) 
 
Composition: sodium alginate crosslinked with Ca²⁺ (CaCl₂ bath), optionally 
reinforced with a small fraction of cellulose nanofibers (polymeric reinforcement 
consistent with LPO).  
 
Bio-compatibility: mild gelation conditions preserve bacterial viability; enables high 
cell loading.  
 
Interface mechanism: physical entrapment + biofilm micro-anchoring within alginate 
pores.  
Justification: simplest scalable immobilization route for packed-bed cartridges; supports 
“replaceable module” operation. 
Material option 2 — PEGDA hydrogel monolith (photocrosslinked poly(ethylene glycol) 
diacrylate) 
 
Composition: PEGDA + photoinitiator; optional porogen to tune pore size.  
 
Bio-compatibility: requires controlled photopolymerization dose to preserve viability 
(post-encapsulation washing to remove residuals).  
 
Interface mechanism: covalent network provides stable confinement; tunable 
permeability.  
Justification: better mechanical stability and dimensional control than purely ionic gels; 
enables monolithic reactors with engineered flow channels. 
Recommended matrix: alginate beads for the cartridge-style, replaceable packed-bed concept; 
PEGDA monolith as an alternative when tighter control of pressure drop/channeling is 
required. 
 
4) Geometry and structural instantiation 
Chosen 3D architecture (Phylum = 3D) 
3D packed-bed cartridge containing hydrogel beads (or, alternatively, a channeled monolith). 
Quantitative geometry (baseline): 
 
Bead diameter: 0.8–1.2 mm (manufacturable by nozzle dripping; balances packing 
and diffusion).  
 
Effective diffusion length to cell-rich regions: target ≤200–300 µm by (i) placing 
cells near bead periphery or (ii) using porous bead formulations.  
 
Cartridge form factor: cylindrical module (e.g., 2–5 cm diameter lab-scale; scaled by 
numbering-up).  
How geometry supports: 
 
Cell viability/function: bead microaqueous domains buffer cells from direct solvent 
exposure; cells remain immobilized and retained.  
 
Mass transport: hydrophobic substrate partitions from the external organic phase into 
the bead boundary layer; short diffusion paths reduce rate limitation.


## Page 260


 
Mechanical performance: packed bed enables straightforward replacement; pressure 
drop managed via bead size distribution and bed porosity control.  
Why this over plausible alternatives: compared with a thin-film (2D) reactor, the 3D packed-
bed provides higher volumetric productivity and easier module replacement; compared with 
loose suspensions, it improves containment and simplifies separation. 
 
5) Application instantiation 
Deployment scenario (CAT + BTP) 
A sealed, replaceable biocatalyst cartridge integrated into a Titan habitat/ISRU chemistry 
skid, executing a defined biotransformation step on a hydrocarbon-rich process stream while 
maintaining a protected aqueous microenvironment inside the polymer scaffold. 
Measurable success metrics 
 
Conversion (%) of target feedstock per pass (or per residence time).  
 
Selectivity (%) to desired intermediate(s).  
 
Space-time yield (g·L⁻¹·h⁻¹) referenced to cartridge volume.  
 
Operational stability: activity half-life under continuous operation; number of swap 
cycles before performance drops below threshold.  
 
Containment: cell leakage (CFU or DNA proxy) downstream of the membrane 
barrier.  
Failure modes + mitigations 
 
Solvent toxicity / dehydration of hydrogel: mitigate by maintaining controlled 
hydration, using solvent-tolerant chassis option, and limiting direct solvent 
penetration via bead formulation.  
 
Mass-transfer limitation: mitigate by reducing diffusion length (cell-peripheral 
localization) and optimizing flow/packing.  
 
Channeling / pressure drop rise: mitigate with controlled bead size distribution, pre-
filters, and monolith fallback.  
 
Biological drift/contamination: mitigate by using resting-cell operation and cartridge 
replacement; genetic safeguards are Not present in the ontology/graph.  
 
6) Production instantiation 
Step-by-step workflow (Production = OPM; “combinations + encapsulation”) 
1. Polymer matrix preparation  
o 
Prepare sterile alginate solution (or PEGDA precursor) with defined viscosity 
and osmolarity for cell compatibility.  
2. Biological preparation  
o 
Cultivate engineered E. coli to target expression state; harvest and 
concentrate cells; switch to resting-cell buffer.  
3. Encapsulation / immobilization  
o 
Alginate route: drip cell–alginate suspension into CaCl₂ to form beads; cure 
to target stiffness; rinse to remove excess Ca²⁺.  
o 
PEGDA route: mix cells into precursor; photopolymerize under viability-
preserving conditions; wash thoroughly.  
4. Cartridge assembly  
o 
Load beads/monolith into a solvent-compatible housing; install cell-retentive 
membrane barrier at outlet; integrate ports and seals.  
5. Conditioning  
o 
Pre-equilibrate with buffered aqueous phase (inside) and then introduce 
organic process stream gradually to avoid osmotic/solvent shock.  
6. Quality control  
o 
Bead size distribution / monolith porosity, cell loading (cells per mL gel), 
leakage test, baseline catalytic activity assay, pressure-drop curve.  
7. Scalability & reproducibility  
o 
Scale via numbering-up of identical cartridges; enforce QC acceptance 
windows for loading/activity/pressure drop.


## Page 261


Why feasible: all steps are standardizable unit operations (culture → immobilization → 
module assembly → QC) consistent with OPM’s “combinations + encapsulation” scope.

C. Integrated paragraph (~100 words) 
BAC–HYB–EUB–3D–LPO–CAT+BTP–OPM defines a bacterial hybrid living polymer built 
as a sealed 3D hydrogel reactor for catalysis and bioprocessing. The biological core is 
immobilized eubacteria—recommended engineered E. coli (K-12 derivative), with 
Pseudomonas putida KT2440 as a solvent-resilient alternative—operated as resting cells to 
prioritize catalytic activity over growth. Cells are confined within Ca-alginate beads (or a 
PEGDA monolith) assembled into a packed-bed cartridge with a cell-retentive membrane. 
Deployed inside a temperature-controlled ISRU/habitat unit, it targets measurable 
conversion/selectivity and long-term stability with cartridge-level replaceability as the 
maintenance strategy.

2) Biological instantiation 
Candidate A — Sporosarcina pasteurii (ureolytic, strong CaCO₃ precipitation) 
 
Functional role: dormant spores embedded in micro-reservoirs; upon repair-cycle 
activation, germinate locally and drive CaCO₃ precipitation to fill cracks (BMN) and 
restore stiffness/sealability.  
 
Viability/growth requirements (design targets):  
o 
Moisture: requires localized wetting in crack zone (brief pulses).  
o 
Temperature: mesophilic operation (habitat-controlled layer; avoid external 
cryogenic exposure).  
o 
pH: tolerates alkaline microenvironments when protected (spore state during 
curing; activation in buffered micro-niche).  
o 
O₂/nutrients: aerobic metabolism; supply via microcapsule nutrient core 
(minimal organics) + dissolved oxygen in repair fluid.  
 
Biocontainment strategy (implementable):  
o 
Physical: double-encapsulation: (i) inner nutrient core + spores, (ii) outer 
brittle shell that ruptures only when crack intersects (mechanical gating).  
o 
Physiological: use sporulation for long shelf-life; activation requires 
provided urea/Ca²⁺ solution (no autonomous growth without feed).  
o 
Genetic (optional): auxotrophy for a non-natural amino acid supplied only in 
repair fluid (limits proliferation outside repair cycle).  
 
Why preferred: high, well-established mineralization capacity and robust spore-
forming survival for harsh processing/long dormancy.  
Candidate B — Bacillus subtilis (spore-former, biofilm/ECM + mineral nucleation) 
 
Functional role: spores germinate in activated cracks; biofilm matrix promotes 
mineral nucleation/anchoring, improving adherence of precipitate to crack faces and 
reducing washout.  
 
Viability/growth requirements (design targets):  
o 
Moisture: intermittent wetting sufficient (short activation cycles).  
o 
Temperature: mesophilic; stable as spores long-term.  
o 
pH: alkaline tolerance improved by encapsulation + buffered micro-reservoir. 
o 
O₂/nutrients: aerobic/facultative; nutrient dosing via microcapsule.  
 
Biocontainment strategy (implementable):


## Page 265


o 
Physical gating: crack-triggered capsule rupture (as above) + cell-retentive 
micro-porous shell that limits outward migration.  
o 
Physiological: sporulation control and nutrient dependence; cells return to 
dormancy as fluid dries.  
 
Why preferred: robust industrial chassis, strong sporulation, and ECM/biofilm 
advantages for crack-surface binding (useful under limited repair-fluid volumes).  
Recommended candidate: Candidate A — Sporosarcina pasteurii 
 
Selection rationale: maximizes mineral precipitation rate per unit water/activation 
time (aligns with your “event-triggered repair cycles”), with spores as a proven long-
dormancy state for embedded formulations.  
 
Trade-offs vs B. subtilis: B. subtilis may improve crack-surface adhesion via biofilm, 
but S. pasteurii is typically the more direct “mineralization workhorse” for rapid 
CaCO₃ sealing; adhesion can be recovered by adding mineral nucleation additives to 
the capsule formulation.

Material candidate 1 — Cementitious “living ceramic” matrix (silicate-rich) 
 
Composition (explicit): hydrated calcium silicate phases (C–S–H–type binder) +

later in localized crack microenvironment (not in bulk paste).  
 
Interface mechanism: crack surfaces provide high pH + Ca²⁺; biomineralized CaCO₃ 
bridges mechanically interlock with C–S–H matrix and rough crack faces (mineral

Why preferred: directly matches “self-healing concrete module” concept; ceramic-

Bio-compatibility: higher alkalinity demands stronger encapsulation/buffering; 
spores remain dormant until activation fluid locally neutralizes.  
 
Interface mechanism: Ca²⁺-enriched activation fluid promotes CaCO₃ precipitation 
that keys into geopolymer pore network and crack asperities.  
 
Why preferred: lower Portland-cement dependency and strong ceramic-like network; 
good for “controlled habitat fabrication” where mix design is tightly managed.  
Selected matrix for this instantiation: Candidate 1 (cementitious silicate matrix) to remain 
maximally consistent with the provided “bacterial concrete” concept while still satisfying

rib, or internal liner) where temperature/humidity are controlled; the living system is 
not exposed to the external cryogenic/hydrocarbon environment (consistent with your 
rationale).  
 
Repair-cycle concept (explicit):  
1. crack detection (e.g., permeability increase / acoustic emission sensor 
embedded in the module),  
2. localized injection or wicking of a minimal repair fluid (water + Ca²⁺ + 
carbon source / urea depending on chassis),  
3. 24–72 h hold for biomineralization, then dry-down back to dormancy.  
 
Measurable success metrics  
o 
Crack closure ratio: % reduction in mean crack aperture (microscopy/µCT).  
o 
Permeability reduction: water/gas permeability drop vs cracked state.  
o 
Mechanical recovery: regain of flexural strength or stiffness (3-point 
bending) after healing.  
o 
Mineral deposition yield: CaCO₃ mass per crack volume (gravimetric/µCT 
segmentation).  
 
Main failure modes + mitigations  
o 
Premature capsule rupture during mixing: mitigate with shell strength QA 
and mixing-energy limits.  
o 
Loss of viability from alkalinity/heat of hydration: mitigate via spore payload 
+ buffered inner core + delayed activation only.  
o 
Insufficient reactant delivery into tight cracks: mitigate with low-viscosity 
repair fluid and capillary-wicking additives.  
o 
Over-mineralization blocking intended pores/vents: mitigate by limiting 
reactant dosing and time-boxing activation.  
 
6) Production instantiation 
Step-by-step production workflow (Production = BMN) 
1. Microcapsule fabrication (biological payload unit)  
o 
Prepare spores of S. pasteurii at high titer; dry and stabilize with trehalose.  
o 
Form an inner core: spores + minimal nutrient/precursor package (e.g., urea 
+ buffering salts).  
o 
Apply a brittle ceramic-like shell (e.g., silica/aluminosilicate sol–gel) tuned 
to rupture under crack-induced strain.  
2. Concrete (ceramic-like) matrix preparation  
o 
Mix cementitious binder + aggregates with controlled water-to-binder ratio.  
o 
Add microcapsules late in mixing to reduce shear damage; verify capsule 
integrity by sieving/microscopy sampling.  
3. Casting/curing of 3D structural module  
o 
Cast into habitat-module geometry; cure under controlled 
humidity/temperature to minimize uncontrolled early cracking.


## Page 267


o 
Maintain microcapsules dry/isolated (no intentional activation during 
curing).  
4. Commissioning: activation readiness  
o 
Integrate ports or capillary-wick pathways for repair-fluid delivery (if design 
includes them).  
o 
Baseline QA: compressive/flexural strength; capsule distribution via X-
ray/CT spot checks.  
5. Operational biomineralization (repair cycle)  
o 
When a crack forms and intersects capsules, shell rupture releases 
spores/precursors into the crack.  
o 
Inject/wick minimal repair fluid to germinate cells locally and drive BMN 
CaCO₃ precipitation.  
o 
Dry-down returns cells to dormancy; repeatable for multiple cycles.  
Manufacturability, scalability, QC 
 
Manufacturability: microcapsules can be batch-produced; concrete casting is 
standard; integration is compatible with modular construction.  
 
Scalability: scale by “numbering up” modules and capsule production batches; 
maintain consistent capsule PSD (particle size distribution).  
 
Quality control (minimum set):  
o 
Capsule size distribution (laser diffraction / microscopy),  
o 
capsule survival fraction after mixing (viability assay post-extraction),  
o 
healing performance on standardized crack coupons.  
Feasibility justification: The core functional mechanism remains biomineralization (BMN) as 
defined by the ontology, while the surrounding steps are conventional manufacturing controls 
required to embed and protect the living BMN-capable system in a ceramic-like structural 
matrix.

3) Matrix/material candidates 
Material candidate 1 — Cementitious ceramic matrix (“structural living concrete”) 
 
Composition: Portland-cement-based mortar/concrete + silica fume (densification) +

 
Bio-compatibility: cells are not dispersed freely; spores are isolated in 
capsules/porous carriers that buffer pH and protect from hydration heat.  
 
Interface mechanisms:  
o 
Capsule rupture → spore release into crack water film.  
o 
Crack faces provide high-energy mineral nucleation surfaces; bacterial ECM 
and cell walls act as nucleation templates.

 
Composition: metakaolin-based (or fly-ash-based) geopolymer binder + 
sand/aggregate; ceramic-like aluminosilicate network.  
 
Bio-compatibility: capsules provide localized buffering; geopolymer can reduce 
calcium hydroxide abundance vs Portland cement, potentially easing micro-niche 
design.  
 
Interface mechanisms:  
o 
Mineralization is directed to cracks via capsule placement and Ca²⁺ reservoirs 
(since geopolymer chemistry may be Ca-poor unless formulated).  
Preferred material system: Portland-cement-based matrix (candidate 1) for straightforward 
structural certification pathways and abundant Ca²⁺ availability supporting CaCO₃ 
biomineralization at crack sites.

o 
Crack water film thickness ~10–100 µm supplies diffusion.  
o 
Target diffusion distance from released spores to active mineralization zone: 
≤500 µm (crack-scale).

Mechanical performance logic:  
o 
Capsules sized to survive mixing but rupture under crack propagation.  
o 
Fiber microreinforcement limits crack width to ≤300–500 µm, matching

capsule–crack intersection and keeps cells dormant and protected until a local damage event, 
aligning with low-maintenance operation.

Excess porosity / strength loss → limit capsule volume fraction; use fibers and

6) Production instantiation (BMN) 
Step-by-step production workflow (implementable) 
1. Spore production (biological prep)  
o 
Grow B. subtilis to sporulation; harvest spores; wash and dry to a stable

2. Healing microcapsule fabrication (cell integration)  
o 
Prepare a buffered hydrogel core (e.g., alginate-based) containing: spores 
(10⁶–10⁸ spores per capsule-equivalent), Ca-source (Ca-lactate or Ca-
acetate), minimal nutrients, and bicarbonate/CO₂-capture chemistry as 
needed.  
o 
Form 200–800 µm capsules via dripping/emulsification; apply a thin 
ceramic-compatible shell (e.g., silica or polymer-ceramic hybrid coating) 
tuned for crack-rupture.  
3. Concrete matrix preparation (material prep)  
o 
Mix cement + fillers + aggregates + fibers (if used) under standard QA specs; 
control water-to-binder ratio for mechanical performance.  
4. Capsule addition (manufacturing integration)  
o 
Add capsules late in mixing at reduced shear; target 0.5–2 vol%.  
5. Casting + curing (post-processing)  
o 
Cast panels/layers; cure under controlled temperature to protect capsule 
integrity and preserve dormant spores.  
6. Quality control & reproducibility  
o 
Capsule size distribution (laser diffraction/sieving).  
o 
Capsule survival fraction post-mixing (microscopy + extraction).  
o 
Mechanical baseline (compressive/flexural) and crack-width control under 
standardized loading.  
o 
Biomineralization readiness test on coupon cracks (sealed-system water 
exposure).  
7. Operational activation protocol (in service)


## Page 273


o 
Damage event → moisture delivery to the affected zone (controlled internal 
loop) → capsule rupture + local biomineralization → drying return to 
dormancy.  
Why feasible: all steps use standard bioprocessing (spores) + established encapsulation + 
conventional concrete manufacturing; the only “living” part is dormant until a local 
damage+moisture trigger, consistent with BMN as the functional production mechanism.

2) Biological instantiation 
Candidate A — Sporosarcina pasteurii (ureolytic MICP specialist; resting cells/spores) 
 
Functional role: On-demand CaCO₃ precipitation inside cracks via carbonate 
generation and crystal nucleation on cell surfaces; mineral bridges restore 
watertightness and stiffness.  
 
Viability/growth requirements:  
o

cracks).  
o

operation reduces nutrient needs.

compatible chassis) 


triggers carbonate precipitation (via metabolic alkalinization/carbonate availability) 
and biofilm-mediated nucleation in cracks.  
 
Viability/growth requirements:  
o 
Moisture: episodic hydration required; spores tolerate desiccation between 
cycles.  
o 
pH: strong alkaliphile; well matched to high-pH cement pore solutions.


## Page 277


o

o

 
Spore-formers / resting-cell systems best match the HYB + 3D + LCE constraints 
(survive mixing/curing, remain dormant for long periods, activate only when water is 
present).  
 
Mineral precipitation is the native “production mechanism” implied by BMN, 
directly aligning biology with the required manufacturing mode.  
Recommended biological candidate 
Recommended: Candidate B (Bacillus pseudofirmus spores). 
 
Reason: maximizes durability in alkaline, dehydrating ceramic matrices and supports 
event-triggered repair cycles with minimal continuous resources.  
 
Trade-offs vs Candidate A: may produce slower mineralization rates than specialized 
ureolytic systems; however it avoids reliance on urea dosing and reduces unwanted 
byproducts in closed habitat loops.  
 
3) Matrix/material candidates 
Material candidate 1 — Cementitious living ceramic matrix (Portland-cement / calcium-
silicate-hydrate dominant) 
 
Exact composition (example specification): OPC-based matrix with silica fume 
(SiO₂) + limestone filler (CaCO₃) + controlled w/c ratio; embedded porous ceramic 
microcarriers (aluminosilicate) loaded with spores and Ca-source.  
 
Compatibility with biology: spores tolerate high pH and dehydration; microcarriers 
protect from shear during mixing and isolate cells from bulk pore chemistry until 
activated.  
 
Interface mechanisms: cell wall / EPS acts as nucleation template; Ca²⁺ availability in 
crack water promotes calcite nucleation and growth; precipitation anchors into C–S–
H pore surfaces.  
Material candidate 2 — Alkali-activated geopolymer living ceramic (aluminosilicate 
network) 
 
Exact composition: metakaolin (Al₂Si₂O₇) and/or slag-derived binder activated with 
Na/K silicate solution; integrated porous silicate ceramic beads containing spores and 
mineral precursors.  
 
Compatibility with biology: higher early alkalinity requires stronger encapsulation; 
spore-only storage recommended; activation fluid buffered to permit short 
germination/mineralization windows.  
 
Interface mechanisms: aluminosilicate surfaces provide heterogeneous nucleation 
sites; mineral precipitation mechanically interlocks within geopolymer gel pores.  
Selection justification 
 
Candidate 1 is the most direct mapping to “bacterial self-healing concrete” while 
remaining within LCE (ceramic-dominant matrices).  
 
Candidate 2 is attractive for habitat manufacturing where alternative binders may be 
prioritized; it remains ceramic-class via silicate/aluminosilicate dominance.  
 
4) Geometry and structural instantiation 
3D architecture (Phylum = 3D) 
 
Structural concept: load-bearing habitat wall panel with an inner self-healing layer 
(20–60 mm) containing distributed living micro-reservoirs.  
 
Quantitative geometry targets  
o 
Micro-reservoir form factor: porous ceramic beads or microcapsules, 200–
800 µm diameter.


## Page 278


o 
Volume fraction: 1–3 vol% (balance healing capacity vs strength loss).  
o 
Crack service envelope: designed for 0.05–0.5 mm crack widths; micro-
reservoir spacing tuned so a crack intersects multiple reservoirs per cm.  
o 
Effective diffusion path during activation: ≤ 0.2–0.5 mm from crack fluid to 
the nearest spore niche (supports short repair cycles).  
 
How geometry supports function  
o 
Cell viability: spores isolated in micro-reservoir pores; dormant storage in 
dry matrix; activation only in wetted crack-connected volumes.  
o 
Mass transport: cracks act as transient conduits for water/Ca²⁺/limited 
nutrients; mineral precipitation progressively occludes crack permeability.  
o 
Mechanical performance: mineral bridges form across crack faces; 
precipitation concentrates at damage zones rather than bulk.  
 
Why 3D is optimal vs 2D/FR alternatives  
o 
Crack networks in structural ceramics are volumetric; embedding living 
reservoirs through the full thickness provides repair capacity independent of 
surface access.  
 
5) Application instantiation 
Deployment scenario (SPE+SLB) 
 
Where/how used: sealed, habitat-integrated structural panels (interior structural layer) 
in space colonization infrastructure; living function is not exposed as an open surface 
biota but embedded within controlled building materials.  
 
Measurable success metrics  
o 
Crack sealing efficiency: % reduction in crack aperture (optical/CT) after 
each activation cycle.  
o 
Permeability reduction: water/air permeability drop across healed region 
(e.g., ≥1–2 orders of magnitude).  
o 
Mechanical recovery: regain of flexural strength or stiffness (e.g., ≥50% of 
pre-crack stiffness after N cycles).  
o 
Mineral deposition rate: CaCO₃ mass per crack volume per activation cycle.  
 
Main failure modes + mitigations  
1. 
Micro-reservoir rupture during mixing → increase shell strength; use lightweight 
ceramic aggregates; lower shear mixing.  
2. 
Premature activation during curing → keep nutrients separated; activate only via 
post-cure injected solution.  
3. 
Spore inactivation by high alkalinity/heat → spore-only loading; thermal 
management during curing; protective ceramic pores.  
4. 
Crack bypasses reservoirs → multi-modal reservoir sizes; gradient distributions near 
tensile zones.  
5. 
Uncontrolled mineralization clogging needed porosity → localized dosing (small 
fluid volumes), short duty cycles, stop when permeability threshold reached.  
 
6) Production instantiation 
Step-by-step workflow (Production = BMN) 
1. Biological preparation  
o 
Culture selected eubacterium; induce sporulation; wash and dry spores.  
o 
QC: spore viability (CFU after rehydration) and mineralization proxy assay 
(carbonate precipitation in test solution).  
2. Micro-reservoir fabrication (HYB requirement)  
o 
Produce porous ceramic beads (silica/aluminosilicate) or ceramic-shelled 
microcapsules (200–800 µm).  
o 
Load spores + dry mineral precursors (e.g., Ca-source + minimal carbon 
source) into pores; seal with a thin permeable ceramic/silicate layer.  
o 
QC: size distribution, crush resistance, spore loading (qPCR/CFU after bead 
dissolution), permeability to activation fluids.


## Page 279


3. Ceramic matrix preparation (LCE requirement)  
o 
Prepare cementitious/geopolymer binder; incorporate standard aggregates 
and admixtures compatible with reservoir stability.  
4. Integration  
o 
Blend micro-reservoirs into fresh mix at controlled shear; cast into habitat 
panel molds.  
o 
Cure under controlled temperature/humidity to avoid reservoir damage and 
preserve spore viability.  
5. Activation protocol (event-triggered)  
o 
Detect crack (embedded strain/acoustic emission sensors or periodic NDT).  
o 
Inject/infuse a small volume activation solution along crack path (water + 
Ca²⁺ + minimal nutrients) via embedded microchannels or surface ports.  
o 
Allow mineralization window (hours–days), then dry-down to return to 
dormant state.  
6. Post-heal verification  
o 
QC: permeability test, crack imaging, ultrasonic pulse velocity; log healing 
cycle count and performance decay.  
Manufacturability / scalability / reproducibility 
 
Manufacturable: compatible with conventional concrete/geopolymer casting; micro-
reservoir addition is a single extra bill-of-materials step.  
 
Scalable: micro-reservoir production can be batch-processed; panels produced by 
standard precast workflows.  
 
Reproducible QC: reservoir mechanical integrity + spore viability + standardized 
crack-heal tests are measurable per lot.  
Why BMN is feasible and preferred here 
 
BMN is explicitly defined as mineral generation by living cells and is applicable to 
HYB systems; it directly implements crack-filling mineral deposition as the 
functional mechanism.
