2) Biological instantiation 
Note: specific organism names/strains are Not present in the ontology/graph; the following 
are explicit engineering instantiation choices consistent with BAC–EUB and BFF. 
Candidate A — Geobacter sulfurreducens (electroactive anode-respiring biofilm former) 
 
Functional role: dense electroactive biofilm; extracellular electron transfer to 
carbon/graphene electrode for energy recovery (ENP) from organics (e.g., acetate) in 
closed-loop wastewater.  
 
Viability & growth requirements  
o 
Oxygen: strict anaerobe (requires O₂-free operation)  
o 
Temperature: 25–35 °C (habitat-controlled loop)  
o 
pH: ~6.8–7.4  
o 
Moisture: fully aqueous; intolerant to drying  
o 
Mechanical stress: tolerates moderate shear if biofilm is mature; avoid high 
shear stripping.  
 
Biocontainment strategy  
o 
Sealed electrochemical module + inline 0.2 µm sterile vent/effluent filtration  
o 
Biofilm immobilization on electrode; no free-cell discharge to downstream  
o 
Operational kill-step for end-of-life cartridges (chemical inactivation or 
thermal, performed off-line). (Specific kill-switch genetics: Not present in the 
ontology/graph.)  
 
Why preferred vs plausible alternatives  
o 
Strong track record in high-current bioanodes; robust electrode-attached 
biofilms; excellent match to ENP.  
Candidate B — Shewanella oneidensis MR-1 (facultative electroactive biofilm former) 
 
Functional role: electroactive biofilm with soluble mediator production; tolerant 
startup; can support ENP with simpler operational control (especially during 
commissioning).  
 
Viability & growth requirements


## Page 283


o 
Oxygen: facultative; tolerates microaerobic transients  
o 
Temperature: ~20–30 °C  
o 
pH: ~6.5–8  
o 
Moisture: aqueous; avoid desiccation  
o 
Mechanical stress: moderate shear tolerance; thinner biofilms than 
Geobacter.  
 
Biocontainment strategy  
o 
Same sealed-cartridge approach; effluent filtration; electrode-immobilized 
biomass.  
 
Why preferred vs plausible alternatives  
o 
Operational flexibility (oxygen transients), easier startup; trade-off is 
typically lower steady-state current density than Geobacter.  
Recommended biological system (final) 
 
Recommended: Geobacter sulfurreducens  
 
Justification vs alternative: For Titan habitat loops (stable, engineered anaerobic 
operation is feasible), Geobacter’s dense electrode-respiring biofilm is the most 
direct match to ENP while still being compatible with electrically assisted 
conversions in BTP/CAT (as a controllable bioelectrochemical interface). Shewanella 
is retained as a commissioning/backup option when oxygen control or rapid recovery 
from upsets dominates.  
 
Trade-offs: higher performance but stricter anaerobic requirement and stronger 
sensitivity to oxidants/sterilants.  
 
3) Matrix/material candidates 
Note: exact commercial grades and process recipes are Not present in the ontology/graph; 
choices below satisfy HYB + LCA (abiotic carbon scaffold + living biofilm interface). 
Material candidate A — Few-layer graphene coating on carbon cloth (graphene–carbon 
electrode laminate) 
 
Composition: carbon cloth current collector + few-layer graphene coating (spray-cast 
or electrophoretic deposition of graphene flakes; dried/annealed at low temperature).  
 
Compatibility with biology: high surface area + conductive carbon; supports stable 
biofilm adhesion (EPS anchoring).  
 
Interface mechanisms  
o 
Physical anchoring into microtexture + EPS adsorption  
o 
π–π interactions with aromatic components; improved initial attachment if 
mild oxygen functionalization is present.  
Material candidate B — Reduced graphene oxide (rGO) paper/film laminated on graphite foil 
 
Composition: stacked rGO nanosheets forming a conductive 2D film (“graphene 
paper”) + graphite foil as inert conductor.  
 
Compatibility with biology: residual oxygenated groups improve wettability and 
initial attachment; conductivity sufficient for bioelectrochemical interfaces.  
 
Interface mechanisms  
o 
Hydrogen bonding / ionic interactions to oxygenated groups  
o 
EPS interpenetration into rGO microvoids improves adhesion and shear 
resistance.  
Final material selection (recommended) 
 
Recommended: rGO film on graphite foil (Candidate B)  
 
Justification: balances wettability/biorecognition (better biofilm nucleation) with high 
conductivity and manufacturable film handling for planar 2D cartridges; graphene-
on-cloth is strong for high surface area, but film-to-film lamination offers simpler 
sealing and consistent planar geometry.



5) Application instantiation 
Deployment scenario (SPE + ENP + BTP + CAT) 
 
Where/how used: inside a Titan habitat’s sealed, temperature-controlled aqueous 
processing loop as a modular bioelectrochemical “living carbon interface” cartridge.  
o 
ENP mode: microbial fuel cell-style energy recovery from organic-rich waste 
streams.  
o 
BTP/CAT mode: electrically assisted bioconversion (electrofermentation / 
electrocatalytic bioprocess support) to steer product selectivity in an internal 
chemical processing skid.  
Measurable success metrics 
 
Electrochemical: areal current density (A/m²), power density (W/m²), coulombic 
efficiency (%).  
 
Bioprocess: conversion rate of target feedstock (mol/L·h), product selectivity (%), 
steady-state operation time to performance drop (days).  
 
Biointerface stability: biofilm coverage (% area), detachment rate (mg/cm²·day), 
electrode sheet resistance drift (% over time).  
Main failure modes + mitigations 
 
Biofilm overgrowth / channel clogging: limit nutrients, maintain shear window; 
periodic short reverse-polarization pulses; cartridge replacement.  
 
Electrode delamination: laminate design + edge sealing; pre-conditioning cycles 
before deployment.  
 
Contamination by non-target microbes: sterile connectors, inline sterilizing filters 
upstream, periodic controlled shutdown + cleaning-in-place (performed off-line to 
protect viability during operation).  
 
Performance decay (toxins / pH drift): buffer control; upstream 
adsorption/neutralization where needed; operational set-point management.  
 
6) Production instantiation


## Page 285


Step-by-step workflow (BFF-compliant) 
1. Substrate fabrication (LCA/HYB):  
o 
Produce rGO film (vacuum filtration or casting) and laminate onto graphite 
foil with biocompatible edge sealing (no leachable solvents post-cure).  
2. Surface conditioning:  
o 
Hydrate and pre-wet electrode in sterile buffered medium; optional mild 
electrochemical conditioning to stabilize surface potential.  
3. Biological preparation (BAC/EUB):  
o 
Grow chosen eubacterium to mid-log phase; wash and resuspend in defined 
medium matching intended electron donor/acceptor regime.  
4. Biofilm seeding (BFF):  
o 
Inoculate sealed flow cell under controlled conditions; hold low-flow (or 
static) for 2–6 h to promote attachment.  
5. Biofilm maturation (BFF):  
o 
Run continuous flow with controlled polarization and substrate feed for 2–7 
days to reach target thickness/current density.  
6. Stabilization & qualification:  
o 
Verify biofilm coverage and electroactivity (baseline polarization curve, 
impedance check, effluent cell count).  
7. Cartridge packaging for deployment (SPE):  
o 
Maintain hydrated, anaerobic (if required) sealed state; integrate quick-
connect sterile fittings and secondary containment.  
Manufacturability, scalability, QC 
 
Manufacturability: planar films enable roll-to-roll compatible coating/lamination; 
standardized gasketed cassettes for module scaling.  
 
Scalability: “numbering-up” identical 2D cartridges to reach required 
power/throughput.  
 
Quality control (examples):  
o 
electrode sheet resistance (Ω/sq), adhesion peel test,  
o 
biofilm areal coverage (imaging) and current density acceptance window,  
o 
leak/pressure test of sealed module,  
o 
sterility/bioburden checks on effluent.

Electrochemistry: low sheet resistance; corrosion resistance in aqueous 
electrolytes.  
Slot 6 — Application: SPE+ENP+BTP+CAT 
 
Meanings:  
o 
SPE: Space exploration and colonization.  
o 
ENP: Energy production and harvesting.  
o 
BTP: Biotechnology and bioprocessing.  
o 
CAT: Catalysis and other chemical processes.  
 
Design constraints  
o 
System integration: sealed, serviceable module inside habitat wet loops.  
o 
Function: (i) energy recovery (ENP), (ii) electrically assisted bioconversion 
(BTP/CAT).  
o 
Reliability: long-lived biofilm, replaceable electrodes, low-maintenance 
operation.  
Slot 7 — Production: BFF 
 
Meaning: Biofilm formation and related ECM growth (biofilm grown in 
dishes/bioreactors).  
 
Design constraints  
o 
Process: staged biofilm growth on the carbon surface under controlled flow 
and potential.  
o 
Quality: reproducible biofilm thickness/composition; stable electroactivity 
over time.  
 
2) Biological instantiation 
Candidate A — Geobacter sulfurreducens (electrode-respiring anaerobe) 
 
Functional role (cell-centric): extracellular electron transfer (EET) from central 
metabolism to the anode (current generation) and/or electrode-driven reducing power 
(cathodic electrosynthesis mode).  
 
Viability/growth requirements: strict anaerobe; 25–35 °C; pH ~6.5–7.5; requires 
buffered aqueous medium; low shear preferred for stable conductive biofilms.  
 
Biocontainment strategy:  
o 
Physical: immobilized biofilm on enclosed electrode cassette + cell-retentive 
membrane on effluent.  
o 
Operational: nutrient-limited “resting biofilm” mode to minimize planktonic 
escape.  
o 
Genetic/kill-switch/auxotrophy: Not present in the ontology/graph 
(engineering option).  
 
Why preferred vs alternatives: strong direct EET; forms electroactive biofilms with 
predictable polarization behavior; best for high-current anodes.  
Candidate B — Shewanella oneidensis MR-1 (facultative anaerobe, versatile respiration) 
 
Functional role (cell-centric): EET via outer-membrane cytochromes; can operate 
under microaerobic transitions (useful for variable loop oxygen ingress).  
 
Viability/growth requirements: 20–35 °C; pH ~6.5–8; tolerates oxygen excursions; 
uses simple carbon sources; biofilm formation is robust but may rely more on 
mediator-based transfer under some conditions.  
 
Biocontainment strategy: same physical containment; additionally, periodic oxidative 
“maintenance pulses” can suppress overgrowth while retaining a basal attached 
community.  
 
Why preferred vs alternatives: better resilience to operational upsets (oxygen leaks, 
intermittent shutdowns) than strict anaerobes.  
Recommended biological candidate: Geobacter sulfurreducens


## Page 289


 
Selection rationale: maximizes direct, cell-to-electrode coupling for ENP (anodic 
current generation) and supports more efficient electron accounting for 
electrosynthesis-assisted routes (BTP/CAT) when operated as a controlled redox 
interface.  
 
Trade-offs vs Shewanella: higher sensitivity to oxygen ingress and some solvents; 
requires stricter anaerobic management but yields tighter electrochemical control 
(less mediator drift).  
 
3) Matrix/material candidates 
Candidate Material 1 — Reduced graphene oxide (rGO) film laminated on graphite foil 
 
Exact composition: multilayer rGO (carbon) on graphite foil current collector 
(carbon).  
 
Bio-compatibility: supports bacterial adhesion via residual oxygen-containing 
groups; allows EPS anchoring without thick insulating binders.  
 
Interface mechanisms:  
o 
Biofilm anchoring via EPS + surface functional groups;  
o 
Electron transfer through conductive percolation network (rGO→graphite).  
 
Why preferred: maximizes “living carbon” identity (LCA) while providing a 
mechanically serviceable 2D sheet electrode.  
Candidate Material 2 — Graphene-coated carbon paper (graphitic fiber mat + graphene 
overlayer) 
 
Exact composition: carbon paper (graphitic fibers) with graphene/graphene-
nanoplatelet coating.  
 
Bio-compatibility: micro-roughness improves biofilm retention; coating increases 
surface conductivity and electroactive area while remaining carbon-dominant.  
 
Interface mechanisms: mechanical interlocking of EPS with microtexture; conductive 
contact to cells across the biofilm–electrode interface.  
 
Why preferred: mechanically tough, high-surface-area planar electrode; readily 
assembled into modular cassettes.  
Recommended material system: Graphene-coated carbon paper 
 
Rationale: best balance of electroactive area, handling robustness, and 
manufacturability (repeatable electrode sheets, scalable cutting/stacking) while 
remaining within LCA.  
 
4) Geometry and structural instantiation 
Phylum-driven architecture (2D): a planar “living electrode sheet” supporting an electroactive 
biofilm. 
 
Quantitative geometry (single sheet):  
o 
Electrode sheet: 100 mm × 100 mm active area (0.01 m²) per cassette plate.  
o 
Carbon paper thickness: ~200–400 µm; graphene overlayer: ~0.5–5 µm 
effective coating.  
o 
Biofilm target thickness: 50–150 µm (kept below strong diffusion limitation). 
o 
Flow channel gap (if plate-in-channel): 0.5–1.5 mm.  
 
How geometry supports function  
o 
Cell viability: thin biofilm limits anoxic/nutrient-depleted dead zones; shear-
controlled flow stabilizes biofilm without sloughing.  
o 
Mass transport: planar flow-by keeps substrate delivery uniform; short 
diffusion paths across biofilm.  
o 
Mechanical performance: carbon paper resists cracking; cassette frame 
prevents flexural fatigue during handling.  
 
Why 2D is optimal vs alternatives  
o 
Compared to 3D foams: easier sterilization/QC, easier replacement, lower 
clog risk in closed loops.  
o 
Compared to 0D beads: avoids electrical contact losses and improves 
deterministic current collection.


## Page 290


5) Application instantiation 
Deployment scenario: a sealed, temperature-controlled bioelectrochemical cartridge installed 
inside habitat aqueous processing loops (waste-to-energy and carbon recycling units). The 
Titan external environment remains isolated; only internal wet-loop conditions are used 
(consistent with the provided rationale). 
 
Where/how used  
o 
ENP mode: anodic module oxidizes organics from waste brines to recover 
electrical energy.  
o 
BTP/CAT mode: electrode potential is controlled to drive selective redox 
bioconversions (electrically assisted bioprocessing), producing targeted 
intermediates for downstream chemistry.  
o 
SPE context: designed as a serviceable, replaceable component in space-
habitat ISRU/process skids.  
 
Measurable success metrics  
o 
Areal current density (mA cm⁻²) at fixed potential; stable performance over 
mission-relevant duty cycles.  
o 
Coulombic efficiency to target product(s) in electrosynthesis mode.  
o 
Biofilm stability: thickness retention and low effluent cell counts.  
o 
Pressure drop across cassette and absence of clogging.  
 
Main failure modes + mitigation  
o 
Biofilm overgrowth/clogging: shear tuning; periodic low-nutrient 
maintenance cycles; removable cassette replacement.  
o 
Oxygen ingress (for strict anaerobe): oxygen-scavenging prechamber; 
dissolved O₂ monitoring; fallback to facultative strain cassette variant.  
o 
Electrode fouling/scaling: periodic polarity reversal or mild electro-cleaning 
pulse; replaceable electrode plates.  
 
6) Production instantiation 
Production route implied by BFF: grow a controlled biofilm directly on the planar living-
carbon scaffold under bioreactor conditions.  
Step-by-step workflow (implementable) 
1. Carbon electrode preparation  
 
Cut graphene-coated carbon paper into plates; attach graphite/titanium current 
collector tabs (non-wetted junction sealed).  
 
Rinse (DI water → ethanol) and dry; optional mild plasma/ozone for wettability 
tuning (kept minimal to avoid over-oxidation).  
2. Module assembly (HYB integration)  
 
Mount plates into a cassette frame with defined flow channels (0.5–1.5 mm gap).  
 
Install gaskets and a cell-retentive outlet membrane (physical containment).  
3. Biological preparation  
 
Pre-culture Geobacter sulfurreducens anaerobically to mid-log; wash into defined 
inoculation medium.  
 
Inoculate cassette in an anaerobic chamber or closed purgeable vessel.  
4. Biofilm formation (BFF)  
 
Operate in a dedicated biofilm-growth bioreactor loop:  
o 
Low flow initially (to promote attachment), then ramp to operational shear.  
o 
Apply controlled electrode potential to select for electroactive attachment.  
 
Grow to target biofilm thickness (50–150 µm) and stabilize in “resting biofilm” 
mode.  
5. Commissioning & stabilization  
 
Switch to process medium gradually (avoid osmotic shock).  
 
Validate electrochemical baseline (polarization curve at commissioning conditions).  
6. Quality control (reproducibility)  
 
Material QC: sheet resistance (Ω/sq), visual uniformity, adhesion of coating.


## Page 291


 
Biofilm QC: start-up time to target current density; biofilm coverage imaging; 
effluent CFU/flow cytometry for leak rate.  
 
Functional QC: product selectivity tests (electrosynthesis mode) and power output 
(ENP mode).  
Manufacturability & scalability 
 
Scales by numbering-up cassettes (repeatable 2D plates) rather than enlarging a 
single unit.  
 
Biofilm growth is batch-commissioned, then shipped/installed as replaceable 
cartridges.

Ontology code: BAC–HYB–EUB–2D–LCA–SPE+ENP+BTP+CAT–BFF  
 
One-line ELM identity: A 2D graphene-coated living-carbon electrode hosting an 
electroactive eubacterial biofilm that couples controlled extracellular electron transfer 
to energy recovery and electrically assisted bioconversions in sealed space-habitat 
aqueous processing loops.

## Page 292


BAC–HYB–EUB–2D–LCA–SPE+ENP+BTP+CAT–BFF defines a planar living-carbon 
bioelectrochemical interface: a graphene-coated carbon electrode supporting an electroactive 
eubacterial biofilm. A Geobacter sulfurreducens biofilm couples cell metabolism to 
extracellular electron transfer at the electrode, enabling controlled anodic energy recovery 
and potential-driven redox bioprocessing consistent with ENP, BTP, and CAT in SPE 
deployments. The 2D architecture is a flow-by electrode sheet with a diffusion-limited 
biofilm thickness target of 50–150 µm. Production follows BFF: electrode fabrication, 
cassette assembly, inoculation, biofilm growth under controlled potential/flow, stabilization, 
and electrochemical/containment QC to ensure reproducible performance.


## Page 293


Iteration 3 
0) Code integrity check 
 
Input ontology code: BAC–HYB–EUB–2D–LCA–SPE+ENP+BTP+CAT–BFF  
 
Each slot code is defined in the ontology: BAC (Bacterial ELMs), HYB (Hybrid 
living materials), EUB (Eubacterial ELMs), 2D (2D ELMs), LCA (Living carbons), 
SPE/ENP/BTP/CAT (Application categories), BFF (Production method).  
 
Multi-label application codification using “+” is explicitly allowed by the ontology 
tables (categories may be combined).  
 
Ambiguity note: The ontology definition of BFF is “biofilm formation and related 
ECM growth” (often associated with bio-ELMs), whereas hybrid biofilms interfacing 
with inorganic materials are explicitly described under BTI. The provided code is 
BFF, so the instantiation keeps BFF and interprets it as biofilm formation on a planar 
carbon scaffold (HYB+LCA) without switching to BTI.  
 
1) Slot-by-slot decoding 
Slot 1 — Domain: BAC 
 
Meaning: Bacterial ELMs populated by bacteria (prokaryotic cells from the bacterial 
domain).  
 
Design constraints  
o 
Biological: bacterial cell systems only (no archaea/eukarya/synthetic cells).  
o 
Material: must support bacterial adhesion and survival at an interface.  
o 
Structural: must allow bacterial colonization (surface 
chemistry/roughness/wetting critical).  
o 
Process: cultivation/handling compatible with bacterial growth and biofilm 
maturation.  
Slot 2 — Super-kingdom: HYB 
 
Meaning: Hybrid living materials combining cells with an abiotic synthetic 
biomaterial/structure (scaffold).  
 
Design constraints  
o 
Biological: cell–scaffold compatibility and containment (abiotic scaffold is 
required).  
o 
Material: scaffold must be explicitly defined and manufacturable.  
o 
Structural: scaffold geometry is a primary design element (not purely cell-
made ECM).  
o 
Process: requires a materials fabrication step plus biological colonization.  
Slot 3 — Kingdom: EUB 
 
Meaning: Eubacterial ELMs (cells from the eubacteria kingdom).  
 
Design constraints  
o 
Biological: concrete candidates must be eubacteria (typical bacterial 
chassis/isolates/consortia).  
Slot 4 — Phylum: 2D 
 
Meaning: Planar/bi-dimensional ELMs, typically biofilms or cells populating a 
planar biomaterial / planar ECM.  
 
Design constraints  
o 
Structural: sheet-like interface; thickness is limited (diffusion + electrical 
contact).  
o 
Mass transport: planar flow-by or flow-through channels; diffusion gradients 
across biofilm thickness.  
o 
Mechanical: adhesion + cohesion must prevent sloughing under shear.  
Slot 5 — Class: LCA 
 
Meaning: Living carbons—ELMs whose extracellular matrices are predominantly 
carbons; examples include graphite, graphene, carbon nanotubes, glassy carbon, 
carbon dots, fullerenes.  
 
Design constraints


## Page 294


o 
Material: carbon scaffold dominates the functional matrix (conductive, 
chemically stable).  
o 
Interface: bacterial adhesion to carbon; biofilm must maintain low-resistance 
electrical coupling.  
o 
Manufacturing: carbon film/coating/electrode must be reproducible at scale.  
Slot 6 — Application: SPE + ENP + BTP + CAT 
 
Meanings  
o 
SPE: Space exploration and colonization.  
o 
ENP: Energy production and harvesting.  
o 
BTP: Biotechnology and bioprocessing.  
o 
CAT: Catalysis and other chemical processes.  
 
Design constraints  
o 
System integration: modular, maintainable, closed-loop operation (space 
context).  
o 
Functional: must support electrochemical energy handling (ENP) plus 
bioprocessing/catalysis (BTP/CAT) at the living interface.  
o 
Reliability: predictable performance under long-duration, low-maintenance 
operation.  
Slot 7 — Production: BFF 
 
Meaning: Biofilm formation and related ECM growth; cultured cells form a biofilm 
and ECM is grown (in Petri dishes or bioreactors).  
 
Design constraints  
o 
Process: controlled inoculation + maturation to a stable biofilm state on the 
carbon surface.  
o 
QC: biofilm thickness/coverage/viability must be measurable and 
reproducible.  
 
2) Biological instantiation 
Candidate A — Geobacter sulfurreducens electroactive biofilm (Not present in the 
ontology/graph.) 
 
Functional role (cell-centric): anode-respiring biofilm; cells couple metabolism to 
electron export at the cell envelope → current generation and electrochemically 
steered redox metabolism.  
 
Viability / growth requirements: aqueous nutrients + electron donor; controlled redox 
environment; temperature/pH maintained by the internal loop; shear-limited to 
prevent detachment.  
 
Biocontainment strategy: sealed cartridge + downstream sterilizing filter; periodic 
cartridge replacement; engineered kill-switch/auxotrophy options (Not present in the 
ontology/graph.)  
Candidate B — Shewanella oneidensis MR-1 electroactive biofilm (Not present in the 
ontology/graph.) 
 
Functional role (cell-centric): surface-attached biofilm enabling electron exchange 
with conductive carbon; supports electrochemically controlled metabolism for redox 
bioconversion at the interface.  
 
Viability / growth requirements: aqueous nutrients; controlled oxygen/redox to avoid 
loss of intended electroactive state; temperature/pH maintained by the loop.  
 
Biocontainment strategy: same as Candidate A (sealed cassette + barriers + 
replaceability).  
Candidate preference rationale (why these over generic eubacterial alternatives) 
 
The code requires a 2D biofilm on a carbon scaffold (LCA) produced via biofilm 
formation (BFF), and the application stack requires ENP + BTP + CAT (energy + 
bioprocessing/catalysis). These two candidates are selected specifically because they 
represent electroactive, surface-attached eubacterial biofilm archetypes suitable for 
an electrode interface (species-level support: Not present in the ontology/graph.)  
Recommended candidate


## Page 295


 
Recommended: Geobacter sulfurreducens (Not present in the ontology/graph.)  
 
Justification vs. alternative: prioritizes a dense, electrode-coupled biofilm concept for 
stable current generation (ENP) while retaining bioconversion steering (BTP/CAT) 
via electrode set-points (comparative performance evidence: Not present in the 
ontology/graph.)  
 
Trade-offs: Candidate B may offer different tolerance/operational flexibility; final 
choice should be driven by cartridge operating window + target substrates (Not 
present in the ontology/graph.)  
 
3) Matrix/material candidates 
Material candidate 1 — Graphene film on graphite foil (laminated living carbon electrode) 
 
Composition: multilayer graphene (or reduced graphene oxide reconstructed into 
conductive film) on graphite foil current collector.  
 
Bio-compatibility: carbon allotrope scaffold consistent with LCA families 
(graphite/graphene).  
 
Interface mechanisms: biofilm anchoring via surface energy + nanoscale roughness; 
short electron-transfer distance at cell–carbon contact points (cell-centric coupling).  
Material candidate 2 — Carbon nanotube (CNT)–graphene hybrid conductive coating on 
carbon cloth 
 
Composition: CNT network (MWCNT or SWCNT) embedded/overcoated with 
graphene flakes on carbon cloth.  
 
Bio-compatibility: CNTs and graphene are explicitly within LCA examples.  
 
Interface mechanisms: high surface area increases colonization density; conductive 
percolation network stabilizes electrical contact under biofilm growth.  
Material selection rationale 
 
Both candidates satisfy “living carbons” (carbon-dominant matrix) and support a 
planar conductive scaffold required by 2D + ENP.  
 
Preferred baseline: graphene-on-graphite foil for simpler QC (sheet resistance, 
thickness uniformity) and robust cartridge assembly.  
 
4) Geometry and structural instantiation 
2D architecture (quantitative specification) 
 
Form factor: gasketed planar electrode cassette.  
 
Carbon scaffold layer: conductive graphene film 10–50 µm on 100–250 µm graphite 
foil (or equivalent conductive carbon collector). (Not present in the ontology/graph.)  
 
Biofilm layer: mature eubacterial biofilm 50–150 µm with controlled thickness via 
growth time and shear. (Not present in the ontology/graph.)  
 
Flow channel: planar gap 0.5–2.0 mm with laminar flow to control nutrient delivery 
and shear. (Not present in the ontology/graph.)  
How geometry supports function 
 
Cell viability: planar diffusion distances allow oxygen/nutrient control; thickness cap 
prevents anoxic dead zones where undesired metabolism dominates.  
 
Mass transport: flow-by geometry enables stable substrate supply and product 
removal; pressure drop stays low.  
 
Electrical performance: 2D conductive scaffold ensures low-resistance current 
collection; thin biofilm maintains short charge-transfer paths.  
Why 2D is optimal vs other geometries 
 
The ontology explicitly associates 2D with biofilms / planar biomaterials; this is the 
most direct architecture for a living electrode interface.  
 
3D scaffolds increase surface area but complicate fouling control and cartridge 
regeneration for long-duration operations (space constraint).  
 
5) Application instantiation 
Deployment scenario (SPE + ENP + BTP + CAT)


## Page 296


 
Where/how used: a sealed replaceable bioelectrochemical cartridge inside a habitat’s 
aqueous processing loop (shielded, temperature controlled) to couple:  
o 
ENP: energy recovery/harvesting from organics via biofilm electrode 
reactions,  
o 
BTP + CAT: electrode-setpoint-controlled bioprocessing/catalysis at the 
living interface,  
o 
SPE: long-duration operation compatible with space habitat maintenance 
constraints.  
Measurable success metrics 
 
Electrochemical: steady current density (A·m⁻²), coulombic efficiency (%), power 
density (W·m⁻²).  
 
Process: substrate conversion (%) and target product formation rate (mol·m⁻²·h⁻¹) 
under defined electrode potentials.  
 
Stability: performance drift per day/week; number of operational cycles before swap.  
Failure modes + mitigation 
 
Biofilm detachment / channel clogging: manage shear; include inlet screens; schedule 
cartridge swaps.  
 
Electrode passivation / fouling: periodic polarity/potential conditioning; replaceable 
electrode plates.  
 
Contamination: sterile connectors + inline sterilizing filtration; closed cartridge 
design.  
 
6) Production instantiation 
Step-by-step production process (BFF on HYB+LCA scaffold) 
1. Carbon scaffold fabrication  
o 
Prepare graphite foil current collector; laminate or coat with graphene film 
(spray-coat / vacuum filtration transfer / roll-to-roll lamination). (Not present 
in the ontology/graph.)  
2. Surface conditioning  
o 
Rinse/sterilize carbon parts (e.g., ethanol + sterile rinse; UV) to avoid 
residues that inhibit biofilm. (Not present in the ontology/graph.)  
3. Reactor cassette assembly  
o 
Assemble gasketed planar cassette with defined channel gap; integrate 
current collectors and sealed ports. (Not present in the ontology/graph.)  
4. Inoculation  
o 
Inoculate with selected eubacterial culture; fill cassette with growth medium; 
hold under controlled flow/no-flow phases to promote initial adhesion. (Not 
present in the ontology/graph.)  
5. Biofilm growth (BFF)  
o 
Mature biofilm in a bench bioreactor loop (controlled flow, temperature, 
redox) until target coverage/thickness is achieved; this is the defining BFF 
step.  
6. Stabilization for deployment  
o 
Transition to operational medium; verify electrochemical response; seal 
cartridge for installation. (Not present in the ontology/graph.)  
Manufacturability, scalability, QC 
 
Scalability: electrode plates produced by roll-to-roll graphene coating/lamination; 
cartridges scaled by numbering-up identical cassettes.  
 
QC checkpoints  
o 
Carbon: thickness, sheet resistance, adhesion of graphene layer.  
o 
Biofilm: surface coverage (%), thickness distribution, live/dead staining or 
metabolic proxies.  
o 
Function: polarization curve + baseline current at standard conditions.  
Why this route is feasible/preferred under the code

 
BFF mandates biofilm formation as the production mechanism, while HYB + LCA + 
2D requires a planar abiotic carbon scaffold colonized by bacteria.  
 
The workflow cleanly separates materials fabrication (repeatable) from biofilm 
maturation (bioprocess-controlled).

C. Integrated paragraph 
BAC–HYB–EUB–2D–LCA–SPE+ENP+BTP+CAT–BFF defines a planar living-carbon 
hybrid electrode: a 2D eubacterial biofilm (recommended Geobacter sulfurreducens, 
alternative Shewanella oneidensis MR-1; Not present in the ontology/graph) immobilized on 
a graphene/graphite (LCA) conductive scaffold. The construct is implemented as a gasketed 
electrode cassette with controlled biofilm thickness and flow-channel transport. Deployed as 
a sealed, replaceable cartridge inside space-habitat aqueous processing loops (SPE), it targets 
energy production/harvesting (ENP) coupled to electrochemically steered bioprocessing and 
catalysis (BTP+CAT). Manufacturing follows BFF: scaffold fabrication, inoculation, biofilm
